zimrrl

Reinforcement learning for zimr, built on zimrnum. This document covers the library as it exists in src/zimrrl.zig. Sections appear when the code they describe does.

1. What zimrrl is#

zimrrl holds the parts of zimr's machine learning that are about agents: environments and their tasks, rewards, returns and advantages, policies and critics, exploration, replay and rollouts, the PPO, SAC, TD3, DQN, AWR and BMPC algorithms, world-model losses and planners. It is built on zimrnum, which holds the numerics and the generic deep learning underneath: tensors, the autograd graph, layers, optimisers, losses, distributions and statistics.

The line between the two follows what a declaration is about. A declaration belongs here when its documentation or its signature speaks of agents — observations, actions, rewards, episodes, policies, critics. It stays in zimrnum when it is generic, even if only reinforcement learning uses it today: a distribution, a statistic, a layer, or a loss that has its own operation on the graph.

const zn = @import("zn");
const zrl = @import("zrl");

Dependencies. zimrrl imports std, zm and zn; zimrnum never imports zimrrl. The same direction holds on the GPU: the reinforcement-learning kernels live in their own files (src/gpu/zrl_binary.zig) and import zrl, while zimrnum's kernels do not. Functions a kernel calls are pure arithmetic over one sample — no allocation and no error union — so the kernel runs the host function itself.

Layers. The source is ordered so that each layer uses only the layers above it, and zimrnum: environments; replay; rollouts, returns and advantages; policy distributions and exploration; value targets; PPO; off-policy training; imitation and rewards; world models; planning. The tests follow.

2. A first agent#

Every algorithm in this library runs the same loop: act, score each action, update the policy, repeat. This chapter shows the loop, then builds the smallest complete agent — a linear policy that learns to balance a cartpole. The chapters after it take the loop apart, one piece at a time, in the order the library's source is organised.

2.1 The shape of a run#

Every algorithm here runs the same four steps in a loop: act in an environment and record what happened, score each action by how much better it did than expected, and update the policy toward the better ones.

The library does not own the network. The policy is built on zimrnum's tape from a network and a distribution — DiagGaussian, SquashedGaussian or Categorical — and the update functions run the loop around it. That is why one ppoUpdate serves both continuous and discrete policies: only the function that produces the log-probabilities differs.

2.2 A first policy, end to end#

The smallest complete agent: a linear policy that learns to balance a cartpole. The model maps observations to a mean action; the spread is fixed.

var graph: zn.Graph(f64) = .init(gpa);

// Leaves: rewritten each update, the graph recomputed around them.
const obs_leaf = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(gpa, &.{ batch, zrl.cartpole_state_dim });
const act_leaf = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(gpa, &.{ batch, 1 });
const obs = try graph.constant(obs_leaf);

// A policy is a mean and a spread. Here the spread is fixed.
const w = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(gpa, &.{ zrl.cartpole_state_dim, 1 });
const policy = try graph.parameter(w);
const mean = try graph.matmul(obs, policy);

const ls = try zn.Tensor(f64).alloc(gpa, &.{ 1, 1 });
ls.data[0] = @log(2.0);
const log_std = try graph.constant(ls);
Graph
/// Reverse-mode automatic differentiation over a recorded tape.
///
/// EAGER FORWARD, RECORDED BACKWARD
///
/// Each operation computes its result immediately AND appends a node describing how to propagate
/// a gradient back through it. Computing eagerly means every shape is known when it is needed, so
/// the graph allocates outputs as it goes and no separate shape-inference pass exists to disagree
/// with the forward pass.
///
/// THE TAPE IS A LIST, NOT A GRAPH SEARCH. Nodes are appended in execution order, and a value
/// can only depend on values recorded before it, so walking the list backwards visits every
/// consumer of a value before the value itself. That is a topological order by construction -
/// no sort, no visited set, no cycle check.
///
/// Everything is allocated from one allocator, usually an arena. A graph has no
/// `deinit`: closing the scope releases the tape, the values and the gradients together, which is
/// the same ownership rule the rest of the library follows.
pub fn Graph(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// What a node needs to push a gradient from its output to its inputs.
        const Op = enum {
            leaf,
            add,
            sub_op,
            mul_op,
            min_op,
            reduce_sum_rows_op,
            reduce_max_rows_op,
            matmul,
            tanh_op,
            relu_op,
            /// A stop-gradient: the value is a copy of `lhs`'s, and the backward pass stops here.
            detach,
            /// SimNorm: a softmax within each group of `geometry[0]` features.
            sim_norm,
            sigmoid_op,
            mse,
            cross_entropy,
            /// Soft-label cross-entropy against a target distribution held in `target` (data: no gradient flows into
            /// it) - the categorical critic's loss.
            soft_cross_entropy,
            softmax_op,
            layernorm_op,
            scale_op,
            dropout_op,
            transpose_op,
            exp_op,
            log_op,
            softplus_op,
            elu_op,
            mish_op,
            slice_op,
            concat_op,
            conv2d_op,
            embedding_op,
        };

        const Node = struct {
            op: Op,
            /// Up to two inputs. Unused slots hold the node's own index and are ignored.
            lhs: Var = 0,
            rhs: Var = 0,
            /// `mse` records its target here; nothing else uses it.
            target: Var = 0,
            /// `cross_entropy` records its class indices here. A slice rather than a `Var`
            /// because labels are integers the caller already holds, not values the graph
            /// differentiates - putting them on the tape would imply a gradient that does not
            /// exist.
            labels: []const usize = &.{},
            /// `scale_op` keeps its factor here, `layernorm_op` its epsilon, `dropout_op` its
            /// keep-probability's reciprocal. One float, three meanings, each used by one op.
            scalar: T = 0,
            /// `dropout_op` keeps its mask as a value of the graph, so `recompute` reuses it and
            /// `checkGradient` sees the same function it is differentiating.
            mask: Var = 0,
            /// `conv2d_op` keeps `{ stride_y, stride_x, pad_y, pad_x }` here. A dedicated field
            /// rather than borrowing `labels`, which means class indices everywhere else - four
            /// words per node is cheap next to a reader wondering which meaning is in play.
            geometry: [4]usize = @splat(0),
        };

        gpa: Allocator,
        values: ArrayList(Tensor(T)),
        grads: ArrayList(Tensor(T)),
        /// Whether a value accumulates a gradient. Constants do not, which keeps the backward
        /// pass from allocating and filling buffers nobody reads.
        wants_grad: ArrayList(bool),
        nodes: ArrayList(Node),
        /// *** `backward`'s TEMPORARIES, reset at the start of every pass. Each op's backward
        /// allocates a contribution (a negation, a transpose product, a broadcast sum), adds it
        /// into the gradient and never reads it again - and these used to come from `gpa`, the
        /// graph's arena, so a graph REUSED for many passes grew by every pass's temporaries:
        /// PPO on the humanoid (one graph, minibatch after minibatch) climbed ~60 MiB per
        /// iteration in the browser until the memory watchdog called it a leak.
        ///
        /// ** Its parent is `gpa` by default, which is right for a short-lived graph in an
        /// arena. A LONG-LIVED graph must give it a real allocator (`useScratchAllocator`): a
        /// reset that keeps its capacity merges its buffers into one fresh allocation, and an
        /// arena parent can never free the old ones, so it would grow on every merge.
        scratch: ArenaAllocator,

        pub fn init(gpa: Allocator) Self {
            return .{
                .gpa = gpa,
                .values = .empty,
                .grads = .empty,
                .wants_grad = .empty,
                .nodes = .empty,
                .scratch = ArenaAllocator.init(gpa),
            };
        }

        /// Take `backward`'s temporaries from `child` - a real allocator, for a graph reused
        /// over many passes (see `scratch`). Pair with `deinitScratch`.
        pub fn useScratchAllocator(self: *Self, child: Allocator) void {
            self.scratch.deinit();
            self.scratch = ArenaAllocator.init(child);
        }

        /// Free `backward`'s temporaries; needed only after `useScratchAllocator`.
        pub fn deinitScratch(self: *Self) void {
            self.scratch.deinit();
        }

        fn push(self: *Self, value: Tensor(T), node: Node, wants: bool) Error!Var {
            const shape: []const usize = value.shape[0..value.rank];
            const grad: Tensor(T) = if (wants) try Tensor(T).alloc(self.gpa, shape) else value;
            if (wants) {
                grad.fill(0);
            }
            try self.values.append(self.gpa, value);
            try self.grads.append(self.gpa, grad);
            try self.wants_grad.append(self.gpa, wants);
            try self.nodes.append(self.gpa, node);
            return @intCast(self.values.items.len - 1);
        }

        /// A value the graph will differentiate with respect to.
        pub fn parameter(self: *Self, value: Tensor(T)) Error!Var {
            return self.push(value, .{ .op = .leaf }, true);
        }

        /// A value that is fixed: inputs, targets, anything not being learned.
        pub fn constant(self: *Self, value: Tensor(T)) Error!Var {
            return self.push(value, .{ .op = .leaf }, false);
        }

        /// The tensor behind a value.
        pub fn valueOf(self: *const Self, v: Var) Tensor(T) {
            return self.values.items[v];
        }

        /// The gradient accumulated into a value by `backward`.
        pub fn gradOf(self: *const Self, v: Var) Error!Tensor(T) {
            if (!self.wants_grad.items[v]) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            return self.grads.items[v];
        }

        /// `out = a + b`, with `b` broadcast to `a`'s shape.
        pub fn add(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const shape: []const usize = va.shape[0..va.rank];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            try zimrnum.add(T, out, va, self.values.items[b]);
            const wants: bool = self.wants_grad.items[a] or self.wants_grad.items[b];
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .add, .lhs = a, .rhs = b }, wants);
        }

        /// `out = a @ b`, rank 2.
        pub fn matmul(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const vb: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[b];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ va.shape[0], vb.shape[1] });
            try zimrnum.matmul(T, out, va, vb);
            const wants: bool = self.wants_grad.items[a] or self.wants_grad.items[b];
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .matmul, .lhs = a, .rhs = b }, wants);
        }

        /// `out = a - b`, elementwise, same shape.
        pub fn sub(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.binary(.sub_op, a, b);
        }

        /// `out = a * b`, elementwise, same shape.
        /// How `reduceRows` folds each row.
        pub const RowReduction = enum { sum, max, mean };

        /// Reduce each row of an `[N, D]` value to one number, giving `[N, 1]`.
        ///
        /// ---- WHY THIS EXISTS: IT WAS ALREADY HERE FIVE TIMES ----
        ///
        /// The tape had no axis reduction, so every function that needed one spelled it as a
        /// matmul against a column of ones: `diagGaussianLogProb`, `klGaussianDiag`,
        /// `categoricalLogProb` and `squashedReparameterize` each allocated that column and
        /// multiplied by it. That works - a matmul against a constant is a linear map and the
        /// tape already knows its gradient - and it is unreadable at the call site, allocates a
        /// vector of ones per call, and does O(N*D) work through a general matmul path.
        ///
        /// Naming it also collapses three things that looked like separate features into one:
        /// a squared row norm, a row-wise dot product, and a max over a set are this operation
        /// with different operands or a different fold.
        ///
        /// ---- `.mean` IS COMPOSED, NOT A THIRD TAG ----
        ///
        /// `mean` is `sum` scaled by `1/D`. Giving it its own tape op would mean a second
        /// backward that differs from `sum`'s by a constant - two implementations of one
        /// derivative, which is how they drift.
        pub fn reduceRows(self: *Self, a: Var, kind: RowReduction) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            if (va.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const rows: usize = va.shape[0];
            const cols: usize = va.shape[1];
            if (cols == 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            switch (kind) {
                .mean => {
                    const summed: Var = try self.reduceRows(a, .sum);
                    return self.scale(summed, 1 / @as(T, @floatFromInt(cols)));
                },
                .sum => {
                    const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ rows, 1 });
                    for (0..rows) |r| {
                        var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
                        for (0..cols) |c| {
                            total.add(va.data[va.base + r * cols + c]);
                        }
                        out.data[out.base + r] = total.value();
                    }
                    return self.push(
                        out,
                        .{ .op = .reduce_sum_rows_op, .lhs = a },
                        self.wants_grad.items[a],
                    );
                },
                .max => {
                    // ---- THE WINNER IS RECORDED AS A MASK, NOT RE-DERIVED ----
                    //
                    // The backward must send the gradient to the element the OUTPUT came from.
                    // Recomputing the argmax there would be a second decision made from the same
                    // data - and `recompute()` runs every minibatch, so on a tie the two
                    // decisions can disagree and the gradient lands somewhere the value did not.
                    //
                    // A mask, held as a graph value exactly as `dropout` holds its own, makes the
                    // backward a multiply with no decision in it at all. `recompute` rebuilds the
                    // mask, which is correct: if the inputs moved, the winner genuinely may have.
                    const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ rows, 1 });
                    const mask: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ rows, cols });
                    fillRowMaxAndMask(T, out, mask, va);
                    const mask_var: Var = try self.constant(mask);
                    return self.push(
                        out,
                        .{ .op = .reduce_max_rows_op, .lhs = a, .mask = mask_var },
                        self.wants_grad.items[a],
                    );
                },
            }
        }

        /// `sum(x^2)` per row - the shape a gradient penalty is measured in.
        pub fn squaredRowNorm(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.reduceRows(try self.mul(a, a), .sum);
        }

        /// `sum(a * b)` per row. A cosine similarity when both sides are unit-length, which is
        /// what ASE's encoder reward assumes and `l2NormalizeRows` is responsible for.
        pub fn rowDot(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.reduceRows(try self.mul(a, b), .sum);
        }

        /// Elementwise minimum of two values.
        ///
        /// ---- WHY THIS IS A PRIMITIVE AND NOT A COMPOSITION ----
        ///
        /// Everything else the RL work needed - `diagGaussianLogProb`, `klGaussianDiag`,
        /// `categoricalLogProb`, `squashedReparameterize` - was composed from ops that already
        /// existed, so none of them needed a new backward. This one cannot be:
        /// `min(a,b) = 0.5*(a + b - |a-b|)` and `abs` is not on the tape either.
        ///
        /// ---- WHAT IT IS FOR ----
        ///
        /// Pessimism about value estimates, which is the single most common fix for the single
        /// most common failure in off-policy RL. A Q-function trained on its own bootstrapped
        /// targets OVERESTIMATES - the max over noisy estimates is biased upward, the bias feeds
        /// back through the target, and the critic diverges upward while the policy chases
        /// actions it has overrated. Taking a minimum over several estimates is the standard
        /// brake:
        ///
        ///   TD3   the smaller of two independently-initialised critics
        ///   DroQ  the smallest over M dropout masks of ONE critic - an ensemble's pessimism
        ///         at a fraction of the compute, which is what makes a high update-to-data
        ///         ratio affordable
        ///
        /// Both are this operation, folded over however many estimates there are.
        pub fn min(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.binary(.min_op, a, b);
        }

        pub fn mul(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.binary(.mul_op, a, b);
        }

        fn binary(self: *Self, op: Op, a: Var, b: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const shape: []const usize = va.shape[0..va.rank];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            const vb: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[b];
            switch (op) {
                .sub_op => try zimrnum.sub(T, out, va, vb),
                .mul_op => try zimrnum.mul(T, out, va, vb),
                .min_op => try zimrnum.minimum(T, out, va, vb),
                else => unreachable,
            }
            const wants: bool = self.wants_grad.items[a] or self.wants_grad.items[b];
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = op, .lhs = a, .rhs = b }, wants);
        }

        /// `out = a` - with NO gradient flowing back through it: a STOP-GRADIENT (PyTorch's `.detach()`, JAX's
        /// `stop_gradient`). The forward value is an exact copy of `a`'s, refreshed by every `recompute`; the backward
        /// pass stops at this node, so nothing upstream of `a` learns from anything built on `out`.
        ///
        /// ---- WHY A LEARNER NEEDS ONE ----
        ///
        /// TD-MPC's world model is trained, among other things, to be CONSISTENT: rolled one step forward through the
        /// learned dynamics, today's latent state must land on the latent state the encoder gives tomorrow's
        /// observation - a loss |dynamics(encode(obs_t), a_t) - encode(obs_t+1)|^2. Both sides come from the SAME
        /// encoder. If the gradient flowed through both, the cheapest way to make them equal would be for the encoder
        /// to send every observation to one and the same point: the loss drops to zero and the latent means nothing.
        /// That is "representation collapse", and in self-predictive learning (BYOL, Grill et al. 2020; SimSiam, Chen
        /// and He 2021; SPR, Schwarzer et al. 2021) the cure is exactly this op: the TARGET side is detached, so the
        /// prediction is pulled toward the target and never the target toward the prediction. Temporal-difference
        /// targets need the same: a value target must not be moved by the value loss that is measured against it.
        ///
        /// ---- WHY IT IS AN OP ----
        ///
        /// Without it the targets need a pass of their own, without gradient: BMPC's PyTorch code encodes a whole
        /// window once without gradient (for the targets) and its first observation again with gradient (to start the
        /// rollout). BMPC-jax encodes observations and next observations in ONE call and cuts the targets from it
        /// through a stop-gradient - one encoder pass instead of two. `detach` is what makes that expressible here.
        pub fn detach(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const input_value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const detached_copy: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, input_value.shape[0..input_value.rank]);
            // Copied through `scale` by exactly 1: exact for every finite value, and correct for any view (a slice, a
            // transpose) `a` may be - where a raw memcpy of the buffer would not be.
            try zimrnum.scale(T, detached_copy, input_value, 1);
            // The last argument is whether this node wants a gradient: never, whatever `a` wants - that IS the op.
            const wants_gradient: bool = false;
            return self.push(detached_copy, .{ .op = .detach, .lhs = a }, wants_gradient);
        }

        /// `out = relu(a)`.
        pub fn relu(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.unary(.relu_op, a);
        }

        /// `out = sigmoid(a)`.
        pub fn sigmoid(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.unary(.sigmoid_op, a);
        }

        fn unary(self: *Self, op: Op, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const shape: []const usize = va.shape[0..va.rank];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            switch (op) {
                .relu_op => try zimrnum.relu(T, out, va),
                .sigmoid_op => try zimrnum.sigmoid(T, out, va),
                .tanh_op => try zimrnum.tanh(T, out, va),
                else => unreachable,
            }
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// `out = tanh(a)`.
        pub fn tanh(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const shape: []const usize = va.shape[0..va.rank];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            try zimrnum.tanh(T, out, va);
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .tanh_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// `x` squashed by tanh into the interval from `low` to `high`, elementwise:
        ///
        ///       low + (high - low) * (tanh(x) + 1) / 2
        ///
        /// TD-MPC2's and BMPC's way of keeping a network's log standard deviation inside chosen bounds (`log_std` in
        /// their `math.py`), and good for any output that must stay inside an interval. Smooth everywhere, which a
        /// clamp is not: an output clamped past its edge gets NO gradient - the network cannot learn its way back
        /// in - while this one always feels a pull, gentler toward the ends where tanh flattens. The ends are reached
        /// only in the limit (and exactly, in floating point, once tanh rounds to +-1). `x` is [rows, cols].
        pub fn tanhIntoRange(self: *Self, x: Var, low: T, high: T) Error!Var {
            if (!(low < high)) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            const x_value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[x];
            if (x_value.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            // The same line written as tanh(x) * half_width + (low + half_width): the midpoint folded into one
            // constant, which every row shares.
            const half_width: T = 0.5 * (high - low);
            const squashed: Var = try self.tanh(x);
            const stretched: Var = try self.scale(squashed, half_width);
            const midpoint: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ 1, x_value.shape[1] });
            @memset(midpoint.data, low + half_width);
            return self.add(stretched, try self.constant(midpoint));
        }

        /// Softmax cross-entropy of rank-2 logits against class indices, as a 1x1 value.
        ///
        /// The backward rule is `softmax(logits) - onehot`, over the row count - the pairing
        /// `exp(x)`, elementwise.
        ///
        /// THE LEVERAGED PAIR - AND WHY `softplus` STILL NEEDS ITS OWN NODE
        ///
        /// `exp` and `log` unlock compositions the tape could not express: `softplus`, `mish`,
        /// `logSumExp` as a differentiable reduction, anything with a ratio of exponentials.
        /// That is the `slice`/`concat` shape again - the primitive is worth more than the
        /// functions it is blocking.
        ///
        /// But `softplus(x) = log(1 + exp(x))` **written that way is wrong**, and the third node
        /// below exists because of it. Measured at f32: `exp(89)` is infinity, so the composition
        /// returns `inf` from x = 89 upward where the true answer is 89 - softplus is very nearly
        /// the identity for large x. **Having the primitives does not mean composing them is
        /// safe**, which is worth knowing before reaching for them.
        pub fn exp(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, source.shape[0..source.rank]);
            try zimrnum.exp(T, out, source);
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .exp_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// The natural logarithm, elementwise. A nonpositive input gives `-inf` or `nan`, which
        /// is `@log`'s behaviour and is left alone: clamping here would hide the caller's bug.
        pub fn log(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, source.shape[0..source.rank]);
            try zimrnum.log(T, out, source);
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .log_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// `log(1 + exp(x))`, computed so it neither overflows nor loses its tail.
        ///
        /// ITS OWN NODE, BECAUSE THE COMPOSITION IS NOT SAFE
        ///
        /// `log(1 + exp(x))` overflows at f32 from x = 89 and at f64 from about 710, returning
        /// `inf` where the answer is x. The tensor version uses `max(x, 0) + log1p(exp(-|x|))`,
        /// which is exact at both ends - see its own comment for the identity and for what the
        /// two earlier forms lost.
        ///
        /// **The backward is the reason this is cheap**: `d/dx softplus(x)` is exactly
        /// `sigmoid(x)` - no special case, no branch, and nothing to derive.
        pub fn softplus(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, source.shape[0..source.rank]);
            try zimrnum.softplus(T, out, source);
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .softplus_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// ELU: `x` above zero, `alpha * (exp(x) - 1)` below.
        ///
        /// A REAL NODE, BECAUSE A BRANCH IS NOT A COMPOSITION
        ///
        /// `silu`, `gelu` and `mish` are all products and sums of nodes that already exist, so
        /// they needed nothing. ELU is not: it switches on the sign, and there is no elementwise
        /// `select` here to build that from. **This is the last activation without a gradient**,
        /// and closing it means one node rather than a fifth composition.
        ///
        /// The backward is `1` above zero and `alpha * exp(x)` below - which is `elu(x) + alpha`,
        /// so it could read the output. It reads the INPUT instead: the two agree mathematically
        /// and only the input says which side of the branch a value was on. At exactly zero both
        /// give `1`, so the kink is continuous in the derivative for `alpha = 1` and the test
        /// probes across it rather than around it.
        pub fn elu(self: *Self, a: Var, alpha: T) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, source.shape[0..source.rank]);
            try zimrnum.elu(T, out, source, alpha);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .elu_op, .lhs = a, .scalar = alpha },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// `x` through `activation` - one place that knows which op each `Activation` is, so a layer's choice of
        /// activation is data, not code.
        pub fn activate(self: *Self, x: Var, activation: Activation) Error!Var {
            return switch (activation) {
                .none => x,
                .relu => try self.relu(x),
                .mish => try self.mish(x),
                .sim_norm => |group| try self.simNorm(x, group),
            };
        }

        /// Mish: `x * tanh(softplus(x))`, as ONE node with one exponential each way - `zm.mish` forward and
        /// `zm.mishSlope` backward, where the identity behind both is derived.
        ///
        /// As a composition - softplus, then tanh, then the product - it took three nodes: an exponential, a
        /// logarithm and a hyperbolic tangent forward, three passes over the values, and three backward rules with a
        /// temporary each. Mish is in every hidden layer of the TD-MPC family, so that difference is paid at every
        /// layer of every forward and backward pass.
        pub fn mish(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[x];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, source.shape[0..source.rank]);
            try zimrnum.mish(T, out, source);
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .mish_op, .lhs = x }, self.wants_grad.items[x]);
        }

        /// SiLU, also called swish: `x * sigmoid(x)`.
        ///
        /// ACTIVATIONS THE TAPE ALREADY CONTAINS, AND THE ONES IT DOES NOT
        ///
        /// `silu`, `gelu`, `softplus` and `elu` all exist at tensor level here and **none of them
        /// had a tape node**, so a network using any of them could be evaluated and not trained.
        /// That is a worse gap than a missing gradient function: the forward works, the loss
        /// falls for the layers that do have nodes, and the activation simply never learns.
        ///
        /// Two of the four are compositions of nodes that already exist, so they cost nothing and
        /// their gradients are the tape's:
        ///
        ///     silu(x) = x * sigmoid(x)
        ///     gelu(x) = 0.5x * (1 + tanh(0.7978845608 * (x + 0.044715 x^3)))
        ///
        /// The other two are not, and the plan records why rather than half-building them:
        /// `softplus` needs `log1p(exp(x))` and the tape has neither log nor exp; `elu` branches
        /// on the sign, which is not an elementwise composition of anything here.
        pub fn silu(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.mul(x, try self.sigmoid(x));
        }

        /// GELU, in the tanh form every transformer uses.
        ///
        /// The exact definition is `x * Phi(x)` with `Phi` the normal CDF, which needs `erf`. The
        /// tanh approximation is what PyTorch, JAX and every published transformer actually run -
        /// it agrees to about 1e-3 and is the thing weights were trained against, so matching it
        /// matters more than matching the definition.
        pub fn gelu(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Var {
            // `x * sigmoid(2u)`, NOT `0.5x * (1 + tanh(u))` - THE SAME FUNCTION, DERIVED
            //
            //     (1 + tanh u) / 2
            //   = (1 + (e^u - e^-u)/(e^u + e^-u)) / 2
            //   = (2 e^u / (e^u + e^-u)) / 2
            //   =  e^u / (e^u + e^-u)
            //   =    1 / (1 + e^-2u)
            //   =  sigmoid(2u)
            //
            // The first form needs a tensor of ones to add - **allocated on every call**, and on
            // a graph rebuilt each training step that is a per-step allocation for a constant.
            // The second needs none, and is two nodes shorter. zimrmath's own `gelu` already used
            // this identity; the tape's did not until it was read.
            //
            // `sqrt(2/pi)` is the 0.7978845608 in the published formula, doubled here because the
            // identity takes `2u`.
            const cubed: Var = try self.mul(x, try self.mul(x, x));
            const inner: Var = try self.add(x, try self.scale(cubed, 0.044715));
            return self.mul(x, try self.sigmoid(try self.scale(inner, 1.5957691216057308)));
        }

        /// A gated linear unit: the first half of the columns, gated by the second.
        ///
        /// NO BACKWARD - AND THAT IS THE POINT
        ///
        /// A hand-derived backward would walk coordinates and apply the product rule by hand.
        /// Here the whole thing is
        ///
        ///     slice(x, 0, half) * sigmoid(slice(x, half, half))
        ///
        /// and every one of those is already a tape node, so **the gradient is the tape's**.
        /// There is no second derivation to keep in step with the first, and no chance of the
        /// pair disagreeing under maintenance.
        ///
        /// This is the third thing `graph.slice` and `graph.concat` paid for, after multi-head
        /// attention and the batched cartpole twin. The ops were built because their absence had
        /// shaped two designs; they keep shaping ones that come after.
        ///
        /// The last axis must be even - half the columns carry the value and half the gate.
        pub fn glu(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.gateWith(x, .sigmoid);
        }

        /// SwiGLU: the same shape, gated by SiLU rather than sigmoid.
        ///
        /// The gate in every recent transformer feed-forward. `silu(v)` is `v * sigmoid(v)`, so
        /// this too is existing nodes and nothing new.
        pub fn swiglu(self: *Self, x: Var) Error!Var {
            return self.gateWith(x, .silu);
        }

        /// Which gate `glu` and `swiglu` apply.
        pub const Gate = enum { sigmoid, silu };

        /// The shared body. Splitting once and gating once, with the gate chosen at comptime.
        fn gateWith(self: *Self, x: Var, comptime gate: Gate) Error!Var {
            const value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[x];
            if (value.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const width: usize = value.shape[1];
            if (width % 2 != 0 or width == 0) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const half: usize = width / 2;
            const carried: Var = try self.slice(x, 1, 0, half);
            const gating: Var = try self.slice(x, 1, half, half);
            const opened: Var = switch (gate) {
                .sigmoid => try self.sigmoid(gating),
                // SiLU is `v * sigmoid(v)` - two nodes, no new op.
                .silu => try self.mul(gating, try self.sigmoid(gating)),
            };
            return self.mul(carried, opened);
        }

        /// PPO's clipped objective, AS A LOSS - the version `ppoClipObjective` could not be.
        ///
        /// WHY THIS EXISTS: THE SCALAR ONE COMPUTES PPO AND CANNOT TRAIN WITH IT
        ///
        /// `ppoClipObjective` returns a `T`. It is correct, it is tested, and **it is not on the
        /// tape** - so it reports what the objective is worth and cannot produce a gradient. The
        /// library had PPO's arithmetic and not PPO's training step, and nothing said so until an
        /// end-to-end test tried to learn with it.
        ///
        /// THE CLIP IS THE WHOLE ALGORITHM, AND IT LIVES IN THE BACKWARD
        ///
        /// The forward takes a minimum, which looks like a detail. The gradient is where it acts:
        /// **when the clip is the smaller branch, the gradient is ZERO** - the update stops
        /// pushing a ratio that has already moved too far. That is what stops a policy step from
        /// destroying the policy, and it is the reason PPO works where plain policy gradient
        /// diverges.
        ///
        /// `log_prob_old` and `advantages` are constants of the step: the old policy is fixed by
        /// definition and the advantages were estimated before it. Only `log_prob_new` carries a
        /// gradient, which is why it is the only `Var`.
        ///
        /// SAC's reparameterised action and its log-probability, both ON THE TAPE.
        ///
        /// ---- WHY SAC CANNOT BE ASSEMBLED WITHOUT THIS ----
        ///
        /// SAC's actor loss is `alpha * log p(a) - Q(s, a)` where `a` is a SAMPLE from the
        /// policy. A gradient has to reach the policy through that sample, and you cannot
        /// differentiate through "draw a random number". The reparameterisation trick moves the
        /// randomness out of the path: draw `eps` once as a CONSTANT, then `u = mean + std*eps`
        /// is an ordinary differentiable expression of the parameters.
        ///
        /// `SquashedGaussian.sample` already returns the pre-squash value, which is the same
        /// draw - what it cannot give is the gradient. This is the SAC-side twin of
        /// `diagGaussianLogProb`.
        ///
        /// ---- THE TANH CORRECTION, AND WHY IT IS WRITTEN THIS WAY ----
        ///
        /// Squashing changes the density. `a = tanh(u)` compresses the tails, so probability
        /// mass piles up near the bounds and the log-density picks up the Jacobian term
        /// `-sum log(1 - tanh(u)^2)`. Omitting it is the classic SAC bug: the entropy term is
        /// then computed against the WRONG density, alpha tunes toward a target that does not
        /// correspond to the policy's actual entropy, and the run merely trains badly.
        ///
        /// Written as `2 * (log(2) - u - softplus(-2u))`, which is that same term rearranged.
        /// The direct form evaluates `1 - tanh(u)^2`, and for |u| past about 9 `tanh(u)` rounds
        /// to exactly 1 in f32 - so the expression is `log(0)`, the loss is infinite, and every
        /// gradient after it is NaN. Saturated actions are not an edge case in continuous
        /// control; they are what a converged policy does.
        ///
        /// ---- THE LOG-PROBABILITY HAS A CLOSED FORM IN `eps` ----
        ///
        /// The Gaussian part does not need recomputing from `u`. Since `u - mean = std * eps`
        /// exactly, the standardised deviation IS `eps`, so the per-dimension term is
        /// `0.5*eps^2 + log_std + 0.5*log(2pi)` - no subtraction, no division, and nothing that
        /// can cancel.
        pub fn squashedReparameterize(
            self: *Self,
            mean: Var,
            log_std: Var,
            noise: Tensor(T),
        ) Error!SquashedDraw {
            const mean_value: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(mean);
            if (mean_value.rank != 2 or noise.rank != 2) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const rows: usize = mean_value.shape[0];
            const dims: usize = mean_value.shape[1];
            if (noise.shape[0] != rows or noise.shape[1] != dims) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const std_value: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(log_std);
            if (std_value.rank != 2 or std_value.shape[0] != 1 or std_value.shape[1] != dims) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }

            const row_ones: Var = try self.constant(try ones(T, self.gpa, &.{ rows, 1 }));
            // `eps` is a CONSTANT. That is the entire trick - if it were a parameter the
            // optimiser could lower the loss by choosing which random numbers it got.
            const eps: Var = try self.constant(noise);

            const std_wide: Var = try self.matmul(row_ones, log_std);
            const scale_wide: Var = try self.exp(std_wide);
            const pre_squash: Var = try self.add(mean, try self.mul(scale_wide, eps));
            const action: Var = try self.tanh(pre_squash);

            // Gaussian term, in closed form: 0.5*eps^2 + log_std + 0.5*log(2pi).
            const half_sq: Var = try self.scale(try self.mul(eps, eps), 0.5);
            const gauss_per_dim: Var = try self.add(half_sq, std_wide);
            const gauss: Var = try self.reduceRows(gauss_per_dim, .sum);

            // Squash correction: 2 * (log(2) - u - softplus(-2u)), per dimension.
            const log_two: T = 0.693147180559945309417232121458;
            const neg_two_u: Var = try self.scale(pre_squash, -2);
            const sp: Var = try self.softplus(neg_two_u);
            const inner: Var = try self.sub(try self.scale(pre_squash, -1), sp);
            const shifted_rows: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ rows, dims });
            @memset(shifted_rows.data, log_two);
            const log_two_wide: Var = try self.constant(shifted_rows);
            const correction_per_dim: Var = try self.scale(try self.add(log_two_wide, inner), 2);
            const correction: Var = try self.reduceRows(correction_per_dim, .sum);

            // log p = -(gaussian sum + D*half_log_tau) - correction.
            const constant_term: T = @as(T, @floatCast(DiagGaussian(T).half_log_tau)) *
                @as(T, @floatFromInt(dims));
            const offset_rows: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ rows, 1 });
            @memset(offset_rows.data, constant_term);
            const offset: Var = try self.constant(offset_rows);
            const gaussian_logp: Var = try self.scale(try self.add(gauss, offset), -1);
            const log_prob: Var = try self.sub(gaussian_logp, correction);

            return .{ .action = action, .log_prob = log_prob };
        }

        /// The log-probability a categorical policy assigns to each CHOSEN action, on the tape.
        ///
        /// ---- WHY THIS IS NEEDED EVEN THOUGH `crossEntropy` EXISTS ----
        ///
        /// `crossEntropy` reduces to a SCALAR - the mean over the batch. PPO needs one
        /// log-probability PER SAMPLE, because the ratio against the old policy is formed per
        /// sample and then clipped per sample. A mean cannot be un-meaned.
        ///
        /// ---- WHY ONE UPDATE FUNCTION SERVES BOTH ACTION SPACES ----
        ///
        /// One `ppoUpdate` (zimrrl) serves both action spaces, because `PpoModel` takes the LOSS
        /// from the caller - so the only thing that differs between discrete and continuous is which
        /// function produced `log_prob_new`. This is the discrete one; `diagGaussianLogProb` is
        /// the continuous one. The update never learns the difference.
        ///
        /// ---- THE SELECTION IS A MASKED ROW SUM ----
        ///
        /// The tape has no gather-by-index returning one column. Multiplying by a one-hot and
        /// summing each row picks the same element and is differentiable by construction - the
        /// gradient flows to the chosen logit and zero elsewhere, which is exactly right.
        pub fn categoricalLogProb(
            self: *Self,
            logits: Var,
            actions: []const usize,
        ) Error!Var {
            const logit_value: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(logits);
            if (logit_value.rank != 2) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const rows: usize = logit_value.shape[0];
            const classes: usize = logit_value.shape[1];
            if (actions.len != rows or classes == 0) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }

            const one_hot: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ rows, classes });
            @memset(one_hot.data, 0);
            for (actions, 0..) |choice, r| {
                if (choice >= classes) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                one_hot.data[r * classes + choice] = 1;
            }

            // `softmaxRows` subtracts the row maximum before exponentiating, so taking its log
            // is safe here - the underflow that makes `log(softmax(x))` a bad idea in general
            // has already been handled one level down.
            const probs: Var = try self.softmaxRows(logits);
            const log_probs: Var = try self.log(probs);
            const masked: Var = try self.mul(log_probs, try self.constant(one_hot));
            return self.reduceRows(masked, .sum);
        }

        /// The gradient of `output` with respect to `input`, AS A GRAPH NODE.
        ///
        /// ---- WHY THIS IS NOT `backward` ----
        ///
        /// `backward` computes gradients into tensors. Those numbers are correct and they are
        /// dead: nothing downstream can be differentiated with respect to them, because no tape
        /// node records how they were produced.
        ///
        /// This walks the same tape the same way and **emits tape nodes instead of writing
        /// tensors**. The result is an ordinary `Var`, so it can be squared, summed, added to a
        /// loss, and differentiated again.
        ///
        /// ---- WHAT IT IS FOR ----
        ///
        /// Gradient penalties. Every adversarial imitation method - AMP, ASE, ADD, HIL - trains
        /// its discriminator with a penalty on the gradient of its OUTPUT with respect to its
        /// INPUT, and then descends that penalty. Without it a discriminator converges toward a
        /// step function: near-perfect classification and a reward that is flat almost
        /// everywhere with a cliff at the boundary, so the policy gets no gradient over most of
        /// its state distribution. The symptom is not a crash - accuracy climbs, the style
        /// reward flattens, and the motion degenerates while every curve looks converged.
        ///
        /// ---- WHY IT NEEDED NO NEW BACKWARD ----
        ///
        /// Every rule below is written with operations that are ALREADY on the tape: a matmul's
        /// backward is a matmul against a transpose, a product rule is two multiplies. So the
        /// emitted graph is differentiable for free, and second-order support costs no
        /// third-order machinery.
        ///
        /// ---- STAGED, DELIBERATELY ----
        ///
        /// The ops below are the ones a discriminator is built from. Anything else returns
        /// `Error.Unsupported` rather than a wrong number, because a second-order implementation
        /// that silently falls back to first-order produces finite, plausible, stable values and
        /// a penalty that is a constant - which is the worst failure available here.
        pub fn gradientOf(self: *Self, output: Var, input: Var) Error!Var {
            if (self.values.items[output].size() != 1) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const count: usize = self.nodes.items.len;
            const seeds: []?Var = try self.gpa.alloc(?Var, count);
            defer self.gpa.free(seeds);
            @memset(seeds, null);

            const one: Tensor(T) = try ones(T, self.gpa, &.{ 1, 1 });
            seeds[output] = try self.constant(one);

            var i: usize = count;
            while (i > 0) {
                i -= 1;
                const seed: Var = seeds[i] orelse continue;
                const node: Node = self.nodes.items[i];
                switch (node.op) {
                    // A stop-gradient contributes nothing - exactly, by definition.
                    .detach => {},
                    .leaf => {},
                    .add => {
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.lhs, seed);
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.rhs, seed);
                    },
                    .sub_op => {
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.lhs, seed);
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.rhs, try self.scale(seed, -1));
                    },
                    .mul_op => {
                        // The product rule, and the reason this is worth doing symbolically: each
                        // side's gradient MENTIONS the other side, so it stays connected to the
                        // graph and can be differentiated again.
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.lhs, try self.mul(seed, node.rhs));
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.rhs, try self.mul(seed, node.lhs));
                    },
                    .scale_op => {
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.lhs, try self.scale(seed, node.scalar));
                    },
                    .matmul => {
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.lhs, try self.matmul(seed, try self.transpose(node.rhs)));
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.rhs, try self.matmul(try self.transpose(node.lhs), seed));
                    },
                    .relu_op => {
                        // ReLU's derivative is piecewise constant, so the mask is a genuine
                        // CONSTANT here rather than a simplification - its own derivative is zero
                        // everywhere the function is differentiable at all.
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const mask: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, source.shape[0..source.rank]);
                        for (mask.data, source.data) |*m, v| {
                            m.* = if (v > 0) 1 else 0;
                        }
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.lhs, try self.mul(seed, try self.constant(mask)));
                    },
                    .sigmoid_op => {
                        // `s * (1 - s)` where `s` is this node's own output - which is already a
                        // Var, so the expression stays differentiable rather than freezing the
                        // derivative at today's values.
                        const s: Var = @intCast(i);
                        const unit: Var = try self.constant(try onesLike(T, self.gpa, self.values.items[i]));
                        const slope: Var = try self.mul(s, try self.sub(unit, s));
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.lhs, try self.mul(seed, slope));
                    },
                    .tanh_op => {
                        const t: Var = @intCast(i);
                        const unit: Var = try self.constant(try onesLike(T, self.gpa, self.values.items[i]));
                        const slope: Var = try self.sub(unit, try self.mul(t, t));
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.lhs, try self.mul(seed, slope));
                    },
                    .reduce_sum_rows_op => {
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const wide: Tensor(T) = try ones(T, self.gpa, &.{ 1, source.shape[1] });
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.lhs, try self.matmul(seed, try self.constant(wide)));
                    },
                    .transpose_op => {
                        try self.seedInto(seeds, node.lhs, try self.transpose(seed));
                    },
                    // Not a fallback to first-order: an unsupported op returns an ERROR rather
                    // than a plausible number. A second-order implementation that silently
                    // degrades produces a penalty that is constant, a gradient that is zero, and
                    // an unregularised discriminator behind curves that all look fine.
                    else => return Error.UnsupportedShape,
                }
            }
            return seeds[input] orelse self.constant(try zeros(T, self.gpa, blk: {
                const v: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[input];
                break :blk v.shape[0..v.rank];
            }));
        }

        /// Accumulate a symbolic gradient into `seeds`, adding if one is already there.
        ///
        /// A value used twice receives a contribution from each use, exactly as `accumulate`
        /// sums into a gradient tensor - the difference is that this sums with a tape `add`, so
        /// the total stays differentiable.
        fn seedInto(
            self: *Self,
            seeds: []?Var,
            target: Var,
            contribution: Var,
        ) Error!void {
            if (seeds[target]) |existing| {
                seeds[target] = try self.add(existing, contribution);
            } else {
                seeds[target] = contribution;
            }
        }

        /// LayerNorm whose gain and bias are produced by a conditioning signal.
        ///
        /// ---- THIS IS HOW THE TIMESTEP REACHES THE NETWORK ----
        ///
        /// A diffusion transformer has to behave differently at every step of the chain. The
        /// obvious way to tell it which step it is on - concatenate the embedding onto the input
        /// - makes the condition one more thing competing for attention among the tokens.
        ///
        /// AdaLayerNorm instead lets the condition set the SCALE AND OFFSET of every normalised
        /// activation. That reaches the whole layer at once and costs two small projections, and
        /// it is what DiT uses. The same mechanism conditions a policy on a goal or a latent
        /// skill, which is why this is not filed with the diffusion code.
        ///
        /// ---- THE GAIN IS `1 + scale`, NOT `scale` ----
        ///
        /// So that a zero-initialised projection is the IDENTITY: at the start of training the
        /// block passes its input through untouched and the condition does nothing, rather than
        /// multiplying everything by zero and destroying the signal before any gradient has
        /// arrived. Networks that skip this are the ones that need a careful warmup.
        ///
        /// The two conditioning inputs are `[1, width]` - one vector for the whole batch,
        /// broadcast across rows the same way `diagGaussianLogProb` broadcasts a `log_std`. They
        /// are named `conditioning_gain` and `conditioning_bias` rather than `scale` and `shift`
        /// because this file exports a tensor `scale`, and because the names then say what they
        /// DO rather than which paper they came from.
        pub fn adaptiveLayerNorm(
            self: *Self,
            x: Var,
            conditioning_gain: Var,
            conditioning_bias: Var,
            epsilon: T,
        ) Error!Var {
            const value: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(x);
            if (value.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const rows: usize = value.shape[0];
            const width: usize = value.shape[1];
            const gain_value: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(conditioning_gain);
            const bias_value: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(conditioning_bias);
            if (gain_value.rank != 2 or gain_value.shape[0] != 1 or gain_value.shape[1] != width) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            if (bias_value.rank != 2 or bias_value.shape[0] != 1 or bias_value.shape[1] != width) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }

            const row_ones: Var = try self.constant(try ones(T, self.gpa, &.{ rows, 1 }));
            const gain_wide: Var = try self.matmul(row_ones, conditioning_gain);
            const bias_wide: Var = try self.matmul(row_ones, conditioning_bias);

            const normed: Var = try self.layerNormRows(x, epsilon);
            const unit: Tensor(T) = try ones(T, self.gpa, &.{ rows, width });
            const gain: Var = try self.add(try self.constant(unit), gain_wide);
            return self.add(try self.mul(normed, gain), bias_wide);
        }

        /// Scaled dot-product attention: `softmax(Q K^T / sqrt(d)) V`.
        ///
        /// ---- COMPOSED, SO THERE IS NO NEW BACKWARD ----
        ///
        /// `matmul`, `transpose`, `scale` and `softmaxRows` are all already on the tape, so the
        /// whole of attention is an expression rather than a primitive. That was not obvious
        /// until it was checked: `Attention` exists in this file as a CPU type and cannot be
        /// trained through, which looked like a gap and was not.
        ///
        /// ---- THE `1/sqrt(d)` IS THE PART PEOPLE DROP ----
        ///
        /// `Q K^T` sums `d` products, so its variance grows with `d`. Without the scaling the
        /// scores reaching the softmax grow as the head widens, the softmax saturates toward
        /// one-hot, and **the gradient through it vanishes** - a wider model trains worse, which
        /// reads as a capacity problem and is an arithmetic one.
        ///
        /// It is a parameter rather than derived from the shape because multi-head attention
        /// scales by the HEAD dimension, not the model dimension, and the caller is the one who
        /// knows which it has just sliced.
        pub fn scaledDotProductAttention(
            self: *Self,
            query: Var,
            key: Var,
            value: Var,
            score_scale: T,
        ) Error!Var {
            const q: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(query);
            const k: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(key);
            const v: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(value);
            if (q.rank != 2 or k.rank != 2 or v.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            // Q and K must agree on the FEATURE width; K and V on the number of tokens. Checked
            // here rather than left to matmul, because a mismatch there reports the shapes of an
            // intermediate nobody wrote.
            if (q.shape[1] != k.shape[1] or k.shape[0] != v.shape[0]) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const scores: Var = try self.matmul(query, try self.transpose(key));
            const weights: Var = try self.softmaxRows(try self.scale(scores, score_scale));
            return self.matmul(weights, value);
        }

        /// The projections and feed-forward weights of one encoder block.
        ///
        /// Named rather than passed loose because six `Var`s in a row is six chances to swap two
        /// of them, and swapping `wq` with `wk` produces a model that trains, converges slowly,
        /// and is computing attention backwards.
        pub const EncoderWeights = struct {
            /// Query, key and value projections, each `[model, head]`.
            wq: Var,
            wk: Var,
            wv: Var,
            /// Output projection, `[head, model]` - back to the residual stream's width.
            wo: Var,
            /// Feed-forward, `[model, hidden]` then `[hidden, model]`.
            w1: Var,
            w2: Var,
        };

        /// One pre-norm transformer encoder block.
        ///
        /// ---- PRE-NORM, AND WHY IT IS NOT A STYLE CHOICE ----
        ///
        /// `x + f(norm(x))` rather than `norm(x + f(x))`. The difference is what the residual
        /// stream carries: in pre-norm there is a path from input to output that passes through
        /// **no normalisation at all**, so a gradient reaches the first layer undiminished.
        /// Post-norm puts a LayerNorm on that path at every layer, and the gradient shrinks with
        /// depth - which is why post-norm transformers need a learning-rate warmup to train and
        /// pre-norm ones largely do not.
        ///
        /// At two layers, which is what HIL uses, either works. The reason to default to the one
        /// that survives depth is that nobody revisits this choice when they add layers.
        ///
        /// ---- THE RESIDUALS ARE NOT DECORATION ----
        ///
        /// They are two `add` calls and they are the difference between a block that trains and
        /// one that does not past a few layers. They get simplified away by someone getting the
        /// shapes right and never put back, because the failure is slow convergence rather than
        /// an error.
        pub fn encoderBlock(
            self: *Self,
            x: Var,
            w: EncoderWeights,
            score_scale: T,
            epsilon: T,
        ) Error!Var {
            const normed: Var = try self.layerNormRows(x, epsilon);
            const attended: Var = try self.scaledDotProductAttention(
                try self.matmul(normed, w.wq),
                try self.matmul(normed, w.wk),
                try self.matmul(normed, w.wv),
                score_scale,
            );
            const mixed: Var = try self.add(x, try self.matmul(attended, w.wo));

            const normed_again: Var = try self.layerNormRows(mixed, epsilon);
            const hidden: Var = try self.relu(try self.matmul(normed_again, w.w1));
            return self.add(mixed, try self.matmul(hidden, w.w2));
        }

        /// `KL(p || q)` between two diagonal Gaussians, per row, ON THE TAPE: `p` is given by the graph values `mean`
        /// and `log_std` (a policy, being trained), `q` by the tensors `reference_mean` and `reference_log_std` (a
        /// reference the policy is pulled toward, which training must never move). The result is [rows, 1].
        ///
        /// ---- WHAT THE NUMBER MEANS ----
        ///
        /// KL(p || q) = E over x drawn from p of [log p(x) - log q(x)]: drawing from p, how much less likely q finds
        /// the draws than p does - on average, in nats. It is zero exactly when the two are the same distribution, and
        /// positive otherwise. One dimension has a closed form, and a diagonal Gaussian's dimensions are independent,
        /// so its KL is the SUM of its dimensions' KLs:
        ///
        ///     KL = sum over d of [ log(s_q / s_p) + (s_p^2 + (m_p - m_q)^2) / (2 s_q^2) - 1/2 ]
        ///
        /// Three pieces: the gap between the centres, measured in q's variance, (m_p - m_q)^2 / (2 s_q^2); p's own
        /// spread measured the same way, s_p^2 / (2 s_q^2); and the log of the width ratio. With the -1/2, the width
        /// part is (r - 1 - log r) / 2 for the variance ratio r = s_p^2 / s_q^2 - never negative, and zero only when
        /// r = 1. So the whole divergence is zero exactly when both the centres and the widths agree.
        ///
        /// ---- THE DIRECTION IS A DESIGN DECISION ----
        ///
        /// KL is not symmetric, and which distribution goes first changes what the policy learns:
        ///
        ///   * KL(policy || target) - this function's order - is MODE-SEEKING. The average is taken under the POLICY,
        ///     so the policy pays for putting probability where the target has little, and hardly at all for missing
        ///     part of the target. A narrow policy inside a wide target is cheap - KL(N(0, 1) || N(0, 2^2)) = 0.318 -
        ///     while a wide policy around a narrow target is dear - KL(N(0, 2^2) || N(0, 1)) = 0.807 (the known answers
        ///     pin both numbers). Given a target with two modes, the policy settles on one of them.
        ///   * KL(target || policy) is MASS-COVERING: the policy pays for missing any part of the target, so it spreads
        ///     to cover all of it - and, given two modes, puts its centre in the gap between them.
        ///
        /// BMPC's code distills its planner into its policy with KL(policy || planner) - this order - although its
        /// paper writes KL(planner || policy); the code is what produced the results. PPO's trust region and a motion
        /// prior use the same order: the policy is the thing being judged against a fixed reference.
        ///
        /// ---- THE REFERENCE IS DATA ----
        ///
        /// It is passed as tensors, not graph values: the planner's target (BMPC), a recorded pose (a motion prior),
        /// the old policy (a trust region) - none of them is something the optimiser may move. Passing them as tensors
        /// makes that structural: there is no way to train the reference to agree with the policy, which would lower
        /// the loss beautifully and learn nothing. The tensors are wrapped, not copied: refill them and `recompute` to
        /// score the next batch against its own targets.
        ///
        /// ---- SHAPES: ONE SPREAD PER DIMENSION, OR ONE PER ROW ----
        ///
        /// `mean` and `reference_mean` are [rows, dims]. Each log standard deviation is either [1, dims] - one spread
        /// per dimension, shared by every row: a state-independent policy's (PPO's), a motion prior's fixed variance -
        /// or [rows, dims] - one per row: a STATE-DEPENDENT policy's (BMPC's, whose network outputs a spread for every
        /// state), the planner's target (whose spread differs at every replayed state). A shared spread is copied to
        /// every row by an exact product with a column of ones; from there on the arithmetic is the same either way.
        ///
        /// ---- A SUM, NOT A MEAN; AND THE SAFE DOMAIN ----
        ///
        /// The result is the true KL, SUMMED over dimensions. BMPC averages over the action's dimensions instead -
        /// `scale` by 1 / dims to match it. The composition exponentiates 2 log s_p and -2 log s_q, which stays finite
        /// at f32 for |log s| up to about 44 - far beyond BMPC's bounds (the policy's log-std in [-3, 1], the planner's
        /// std in [0.05, 2]). Composed from existing ops, so there is no new backward to get wrong.
        pub fn klGaussianDiag(
            self: *Self,
            mean: Var,
            log_std: Var,
            reference_mean: Tensor(T),
            reference_log_std: Tensor(T),
        ) Error!Var {
            const policy_mean_value: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(mean);
            if (policy_mean_value.rank != 2 or reference_mean.rank != 2) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const row_count: usize = policy_mean_value.shape[0];
            const dimension_count: usize = policy_mean_value.shape[1];
            if (reference_mean.shape[0] != row_count or reference_mean.shape[1] != dimension_count) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const policy_spread_is_shared: bool = try logStdIsShared(self.valueOf(log_std), row_count, dimension_count);
            const reference_spread_is_shared: bool = try logStdIsShared(reference_log_std, row_count, dimension_count);

            // Every quantity as [rows, dims]: a shared spread copied to each row, a per-row one taken as it is.
            const reference_mean_rows: Var = try self.constant(reference_mean);
            const reference_log_std_given: Var = try self.constant(reference_log_std);
            const policy_log_std_rows: Var = if (policy_spread_is_shared)
                try self.copyToEveryRow(log_std, row_count)
            else
                log_std;
            const reference_log_std_rows: Var = if (reference_spread_is_shared)
                try self.copyToEveryRow(reference_log_std_given, row_count)
            else
                reference_log_std_given;

            // 1. The gap between the centres, squared: (m_p - m_q)^2.
            const mean_gap: Var = try self.sub(mean, reference_mean_rows);
            const squared_mean_gap: Var = try self.mul(mean_gap, mean_gap);
            // 2. The policy's variance, s_p^2 = exp(2 log s_p), and 1 / (2 s_q^2) = 0.5 exp(-2 log s_q).
            const policy_variance: Var = try self.exp(try self.scale(policy_log_std_rows, 2));
            const inverse_reference_variance: Var = try self.exp(try self.scale(reference_log_std_rows, -2));
            const half_inverse_reference_variance: Var = try self.scale(inverse_reference_variance, 0.5);
            // 3. The policy's spread and the centres' gap, measured in the reference's variance.
            const spread_plus_squared_gap: Var = try self.add(policy_variance, squared_mean_gap);
            const in_reference_variances: Var = try self.mul(spread_plus_squared_gap, half_inverse_reference_variance);
            // 4. The log of the width ratio: log s_q - log s_p.
            const log_std_gap: Var = try self.sub(reference_log_std_rows, policy_log_std_rows);
            // 5. Each dimension's divergence (without its -1/2), summed over the dimensions.
            const per_dimension: Var = try self.add(log_std_gap, in_reference_variances);
            const summed_over_dimensions: Var = try self.reduceRows(per_dimension, .sum);
            // 6. The -1/2 of every dimension, folded into one constant per row.
            const minus_half_per_dimension: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ row_count, 1 });
            @memset(minus_half_per_dimension.data, -0.5 * @as(T, @floatFromInt(dimension_count)));
            return self.add(summed_over_dimensions, try self.constant(minus_half_per_dimension));
        }

        /// A log standard deviation for `klGaussianDiag`: `true` when it is [1, dims] - one per dimension, shared by
        /// every row - and `false` when it is [rows, dims] - one per row. Anything else is refused.
        fn logStdIsShared(
            log_std: Tensor(T),
            row_count: usize,
            dimension_count: usize,
        ) Error!bool {
            if (log_std.rank != 2 or log_std.shape[1] != dimension_count) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            if (log_std.shape[0] == 1) {
                return true;
            }
            if (log_std.shape[0] == row_count) {
                return false;
            }
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }

        /// A [1, dims] value copied to every one of `row_count` rows - `ones[rows, 1] . value`, an exact copy (each
        /// entry is 1 * x) that the tape already knows how to differentiate: the gradient of the copy is summed back
        /// over the rows, as a shared parameter's must be. How a per-feature parameter (a layer norm's gain, a shared
        /// log-std) meets a batch wherever an op wants equal shapes (`mul` does; `add` broadcasts by itself).
        pub fn copyToEveryRow(self: *Self, value: Var, row_count: usize) Error!Var {
            const column_of_ones: Var = try self.constant(try ones(T, self.gpa, &.{ row_count, 1 }));
            return self.matmul(column_of_ones, value);
        }

        /// A [rows, 1] column copied to every one of `column_count` columns - `column . ones[1, columns]`, an exact
        /// copy (each entry is x * 1): `copyToEveryRow`'s twin, along the other axis. The gradient of the copy is
        /// summed back across the columns. How a per-row weight - a 0 / 1 mask of the rows that count - meets a
        /// [rows, width] value wherever an op wants equal shapes (`mul` does).
        pub fn copyToEveryColumn(
            self: *Self,
            column: Var,
            column_count: usize,
        ) Error!Var {
            const row_of_ones: Var = try self.constant(try ones(T, self.gpa, &.{ 1, column_count }));
            return self.matmul(column, row_of_ones);
        }

        /// The differential ENTROPY of a diagonal Gaussian, per row, ON THE TAPE - `DiagGaussian.entropy`'s twin:
        ///
        ///       entropy = sum over d of ( log s_d + log(2 pi e) / 2 )
        ///
        /// In nats. It measures the Gaussian's SPREAD, so the mean never enters: shifting a Gaussian moves it without
        /// changing its shape. `log_std` is [rows, dims] - a spread per row, as a state-dependent policy has - or
        /// [1, dims], one shared spread; the result is [rows, 1]. Its gradient is exactly 1 for every log std (making
        /// any dimension e times wider adds one nat), so an entropy BONUS in a policy loss, `- coefficient *
        /// entropy`, pushes every log std up by the same steady amount - all it is meant to do: keep a policy from
        /// collapsing to a point before its targets justify it.
        pub fn diagGaussianEntropy(self: *Self, log_std: Var) Error!Var {
            const log_std_value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[log_std];
            if (log_std_value.rank != 2 or log_std_value.shape[1] == 0) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const row_count: usize = log_std_value.shape[0];
            const dimension_count: T = @as(T, @floatFromInt(log_std_value.shape[1]));
            const summed_log_std: Var = try self.reduceRows(log_std, .sum);
            // The part that does not depend on the spread - log(2 pi e) / 2 per dimension - one constant per row.
            const shape_free_part: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ row_count, 1 });
            @memset(shape_free_part.data, dimension_count * (DiagGaussian(T).half_log_tau + 0.5));
            return self.add(summed_log_std, try self.constant(shape_free_part));
        }

        /// The log-probability a diagonal Gaussian assigns to each action, ON THE TAPE.
        ///
        /// ---- WHY THIS HAS TO EXIST FOR CONTINUOUS CONTROL ----
        ///
        /// `PpoClipLoss` takes the new log-probabilities as a `Var`, because the gradient has to
        /// flow back through them to the policy. `DiagGaussian.logProb` works on Tensors and is
        /// therefore a dead end: it gives the right number and no gradient, so a continuous
        /// policy could be evaluated and never trained. This is the missing link between a
        /// network that outputs `mean` and `log_std` and a loss that can be descended.
        ///
        /// ---- COMPOSED, NOT PRIMITIVE ----
        ///
        /// Every step is an existing differentiable op, so there is no new backward to get
        /// wrong. Two of them are doing a job their names do not suggest:
        ///
        ///   `matmul(ones[N,1], log_std[1,D])` BROADCASTS the per-dimension log_std across the
        ///   batch, because `mul` requires identical shapes here.
        ///   `matmul(per_dim[N,D], ones[D,1])` SUMS each row, because the tape has no axis
        ///   reduction. A Gaussian's log density is a sum over dimensions, so this is the step
        ///   that makes it one number per action rather than one per component.
        ///
        /// Both are differentiable by construction - a matmul against a constant is just a
        /// linear map, and the tape already knows its gradient.
        ///
        /// `actions` is a CONSTANT: they were drawn by the acting policy and are not something
        /// the update may move. Attaching them as a parameter instead would let the optimiser
        /// change history to lower the loss.
        pub fn diagGaussianLogProb(
            self: *Self,
            mean: Var,
            log_std: Var,
            actions: Tensor(T),
        ) Error!Var {
            const mean_value: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(mean);
            if (mean_value.rank != 2 or actions.rank != 2) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const rows: usize = mean_value.shape[0];
            const dims: usize = mean_value.shape[1];
            if (actions.shape[0] != rows or actions.shape[1] != dims) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            // `[1, D]`, not `[D]`. The broadcast across the batch is a matmul, and matmul needs
            // rank 2 - whereas `DiagGaussian.logProb` indexes with `at1` and wants rank 1. The
            // two spellings describe the same distribution; this one is what the tape can use.
            const std_value: Tensor(T) = self.valueOf(log_std);
            if (std_value.rank != 2 or std_value.shape[0] != 1 or std_value.shape[1] != dims) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }

            const ones_rows: Tensor(T) = try ones(T, self.gpa, &.{ rows, 1 });

            const row_ones: Var = try self.constant(ones_rows);
            const act: Var = try self.constant(actions);

            // log_std, one row per sample.
            const std_wide: Var = try self.matmul(row_ones, log_std);
            // (a - mu) / sigma, with sigma = exp(log_std).
            const centred: Var = try self.sub(act, mean);
            const inv_std: Var = try self.exp(try self.scale(std_wide, -1));
            const z: Var = try self.mul(centred, inv_std);
            // 0.5 * z^2 + log_std, per dimension.
            const half_sq: Var = try self.scale(try self.mul(z, z), 0.5);
            const per_dim: Var = try self.add(half_sq, std_wide);
            // Sum over dimensions, then the constant term, then negate.
            const summed: Var = try self.reduceRows(per_dim, .sum);
            const constant_term: T = @as(T, @floatCast(DiagGaussian(T).half_log_tau)) *
                @as(T, @floatFromInt(dims));
            const shifted: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ rows, 1 });
            @memset(shifted.data, constant_term);
            const offset: Var = try self.constant(shifted);
            return self.scale(try self.add(summed, offset), -1);
        }

        pub fn crossEntropy(self: *Self, logits: Var, labels: []const usize) Error!Var {
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ 1, 1 });
            out.data[0] = try crossEntropyRows(T, self.values.items[logits], labels);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .cross_entropy, .lhs = logits, .labels = labels },
                self.wants_grad.items[logits],
            );
        }

        /// SOFT-LABEL CROSS-ENTROPY of `logits` against the target DISTRIBUTION `targets` - both [rows, atoms] -
        /// averaged over the rows: the loss a categorical critic trains on (the math, and why it is computed in log
        /// space: `zimrnum.softCrossEntropyRows`). A single value, like every loss here.
        ///
        /// THE TARGET IS DATA, as in `mseLoss`: no gradient flows into it. A categorical critic's target is built
        /// from frozen target networks and a projection, and training must move the prediction toward it - never it
        /// toward the prediction. Refill the target's constant each update and `recompute`.
        pub fn softCrossEntropy(self: *Self, logits: Var, targets: Var) Error!Var {
            const predicted_logits: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[logits];
            const target_distribution: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[targets];
            const loss_value: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ 1, 1 });
            loss_value.data[0] = try softCrossEntropyRows(T, predicted_logits, target_distribution);
            // The target is recorded in `target` (read by the backward pass and `recompute`) but asks for no gradient:
            // only the logits decide whether this node wants one.
            const node: Node = .{ .op = .soft_cross_entropy, .lhs = logits, .target = targets };
            return self.push(loss_value, node, self.wants_grad.items[logits]);
        }

        /// Row-wise softmax of a rank-2 value.
        pub fn softmaxRows(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, va.shape[0..va.rank]);
            try zimrnum.softmaxRows(T, out, va);
