            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .softmax_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// SimNorm of a rank-2 value: a softmax within each consecutive group of `group` features - the last step of
        /// TD-MPC's encoder and dynamics (the math, and why a world model wants it: `zimrnum.simNormRows`). `group`
        /// equal to the width is `softmaxRows`. The group size travels in the node's `geometry[0]`, where the
        /// backward pass and `recompute` read it.
        pub fn simNorm(self: *Self, a: Var, group: usize) Error!Var {
            const input_value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const normalised: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, input_value.shape[0..input_value.rank]);
            try simNormRows(T, normalised, input_value, group);
            var geometry: [4]usize = @splat(0);
            geometry[0] = group;
            const node: Node = .{ .op = .sim_norm, .lhs = a, .geometry = geometry };
            return self.push(normalised, node, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// Row-wise layer normalisation of a rank-2 value.
        pub fn layerNormRows(self: *Self, a: Var, epsilon: T) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, va.shape[0..va.rank]);
            try zimrnum.layerNormRows(T, out, va, epsilon);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .layernorm_op, .lhs = a, .scalar = epsilon },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// 2-D convolution of a rank-2 value with a learned kernel.
        ///
        /// THE BACKWARD PASS IS THE FORWARD LOOP WITH THE ACCUMULATION REVERSED
        ///
        /// Forward, each output reads a window: `y[i][j] += x[i*s+a-p][j*s+b-p] * k[a][b]`. Both
        /// gradients fall out of that one line by moving what is read and what is written:
        ///
        /// dx[i*s+a-p][j*s+b-p] += g[i][j] * k[a][b]
        /// dk[a][b]             += g[i][j] * x[i*s+a-p][j*s+b-p]
        ///
        /// So the backward rule is the SAME loop nest with the same bounds and the same
        /// in-range test - which is why it is written as one pass producing both, rather than as
        /// the "full correlation with a flipped kernel" the textbooks give. That form is correct
        /// and is a second thing to get right; this one cannot disagree with the forward pass
        /// about which taps are in range, because it asks the identical question.
        pub fn conv2d(
            self: *Self,
            input: Var,
            kernel: Var,
            stride: [2]usize,
            pad: [2]usize,
        ) Error!Var {
            const x: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[input];
            const k: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[kernel];
            if (x.rank != 2 or k.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            if (stride[0] == 0 or stride[1] == 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            const out_h: usize = (x.shape[0] + 2 * pad[0] - k.shape[0]) / stride[0] + 1;
            const out_w: usize = (x.shape[1] + 2 * pad[1] - k.shape[1]) / stride[1] + 1;
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ out_h, out_w });
            try zimrnum.conv2d(T, out, x, k, stride, pad);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{
                    .op = .conv2d_op,
                    .lhs = input,
                    .rhs = kernel,
                    .geometry = .{ stride[0], stride[1], pad[0], pad[1] },
                },
                self.wants_grad.items[input] or self.wants_grad.items[kernel],
            );
        }

        /// A contiguous run along one axis, as a differentiable operation.
        ///
        /// THE VIEW ALREADY EXISTS; WHAT IS NEW IS THE GRADIENT
        ///
        /// `Tensor.slice` gives a strided view for free, but a view carries no gradient, so
        /// slicing a computed value needs this node: multi-head attention splitting a projection,
        /// an LSTM slicing four fused gates out of one matmul.
        ///
        /// The backward is a scatter: the gradient lands in the rows the slice took and every
        /// other row gets zero, because a row nobody read cannot have influenced anything.
        pub fn slice(
            self: *Self,
            a: Var,
            axis: usize,
            start: usize,
            len: usize,
        ) Error!Var {
            const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            if (axis >= source.rank or start + len > source.shape[axis]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var shape: [max_rank]usize = source.shape;
            shape[axis] = len;
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, shape[0..source.rank]);
            try materialise(T, out, try source.slice(axis, start, len));
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .slice_op, .lhs = a, .geometry = .{ axis, start, len, 0 } },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// Two values joined along one axis, as a differentiable operation.
        ///
        /// The backward is the slice's, read in the other direction: each input gets back the
        /// part of the gradient that came from its own rows.
        pub fn concat(self: *Self, a: Var, b: Var, axis: usize) Error!Var {
            const first: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const second: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[b];
            if (first.rank != second.rank or axis >= first.rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var shape: [max_rank]usize = first.shape;
            shape[axis] = first.shape[axis] + second.shape[axis];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, shape[0..first.rank]);
            try zimrnum.concat(T, out, first, second, axis);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{
                    .op = .concat_op,
                    .lhs = a,
                    .rhs = b,
                    .geometry = .{ axis, first.shape[axis], 0, 0 },
                },
                self.wants_grad.items[a] or self.wants_grad.items[b],
            );
        }

        /// Gather rows of a table by index: `out[i] = table[rows[i]]`.
        ///
        /// `rows` reuses the node's `labels` field, and here that is not a borrow - an
        /// embedding's indices ARE the same kind of thing as a classifier's labels: positions
        /// into a first axis, with no gradient of their own.
        ///
        /// The gradient is a SCATTER-ADD, and `+=` is the whole point: a token appearing three
        /// times in a batch must accumulate three contributions into one row of the table. A
        /// scatter that assigned would train on the last occurrence and silently discard the
        /// rest.
        pub fn embedding(self: *Self, table: Var, rows: []const usize) Error!Var {
            const t: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[table];
            if (t.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            for (rows) |r| {
                if (r >= t.shape[0]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
            }
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ rows.len, t.shape[1] });
            try gatherRows(T, out, t, rows);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .embedding_op, .lhs = table, .labels = rows },
                self.wants_grad.items[table],
            );
        }

        /// The transpose of a rank-2 value, MATERIALISED.
        ///
        /// A view would be free on the CPU, but a value of the graph must be a tensor a later
        /// `matmul` can read and a later `recompute` can overwrite in place - and a transposed
        /// view of another value's storage is neither. The copy is the price of the tape owning
        /// every value it records. Its gradient is the transpose of the incoming one.
        pub fn transpose(self: *Self, a: Var) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            if (va.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ va.shape[1], va.shape[0] });
            try materialise(T, out, try va.transpose(0, 1));
            return self.push(out, .{ .op = .transpose_op, .lhs = a }, self.wants_grad.items[a]);
        }

        /// THE CHOSEN COLUMNS OF EVERY ROW, DIFFERENTIABLY: out[r][k] = input[r][columns[k]] - `gatherColumns` on the
        /// graph, for a policy that watches only part of the state (an asymmetric observation).
        ///
        /// No new op: a product with a constant 0/1 SELECTION MATRIX S, [width, chosen], whose column k holds a single
        /// 1, in row columns[k]:
        ///
        ///       out = input . S          S for columns = { 3, 0 } of a width-4 input:     [ 0  1 ]
        ///                                                                                  [ 0  0 ]
        ///                                                                                  [ 0  0 ]
        ///                                                                                  [ 1  0 ]
        ///
        /// It is EXACT, not approximate: each output is one input times 1 plus every other times 0, and adding zeros
        /// changes nothing - so choosing every column in order gives back the input, equal as numbers (a negative zero
        /// comes back as zero: -0 + 0 is +0). The gradient returns through matmul's own backward, grad . S^T: each
        /// chosen column gets its output's gradient (both, summed, if chosen twice) and every other exactly zero.
        ///
        /// S is the one-hot ROWS of `columns` (`oneHotRows`), transposed. The input must be finite - 0 * infinity is
        /// NaN, so one infinite feature, chosen or not, would spoil its row. The product costs rows * width * chosen,
        /// where a dedicated gather would cost rows * chosen; for observations a few hundred wide on the CPU that is
        /// cheap, and needs no backward of its own (a dedicated gather belongs to the GPU kit).
        pub fn gatherColumns(self: *Self, input: Var, columns: []const usize) Error!Var {
            const input_value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[input];
            if (input_value.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const input_width: usize = input_value.shape[1];
            for (columns) |column| {
                if (column >= input_width) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
            }
            const picks_by_row: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ columns.len, input_width });
            try oneHotRows(T, picks_by_row, columns);
            const picks_by_row_var: Var = try self.constant(picks_by_row);
            const selection_matrix: Var = try self.transpose(picks_by_row_var);
            return self.matmul(input, selection_matrix);
        }

        /// `out = a * factor`.
        pub fn scale(self: *Self, a: Var, factor: T) Error!Var {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, va.shape[0..va.rank]);
            try zimrnum.scale(T, out, va, factor);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .scale_op, .lhs = a, .scalar = factor },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// Inverted dropout: each element is kept with probability `1 - rate` and scaled by
        /// `1 / (1 - rate)`, so the expected value is unchanged.
        ///
        /// THE MASK IS DRAWN ONCE AND STORED
        ///
        /// A dropout that redrew its mask on every `recompute` would be a different function each
        /// time - `checkGradient` would compare a derivative against a finite difference of
        /// something else and report noise. The mask is a value of the graph, fixed until
        /// `resampleDropout` is called, which a training loop does once per step.
        ///
        /// Inverted scaling means nothing changes at inference: dropping the operation from the
        /// graph is exactly equivalent to `rate = 0`, with no `1 - rate` factor to remember.
        ///
        /// ONE GENERATOR PER NODE
        ///
        /// The mask is a function of `rng` and each element's index alone, so two dropout nodes of
        /// one shape given the same generator draw the SAME mask. Give each its own split - one per
        /// network, per step, per head - as `resampleDropout` does for every node.
        pub fn dropout(self: *Self, a: Var, rate: T, rng: Rng) Error!Var {
            if (rate < 0 or rate >= 1) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[a];
            const shape: []const usize = va.shape[0..va.rank];
            const mask: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            const keep: T = 1 - rate;
            fillDropoutMask(T, mask, rng, keep);
            const mask_var: Var = try self.constant(mask);
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, shape);
            try zimrnum.mul(T, out, va, mask);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .dropout_op, .lhs = a, .mask = mask_var, .scalar = keep },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// Draw fresh masks for every dropout node, from a new generator: each node from its own
        /// split of it, `rng.split(node index)`, so no two nodes share a mask - two value heads, or
        /// one head at two steps of a rollout, drop independently, as separate forward passes would
        /// - and the masks are reproducible from the generator.
        pub fn resampleDropout(self: *Self, rng: Rng) void {
            for (self.nodes.items, 0..) |node, node_index| {
                if (node.op == .dropout_op) {
                    const node_rng: Rng = rng.split(@intCast(node_index));
                    fillDropoutMask(T, self.values.items[node.mask], node_rng, node.scalar);
                }
            }
        }

        /// Mean squared error against `target`, as a 1x1 value so it is a `Var` like any other.
        pub fn mseLoss(self: *Self, a: Var, target: Var) Error!Var {
            const out: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(self.gpa, &.{ 1, 1 });
            out.data[0] = try zimrnum.mseLoss(T, self.values.items[a], self.values.items[target]);
            return self.push(
                out,
                .{ .op = .mse, .lhs = a, .target = target },
                self.wants_grad.items[a],
            );
        }

        /// TD4'S PRE-ACTIVATION PENALTY: how far a tanh policy's pre-activations `u` ([rows, actions]) stray outside
        /// [-1, 1], squared - a [1, 1] value:
        ///
        ///       penalty = mean over rows of  sum over columns of  relu(u - 1)^2 + relu(-u - 1)^2
        ///
        ///       one value's share:    (|u| - 1)^2 outside [-1, 1], nothing inside
        ///
        ///             \                                   /
        ///              \                                 /
        ///               '.                             .'
        ///       ___________'-..___________________..-'___________  u
        ///                       -1               1
        ///
        /// A tanh actor saturates: once |u| is large, tanh(u) is flat, the critic's gradient barely reaches the
        /// weights, and the action sticks at its bound. The penalty pulls u back toward the range where tanh still
        /// responds, and costs nothing inside it. Written as two relus rather than relu(|u| - 1): an absolute value
        /// would put a kink at u = 0 on the tape, where each relu's kink sits at +-1, where the penalty is zero
        /// anyway. The actor's loss adds it with a small coefficient (TD4's; off in their benchmark).
        pub fn preActivationPenalty(self: *Self, pre_activation: Var) Error!Var {
            const pre_activation_value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[pre_activation];
            if (pre_activation_value.rank != 2) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            const column_count: usize = pre_activation_value.shape[1];
            // One row of -1s; `add` broadcasts it over every row, so u + (-1) is u - 1 everywhere.
            const minus_one_row: Var = try self.constant(try full(T, self.gpa, &.{ 1, column_count }, -1));
            const excess_above_one: Var = try self.relu(try self.add(pre_activation, minus_one_row));
            const negated_pre_activation: Var = try self.scale(pre_activation, -1);
            const excess_below_minus_one: Var = try self.relu(try self.add(negated_pre_activation, minus_one_row));
            // Per row, the sum of both excesses squared: [rows, 1].
            const above_per_row: Var = try self.squaredRowNorm(excess_above_one);
            const below_per_row: Var = try self.squaredRowNorm(excess_below_minus_one);
            const penalty_per_row: Var = try self.add(above_per_row, below_per_row);
            // The mean over rows: the column of per-row values laid on its side, then averaged across.
            const penalty_per_row_as_one_row: Var = try self.transpose(penalty_per_row);
            return self.reduceRows(penalty_per_row_as_one_row, .mean);
        }

        /// Propagate a gradient of 1 from `loss` back to every parameter.
        ///
        /// THE SEED IS 1, AND THAT IS WHY `loss` MUST BE A SINGLE VALUE. Differentiating a
        /// tensor with respect to parameters gives a Jacobian, not a gradient; reverse mode
        /// answers "how does THIS ONE NUMBER change", so the entry point takes a scalar and
        /// seeds it with its own derivative with respect to itself.
        pub fn backward(self: *Self, loss: Var) Error!void {
            // One pass's temporaries (see `scratch`): last pass's are dead by now.
            _ = self.scratch.reset(.retain_capacity);
            const temp: Allocator = self.scratch.allocator();
            if (self.values.items[loss].size() != 1) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            for (self.grads.items, self.wants_grad.items) |g, wants| {
                if (wants) {
                    g.fill(0);
                }
            }
            self.grads.items[loss].fill(1);

            var i: usize = self.nodes.items.len;
            while (i > 0) {
                i -= 1;
                if (!self.wants_grad.items[i]) {
                    continue;
                }
                const node: Node = self.nodes.items[i];
                const g: Tensor(T) = self.grads.items[i];
                switch (node.op) {
                    .leaf => {},
                    .add => {
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, g);
                    },
                    .sub_op => {
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, g);
                        const shape: []const usize = g.shape[0..g.rank];
                        const negated: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        try neg(T, negated, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, negated);
                    },
                    .reduce_sum_rows_op => {
                        // Every element of a row contributed equally, so each receives the row's
                        // whole incoming gradient. A broadcast, not a division - `mean` gets its
                        // `1/D` from the `scale` it is composed with.
                        const in: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const rows: usize = in.shape[0];
                        const cols: usize = in.shape[1];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, &.{ rows, cols });
                        for (0..rows) |r| {
                            const share: T = g.data[g.base + r];
                            for (0..cols) |c| {
                                scratch.data[scratch.base + r * cols + c] = share;
                            }
                        }
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .reduce_max_rows_op => {
                        // The mask says which element the value came from, so this is the same
                        // broadcast as `.sum` with the losers zeroed. No argmax is computed here
                        // - see `reduceRows` for why re-deriving it would be a second decision.
                        const in: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const mask: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.mask];
                        const rows: usize = in.shape[0];
                        const cols: usize = in.shape[1];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, &.{ rows, cols });
                        for (0..rows) |r| {
                            const share: T = g.data[g.base + r];
                            for (0..cols) |c| {
                                const at: usize = r * cols + c;
                                scratch.data[scratch.base + at] = share * mask.data[mask.base + at];
                            }
                        }
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .min_op => {
                        // ---- THE GRADIENT GOES TO WHICHEVER SIDE WON ----
                        //
                        // `min` is not differentiable where the two inputs are equal, and that
                        // is not a corner case to be clever about: the convention is to route
                        // the whole gradient to ONE side and zero to the other. Splitting it
                        // evenly at ties would be defensible in isolation and wrong in context -
                        // DroQ takes the min over dropout masks of the SAME network, so the two
                        // inputs are frequently equal early in training when dropout has not yet
                        // diversified them, and a half-gradient to each would quietly halve the
                        // effective learning rate exactly when the critic is furthest from right.
                        //
                        // Ties go to the LEFT, matching `@min`'s own behaviour and the
                        // lowest-index rule `Categorical.greedy` uses: decided, not better.
                        const left: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const right: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.rhs];
                        const shape: []const usize = g.shape[0..g.rank];
                        const to_left: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        const to_right: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        for (g.data, left.data, right.data, 0..) |grad, l, r, k| {
                            const left_wins: bool = l <= r;
                            to_left.data[k] = if (left_wins) grad else 0;
                            to_right.data[k] = if (left_wins) 0 else grad;
                        }
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, to_left);
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, to_right);
                    },
                    .mul_op => {
                        // Each side's gradient is the OTHER side's value times the incoming one.
                        const shape: []const usize = g.shape[0..g.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        try zimrnum.mul(T, scratch, g, self.values.items[node.rhs]);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                        const other: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        try zimrnum.mul(T, other, g, self.values.items[node.lhs]);
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, other);
                    },
                    // A stop-gradient: whatever arrived here goes no further.
                    .detach => {},
                    .relu_op => {
                        // reluGrad takes the forward INPUT, which is what `lhs` holds.
                        const in: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const shape: []const usize = in.shape[0..in.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        try reluGrad(T, scratch, in, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .sigmoid_op => {
                        // sigmoidGrad takes the forward OUTPUT, which is this node's value.
                        const out: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        try sigmoidGrad(T, scratch, out, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .cross_entropy => {
                        const in: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const shape: []const usize = in.shape[0..in.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        try crossEntropyRowsGrad(T, scratch, in, node.labels);
                        try zimrnum.scale(T, scratch, scratch, g.data[g.base]);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .soft_cross_entropy => {
                        // d loss / d logits = (target mass * predicted - target) / rows, the row's target mass being
                        // the sum of its target - predicted - target for a target that is a distribution, and exactly
                        // zero for an all-zero target row (how WorldModelLoss leaves out a finished window) - times
                        // the gradient arriving from above (1 when this IS the loss; anything else when the loss was
                        // scaled or summed on top of it). Nothing flows into the target: it is data.
                        const predicted_logits: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const shape: []const usize = predicted_logits.shape[0..predicted_logits.rank];
                        const logit_gradient: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        const target_distribution: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.target];
                        try softCrossEntropyRowsGrad(T, logit_gradient, predicted_logits, target_distribution);
                        try zimrnum.scale(T, logit_gradient, logit_gradient, g.data[g.base]);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, logit_gradient);
                    },
                    .softmax_op => {
                        // dx = y * (g - sum(g * y)) per row: the Jacobian of a softmax applied
                        // to g, without forming it.
                        const y: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, y.shape[0..y.rank]);
                        try softmaxBackward(T, scratch, y, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .sim_norm => {
                        // A softmax's backward, group by group, from this node's OUTPUT (as softmax's is).
                        const normalised_output: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const shape: []const usize = normalised_output.shape[0..normalised_output.rank];
                        const input_gradient: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        const group: usize = node.geometry[0];
                        try simNormRowsGrad(T, input_gradient, normalised_output, g, group);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, input_gradient);
                    },
                    .layernorm_op => {
                        const y: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const x: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, y.shape[0..y.rank]);
                        try layerNormRowsBackward(T, scratch, x, g, node.scalar);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .conv2d_op => {
                        const x: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const k: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.rhs];
                        const dx: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, x.shape[0..2]);
                        const dk: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, k.shape[0..2]);
                        dx.fill(0);
                        dk.fill(0);
                        try conv2dBackward(T, dx, dk, x, k, g, node.geometry);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, dx);
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, dk);
                    },
                    .embedding_op => {
                        const t: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const dt: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, t.shape[0..2]);
                        dt.fill(0);
                        try scatterAddRows(T, dt, g, node.labels);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, dt);
                    },
                    .transpose_op => {
                        const in: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, in.shape[0..in.rank]);
                        try materialise(T, scratch, try g.transpose(0, 1));
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .exp_op => {
                        // `d/dx exp(x)` is `exp(x)` - the value already computed, so the
                        // backward reads the forward's output rather than recomputing it.
                        const value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                            temp,
                            value.shape[0..value.rank],
                        );
                        try zimrnum.mul(T, scratch, g, value);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .log_op => {
                        // `d/dx log(x)` is `1/x`, and `x` is the INPUT - the one case here where
                        // the output is not enough.
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                            temp,
                            source.shape[0..source.rank],
                        );
                        try zimrnum.div(T, scratch, g, source);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .softplus_op => {
                        // `d/dx softplus(x)` is exactly `sigmoid(x)` - no branch and nothing to
                        // derive, which is what makes a dedicated node cheap despite the stable
                        // forward being more than one line.
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                            temp,
                            source.shape[0..source.rank],
                        );
                        try zimrnum.sigmoid(T, scratch, source);
                        try zimrnum.mul(T, scratch, g, scratch);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .elu_op => {
                        // `1` above zero, `alpha * exp(x)` below - read from the INPUT, because
                        // only the input says which side of the branch a value was on.
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                            temp,
                            source.shape[0..source.rank],
                        );
                        var walk: Walk = .over(source.shape[0..source.rank]);
                        while (walk.next()) |at| {
                            const x: T = try source.at(at);
                            const slope: T = if (x > 0) 1 else node.scalar * @exp(x);
                            try scratch.setAt(at, (try g.at(at)) * slope);
                        }
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .mish_op => {
                        // The slope read from the INPUT, one exponential per element (`zm.mishSlope`).
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                            temp,
                            source.shape[0..source.rank],
                        );
                        var walk: Walk = .over(source.shape[0..source.rank]);
                        while (walk.next()) |at| {
                            const slope: T = zm.mishSlope(try source.at(at));
                            try scratch.setAt(at, (try g.at(at)) * slope);
                        }
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .slice_op => {
                        // A SCATTER: the gradient lands where the slice read, and every other
                        // row gets zero - a row nobody read influenced nothing.
                        const source: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                            temp,
                            source.shape[0..source.rank],
                        );
                        scratch.fill(0);
                        const window: Tensor(T) = try scratch.slice(
                            node.geometry[0],
                            node.geometry[1],
                            node.geometry[2],
                        );
                        var walk: Walk = .over(g.shape[0..g.rank]);
                        while (walk.next()) |at| {
                            try window.setAt(at, try g.at(at));
                        }
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .concat_op => {
                        // The slice's backward read the other way: each input takes back the
                        // part of the gradient that came from its own rows.
                        const axis: usize = node.geometry[0];
                        const split: usize = node.geometry[1];
                        const left: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const right: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.rhs];
                        const left_grad: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                            temp,
                            left.shape[0..left.rank],
                        );
                        try materialise(T, left_grad, try g.slice(axis, 0, split));
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, left_grad);
                        const right_grad: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                            temp,
                            right.shape[0..right.rank],
                        );
                        try materialise(T, right_grad, try g.slice(axis, split, right.shape[axis]));
                        try self.accumulate(node.rhs, right_grad);
                    },
                    .scale_op => {
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, g.shape[0..g.rank]);
                        try zimrnum.scale(T, scratch, g, node.scalar);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .dropout_op => {
                        // The same mask that scaled the forward pass scales the gradient.
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, g.shape[0..g.rank]);
                        try zimrnum.mul(T, scratch, g, self.values.items[node.mask]);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .matmul => try self.matmulBackward(node, g),
                    .tanh_op => {
                        const out: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                        const shape: []const usize = out.shape[0..out.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        try tanhGrad(T, scratch, out, g);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                    .mse => {
                        const a: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const shape: []const usize = a.shape[0..a.rank];
                        const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(temp, shape);
                        try zimrnum.sub(T, scratch, a, self.values.items[node.target]);
                        const n: T = @floatFromInt(a.size());
                        try zimrnum.scale(T, scratch, scratch, 2 * g.data[g.base] / n);
                        try self.accumulate(node.lhs, scratch);
                    },
                }
            }
        }

        /// Replay the recorded operations forward, refreshing every computed value.
        ///
        /// WHAT MAKES THE TAPE A MODEL RATHER THAN A ONE-SHOT
        ///
        /// Without this a graph is single-use: a training loop would have to rebuild it every
        /// step, allocating the whole tape again for a forward pass that differs only in the
        /// numbers. Replaying instead writes new values into the buffers already allocated.
        ///
        /// It is also what makes `checkGradient` possible. Verifying a derivative numerically
        /// means evaluating the SAME function at a perturbed input, and "the same function" is
        /// exactly what the tape is a record of.
        ///
        /// Leaves are skipped: their values are whatever the caller last wrote, which is how a
        /// new batch of inputs enters an existing graph.
        pub fn recompute(self: *Self) Error!void {
            for (self.nodes.items, 0..) |node, i| {
                const out: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[i];
                switch (node.op) {
                    .leaf => {},
                    .add => try zimrnum.add(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                    ),
                    .sub_op => try zimrnum.sub(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                    ),
                    .mul_op => try zimrnum.mul(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                    ),
                    .min_op => try zimrnum.minimum(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                    ),
                    .matmul => try zimrnum.matmul(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                    ),
                    .tanh_op => try zimrnum.tanh(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .detach => try zimrnum.scale(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs], 1),
                    .relu_op => try zimrnum.relu(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .sigmoid_op => try zimrnum.sigmoid(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .softmax_op => try zimrnum.softmaxRows(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .sim_norm => try simNormRows(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs], node.geometry[0]),
                    .layernorm_op => try zimrnum.layerNormRows(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        node.scalar,
                    ),
                    .conv2d_op => try zimrnum.conv2d(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                        .{ node.geometry[0], node.geometry[1] },
                        .{ node.geometry[2], node.geometry[3] },
                    ),
                    .embedding_op => try gatherRows(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        node.labels,
                    ),
                    .transpose_op => try materialise(
                        T,
                        out,
                        try self.values.items[node.lhs].transpose(0, 1),
                    ),
                    .exp_op => try zimrnum.exp(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .log_op => try zimrnum.log(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .softplus_op => try zimrnum.softplus(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .elu_op => try zimrnum.elu(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs], node.scalar),
                    .mish_op => try zimrnum.mish(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs]),
                    .slice_op => try materialise(T, out, try self.values.items[node.lhs].slice(
                        node.geometry[0],
                        node.geometry[1],
                        node.geometry[2],
                    )),
                    .concat_op => try zimrnum.concat(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.rhs],
                        node.geometry[0],
                    ),
                    .scale_op => try zimrnum.scale(T, out, self.values.items[node.lhs], node.scalar),
                    .reduce_sum_rows_op => {
                        const in: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
                        const cols: usize = in.shape[1];
                        for (0..in.shape[0]) |r| {
                            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
                            for (0..cols) |c| {
                                total.add(in.data[in.base + r * cols + c]);
                            }
                            out.data[out.base + r] = total.value();
                        }
                    },
                    .reduce_max_rows_op => fillRowMaxAndMask(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.mask],
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                    ),
                    .dropout_op => try zimrnum.mul(
                        T,
                        out,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.mask],
                    ),
                    .cross_entropy => out.data[out.base] = try crossEntropyRows(
                        T,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        node.labels,
                    ),
                    .soft_cross_entropy => out.data[out.base] = try softCrossEntropyRows(
                        T,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.target],
                    ),
                    .mse => out.data[out.base] = try zimrnum.mseLoss(
                        T,
                        self.values.items[node.lhs],
                        self.values.items[node.target],
                    ),
                }
            }
        }

        /// The largest disagreement between the tape's gradient for `param` and a central finite
        /// difference of `loss`, over every element of `param`.
        ///
        /// THE ONE CHECK THAT DOES NOT REPEAT THE DERIVATION
        ///
        /// A hand-written backward pass and a tape rule can both be wrong in the same way,
        /// because the same person derived them. A finite difference does not know the
        /// derivative at all - it only evaluates the function twice - so agreement between the
        /// two is evidence about the derivative rather than about the deriver.
        ///
        /// CENTRAL, NOT FORWARD. `(f(x+h) - f(x-h)) / 2h` has error O(h^2) where
        /// `(f(x+h) - f(x)) / h` has O(h); at a usable `h` that is the difference between
        /// agreeing to six digits and to three, which decides whether a real error is visible
        /// above the noise.
        ///
        /// Every value is restored and the graph recomputed before returning, so calling this
        /// leaves the model exactly as it found it.
        pub fn checkGradient(self: *Self, loss: Var, param: Var, h: T) Error!T {
            comptime requireFloat(T);
            if (h <= 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            try self.recompute();
            try self.backward(loss);
            const analytic: Tensor(T) = try self.gradOf(param);
            const value: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[param];
            const scratch: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                self.gpa,
                analytic.shape[0..analytic.rank],
            );
            try materialise(T, scratch, analytic);

            var worst: T = 0;
            var index: usize = 0;
            while (index < value.size()) : (index += 1) {
                const at: usize = value.base + index;
                const original: T = value.data[at];
                value.data[at] = original + h;
                try self.recompute();
                const up: T = self.values.items[loss].data[self.values.items[loss].base];
                value.data[at] = original - h;
                try self.recompute();
                const down: T = self.values.items[loss].data[self.values.items[loss].base];
                value.data[at] = original;
                const numeric: T = (up - down) / (2 * h);
                worst = @max(worst, @abs(numeric - scratch.data[scratch.base + index]));
            }
            try self.recompute();
            return worst;
        }

        /// `grad[v] += contribution`, summing over any axis `v` was broadcast along.
        ///
        /// THE SUM IS WHAT MAKES BROADCASTING DIFFERENTIABLE. A bias of shape (1, n) added to
        /// (m, n) is used m times in the forward pass, so its gradient is the sum of m
        /// contributions. Adding the raw gradient instead would under-count by a factor of m and
        /// produce a bias that learns m times too slowly - a bug that leaves the network training,
        /// just badly, which is the hardest kind to notice.
        fn accumulate(self: *Self, v: Var, contribution: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
            if (!self.wants_grad.items[v]) {
                return;
            }
            const dest: Tensor(T) = self.grads.items[v];
            if (dest.size() == contribution.size()) {
                return zimrnum.add(T, dest, dest, contribution);
            }
            // SUM OVER EVERY BROADCAST AXIS, NOT JUST THE LEADING ONE
            //
            // The first version summed rows only, which is right for a `(1, width)` bias under a
            // `(rows, width)` gradient - `Dense`'s case, and the only one that existed. A `(1, 1)`
            // bias broadcasting over BOTH axes, which `Conv2d` has, took column 0 and **silently
            // dropped the rest**: a gradient wrong by a factor of the width, reported as a
            // number rather than an error. The finite-difference check caught it at 0.7 where
            // the kernel and input were at 8e-12.
            //
            // An axis of the destination that is 1 where the contribution is larger is a
            // broadcast axis and is summed; an axis that matches is walked. Any other pairing is
            // a shape error, which is what the first version should have returned.
            if (dest.rank != contribution.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < dest.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                const same: bool = dest.shape[axis] == contribution.shape[axis];
                if (!same and dest.shape[axis] != 1) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var target: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            const shape: []const usize = contribution.shape[0..contribution.rank];
            var remaining: usize = contribution.size();
            while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
                axis = 0;
                while (axis < dest.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                    target[axis] = if (dest.shape[axis] == 1) 0 else walker[axis];
                }
                const at: []const usize = target[0..dest.rank];
                const slot: usize = dest.offsetOf(at);
                dest.data[slot] += contribution.data[contribution.offsetOf(walker[0..contribution.rank])];
                advance(shape, walker[0..contribution.rank]);
            }
        }

        /// `dA = g @ BT` and `dB = AT @ g`.
        fn matmulBackward(self: *Self, node: Node, g: Tensor(T)) Error!void {
            const va: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.lhs];
            const vb: Tensor(T) = self.values.items[node.rhs];
            if (self.wants_grad.items[node.lhs]) {
                // A pass temporary like every other in `backward`: from `scratch` (see there).
                const da: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                    self.scratch.allocator(),
                    &.{ va.shape[0], va.shape[1] },
                );
                try zimrnum.matmul(T, da, g, try vb.transpose(0, 1));
                try self.accumulate(node.lhs, da);
            }
            if (self.wants_grad.items[node.rhs]) {
                const db: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(
                    self.scratch.allocator(),
                    &.{ vb.shape[0], vb.shape[1] },
                );
                try zimrnum.matmul(T, db, try va.transpose(0, 1), g);
                try self.accumulate(node.rhs, db);
            }
        }
    };
}
Tensor
/// A view of `T`-typed storage as a multidimensional array.
///
/// A TENSOR NEVER OWNS ITS STORAGE, AND THAT IS THE DESIGN
///
/// There is no ownership flag and no `deinit`. `data` is a slice the tensor addresses; whoever
/// allocated it frees it, with the allocator that allocated it. Usually that is an arena, in
/// which case nobody frees anything and `arena.deinit()` ends the matter.
///
/// The alternative - an `owns: bool` and a `deinit` that is a no-op for views - is the shape
/// this library was warned about before it had a tensor: both of its failure modes are SILENT.
/// `deinit` on a view does nothing and reads as if it did something; a missing `deinit` on an
/// owner just grows memory. Having no `deinit` at all means a free appears exactly where an
/// allocation appears, and a view has neither.
///
/// Strides are in ELEMENTS and signed, so a reversed or negatively-strided view is expressible
/// without changing this type. `at` computes a signed offset and casts once.
pub fn Tensor(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The storage this tensor addresses. Not owned - see the type's doc comment.
        data: []T,
        /// Extent of each axis. Only the first `rank` entries are meaningful.
        shape: [max_rank]usize,
        /// Step in ELEMENTS between consecutive positions along each axis.
        strides: [max_rank]isize,
        rank: u8,
        /// WHERE THIS VIEW STARTS INSIDE `data`
        ///
        /// Every view until now began at element 0, which is why `slice` could not exist: a
        /// window into the middle of a tensor IS an offset. With this, `slice` and the other
        /// indexing operations become views rather than copies.
        ///
        /// IT ALSO WEAKENS WHAT `data.len` MEANS. `data` is the whole backing store; `base`
        /// and the strides say which of it this view addresses. Everything that walked
        /// `data[0..size()]` assuming those coincide had to be corrected - five sites, all of
        /// them fast paths that skip the index walk, which is exactly where such an assumption
        /// hides.
        base: usize = 0,

        /// A dense row-major tensor over `shape`, allocated from `gpa`.
        ///
        /// The caller frees `result.data` with the same allocator - or allocates from an arena
        /// and frees nothing.
        pub fn alloc(gpa: Allocator, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            const storage: []T = gpa.alloc(T, count) catch return Error.OutOfMemory;
            return fromSlice(storage, shape);
        }

        /// A dense row-major view of `data` as `shape`. `data.len` must be exactly the product of
        /// the extents - a slice that is merely LARGE ENOUGH is rejected, because the usual reason
        /// for the mismatch is a shape that is wrong rather than storage that is generous.
        pub fn fromSlice(data: []T, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != data.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }

            var self: Self = .{
                .data = data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                .base = 0,
            };
            // Row-major: the last axis is contiguous, and each earlier stride is the product of
            // the extents to its right.
            var stride: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = shape.len;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                self.shape[axis] = shape[axis];
                self.strides[axis] = stride;
                stride *= @intCast(shape[axis]);
            }
            return self;
        }

        /// The number of elements this tensor addresses.
        pub fn size(self: Self) usize {
            var count: usize = 1;
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                count *= self.shape[axis];
            }
            return count;
        }

        /// Whether the strides are dense row-major, so `data[0..size()]` is the tensor in order.
        ///
        /// An operation that walks memory linearly must ask this rather than assume it: a view
        /// produced by `transpose` addresses the same storage in a different order, and a linear
        /// walk over it silently reads the wrong elements rather than failing.
        pub fn isContiguous(self: Self) bool {
            var expected: isize = 1;
            var axis: usize = self.rank;
            while (axis > 0) {
                axis -= 1;
                if (self.shape[axis] != 1 and self.strides[axis] != expected) {
                    return false;
                }
                expected *= @intCast(self.shape[axis]);
            }
            return true;
        }

        /// The offset into `data` of the element at `indices`.
        pub fn flatIndex(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!usize {
            if (indices.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                if (i >= self.shape[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// The element at `indices`.
        pub fn at(self: Self, indices: []const usize) Error!T {
            return self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)];
        }

        /// The flat offset for a coordinate, with no error.
        ///
        /// THE 45 `flatIndex(...) catch unreachable` SITES ARE ALL THIS SHAPE
        ///
        ///     const at: []const usize = walker[0..out.rank];
        ///     out.data[out.offsetOf(at)] = ...
        ///
        /// The slice is `walker[0..rank]`, so `indices.len != rank` **cannot** happen - the
        /// caller built it from the rank. And the coordinate came from a `Walk` over this very
        /// shape, so the bounds cannot fail either. Two impossible checks, one `catch
        /// unreachable`, on the hottest line in the library.
        ///
        /// This computes the offset and returns it. The remaining safety is `data[offset]`,
        /// which is where Zig puts it: a panic with a message in Debug and ReleaseSafe.
        ///
        /// **Use it where the rank is an invariant you have already established** - after a
        /// shape check, or inside a walk over the tensor's own shape. Use `flatIndex` where the
        /// coordinate came from outside and might be wrong.
        pub fn offsetOf(self: Self, indices: []const usize) usize {
            var offset: isize = @intCast(self.base);
            for (indices, 0..) |i, axis| {
                offset += self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            }
            return @intCast(offset);
        }

        /// Read from a rank-2 tensor. **Does not return an error.**
        ///
        /// WHY `at` HAS TO AND THIS DOES NOT
        ///
        /// `at(indices: []const usize)` can fail for two reasons. One is a real bounds check.
        /// The other is `indices.len != rank` - and that check exists ONLY because a slice hides
        /// its arity from the type system. Every call site writes a literal `&.{ i, j }`; the
        /// compiler could know it is two, and does not.
        ///
        /// Naming the arity in the function removes that half entirely. What remains is the
        /// bounds check, and **Zig already has a name for that**: it is what `data[idx]` does -
        /// a panic with a message and a stack trace in Debug and ReleaseSafe, the same UB as any
        /// other slice index in ReleaseFast.
        ///
        /// Nobody writes `arr[i] catch unreachable`. This file had **105 `catch unreachable`**,
        /// most of them on an `at` whose rank the author had checked three lines earlier, and
        /// that noise is the single biggest thing that makes numeric Zig look worse than it is.
        ///
        /// The safety is not weaker - it is Zig's, in the place Zig puts it.
        pub fn at2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-2 tensor. The mirror of `at2`, and error-free for the same reason.
        pub fn setAt2(self: Self, i: usize, j: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i)) +
                self.strides[1] * @as(isize, @intCast(j));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Read from a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn at1(self: Self, i: usize) T {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            return self.data[@intCast(offset)];
        }

        /// Write to a rank-1 tensor, error-free.
        pub fn setAt1(self: Self, i: usize, value: T) void {
            const offset: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) +
                self.strides[0] * @as(isize, @intCast(i));
            self.data[@intCast(offset)] = value;
        }

        /// Write `value` at `indices`.
        pub fn setAt(self: Self, indices: []const usize, value: T) Error!void {
            self.data[try self.flatIndex(indices)] = value;
        }

        /// Set every element to `value`.
        pub fn fill(self: Self, value: T) void {
            if (self.isContiguous()) {
                @memset(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], value);
                return;
            }
            var walker: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var remaining: usize = self.size();
            while (remaining > 0) : (remaining -= 1) {
                self.data[self.offsetOf(walker[0..self.rank])] = value;
                advance(self.shape[0..self.rank], walker[0..self.rank]);
            }
        }

        /// The same storage seen as `shape`. Requires a contiguous tensor and an equal element
        /// count; both are checked rather than assumed.
        pub fn reshape(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (!self.isContiguous()) {
                return Error.UnsupportedShape;
            }
            var count: usize = 1;
            for (shape) |extent| {
                count *= extent;
            }
            if (count != self.size()) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            // From `base`, not 0: reshaping a slice must keep addressing that slice.
            return fromSlice(self.data[self.base..][0..self.size()], shape);
        }

        /// Whether two distinct index positions address the same element, which happens exactly
        /// when a stretched axis (stride 0) has extent above 1.
        ///
        /// An operation that WRITES must refuse an aliased destination. Many indices landing on
        /// one element means the last write wins and every earlier one vanishes - a result that is
        /// wrong in a way no bounds check catches. `map` and `zip` check this so a caller who
        /// passes a broadcast view as an output gets an error rather than a plausible answer.
        pub fn isAliased(self: Self) bool {
            var axis: usize = 0;
            while (axis < self.rank) : (axis += 1) {
                if (self.shape[axis] > 1 and self.strides[axis] == 0) {
                    return true;
                }
            }
            return false;
        }

        /// A read-only view of this tensor stretched to `shape` by the broadcasting rule.
        ///
        /// BROADCASTING IS A STRIDE OF ZERO, NOT A COPY
        ///
        /// An axis whose extent is 1 is stretched by setting its stride to 0, so every position
        /// along it addresses the same element. No storage is allocated and no data moves; a
        /// `(3, 1, 5)` tensor viewed as `(3, 4, 5)` is the same 15 values read four times.
        ///
        /// **WRITING THROUGH A STRETCHED VIEW IS ALMOST CERTAINLY A MISTAKE.** Many indices
        /// alias one element, so the last write wins and the others vanish. This returns a view
        /// because that is what the type system has; treat it as an input.
        ///
        /// `isContiguous` reports false for any stretched axis with extent > 1, which is what
        /// stops a linear walk from reading the underlying 15 values as if they were 60.
        pub fn broadcastTo(self: Self, shape: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (shape.len > max_rank or shape.len < self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = .{
                .data = self.data,
                .shape = @splat(1),
                .strides = @splat(0),
                .rank = @intCast(shape.len),
                // Stretching does not move where the view starts.
                .base = self.base,
            };
            // Align at the TRAILING axis, as the broadcast rule does: an axis this tensor does not
            // have behaves as extent 1, and so does an axis it has with extent 1. Both get a
            // stride of 0.
            for (shape, 0..) |extent, axis| {
                const from_end: usize = shape.len - axis;
                view.shape[axis] = extent;
                if (self.rank < from_end) {
                    continue;
                }
                const own_axis: usize = self.rank - from_end;
                const own_extent: usize = self.shape[own_axis];
                if (own_extent == extent) {
                    view.strides[axis] = self.strides[own_axis];
                } else if (own_extent != 1) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A window into this tensor: `len` positions along `axis`, starting at `start`.
        ///
        /// A SLICE IS AN OFFSET, WHICH IS WHY `base` EXISTS
        ///
        /// Nothing is copied: the view keeps every stride and simply starts `start` steps further
        /// into the data along one axis. A batch of 32 rows out of 4096 is a `Tensor` the same
        /// size as any other, addressing 32 rows of the same buffer.
        ///
        /// `start + len` must fit. Clamping instead would hand back a view SMALLER than asked
        /// for, and the caller's next loop would read the shape it requested rather than the one
        /// it got.
        pub fn slice(self: Self, axis: usize, start: usize, len: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (start + len > self.shape[axis]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            const step: isize = self.strides[axis] * @as(isize, @intCast(start));
            const moved: isize = @as(isize, @intCast(self.base)) + step;
            if (moved < 0) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            view.base = @intCast(moved);
            view.shape[axis] = len;
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with the axes reordered: `out` axis `i` is this tensor's axis `order[i]`.
        ///
        /// EVERY `manip` OPERATION HERE IS METADATA
        ///
        /// `permute`, `squeeze`, `unsqueeze`, `flatten` and `moveAxis` all return a view: they
        /// permute or insert entries in two fixed-size arrays and copy nothing. That is why they
        /// need no GPU kernel and no sweep row - **there is no arithmetic to disagree about.**
        /// The cost appears later, when a permuted view has to be BOUND to a kernel and must be
        /// densified first, which is the constraint recorded in section 10.3.
        ///
        /// `order` must be a permutation of `0..rank`. A repeated axis would produce a view
        /// where two indices alias, which is `isAliased`'s job to reject at write time - but
        /// catching it here names the actual mistake.
        pub fn permute(self: Self, order: []const usize) Error!Self {
            if (order.len != self.rank) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var seen: [max_rank]bool = @splat(false);
            for (order) |axis| {
                if (axis >= self.rank or seen[axis]) {
                    return Error.OutOfRange;
                }
                seen[axis] = true;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            for (order, 0..) |axis, i| {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[axis];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[axis];
            }
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis removed.
        ///
        /// Removing an axis whose extent is NOT 1 would change how many elements the view
        /// addresses, so it is `ShapeMismatch` rather than a silent reshape.
        pub fn squeeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            if (self.shape[axis] != 1) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = axis;
            while (i + 1 < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i + 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i + 1];
            }
            view.shape[self.rank - 1] = 1;
            view.strides[self.rank - 1] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank - 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A view with a size-1 axis inserted at `axis`. `axis == rank` appends.
        ///
        /// The new axis gets a stride of 0, which is harmless because its extent is 1 - a
        /// single position cannot step anywhere. `isAliased` only objects when extent exceeds 1.
        pub fn unsqueeze(self: Self, axis: usize) Error!Self {
            if (axis > self.rank or self.rank + 1 > max_rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            var i: usize = self.rank;
            while (i > axis) : (i -= 1) {
                view.shape[i] = self.shape[i - 1];
                view.strides[i] = self.strides[i - 1];
            }
            view.shape[axis] = 1;
            view.strides[axis] = 0;
            view.rank = @intCast(self.rank + 1);
            return view;
        }

        /// A rank-1 view of the whole tensor. Contiguous only, for `reshape`'s reason.
        pub fn flatten(self: Self) Error!Self {
            return self.reshape(&.{self.size()});
        }

        /// A view with one axis moved from `from` to `to`, the others keeping their order.
        pub fn moveAxis(self: Self, from: usize, to: usize) Error!Self {
            if (from >= self.rank or to >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var order: [max_rank]usize = @splat(0);
            var k: usize = 0;
            var i: usize = 0;
            while (i < self.rank) : (i += 1) {
                if (i == from) {
                    continue;
                }
                order[k] = i;
                k += 1;
            }
            // Open a slot at `to` and drop the moved axis into it.
            var j: usize = self.rank - 1;
            while (j > to) : (j -= 1) {
                order[j] = order[j - 1];
            }
            order[to] = from;
            return self.permute(order[0..self.rank]);
        }

        /// A view with axes `a` and `b` exchanged. Shares storage; changes through one are visible
        /// through the other.
        pub fn transpose(self: Self, a: usize, b: usize) Error!Self {
            if (a >= self.rank or b >= self.rank) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var view: Self = self;
            view.shape[a] = self.shape[b];
            view.shape[b] = self.shape[a];
            view.strides[a] = self.strides[b];
            view.strides[b] = self.strides[a];
            return view;
        }
    };
}
cartpole_state_dim
/// How many numbers a cartpole observation is: cart, cart rate, pole angle, pole rate.
///
/// Named because a policy network's input width has to match it, and a mismatch is a shape
/// error at best and a silently-misread state at worst.
pub const cartpole_state_dim: usize = 4;

Collecting a rollout: observe the state, sample an action, step the environment, record it.

var state = zrl.cartpoleTaskReset(f64, rng, episode, .hold);
while (steps < horizon) : (steps += 1) {
    var obs_row: [zrl.cartpole_state_dim]f64 = undefined;
    try zrl.cartpoleObserve(f64, state, &obs_row);

    const force = predicted_mean + sigma * rng.normal(f64, draw_index);
    const outcome = zrl.cartpoleTaskStep(f64, state, force, .hold);

    state = outcome.state;
    if (outcome.failed) break;
}
cartpoleTaskReset
/// A fresh episode for a named task.
///
/// The only difference is WHERE the pole starts, and it is the whole difference: `hold` begins
/// near upright, `swingup` begins near `pi` - hanging - because a swingup that started upright
/// would be a hold with a worse reward function.
pub fn cartpoleTaskReset(
    comptime T: type,
    rng: Rng,
    episode: u32,
    task: CartpoleTask,
) CartpoleState(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    var state: CartpoleState(T) = cartpoleReset(T, rng, episode);
    if (task == .swingup) {
        state.pole_rad += pi;
    }
    return state;
}
cartpole_state_dim
/// How many numbers a cartpole observation is: cart, cart rate, pole angle, pole rate.
///
/// Named because a policy network's input width has to match it, and a mismatch is a shape
/// error at best and a silently-misread state at worst.
pub const cartpole_state_dim: usize = 4;
cartpoleObserve
/// Write a state into the flat vector a policy network takes.
///
/// The order is the declaration order of `CartpoleState` and it is FIXED - a policy trained
/// against one ordering and evaluated against another produces confident nonsense, and the
/// shapes match so nothing catches it. That is the whole reason `CartpoleState` is a named
/// struct rather than a `[4]T`: the conversion happens in exactly one place, here.
pub fn cartpoleObserve(comptime T: type, state: CartpoleState(T), out: []T) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.len != cartpole_state_dim) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    out[0] = state.cart;
    out[1] = state.cart_rate;
    out[2] = state.pole_rad;
    out[3] = state.pole_rate_rad;
}
cartpoleTaskStep
/// One continuous step under a named task.
///
/// `cartpoleContinuousStep` is this with `.hold`. The dynamics are identical - a task changes what is
/// REWARDED and what ENDS an episode, never how the cart moves.
pub fn cartpoleTaskStep(
    comptime T: type,
    state: CartpoleState(T),
    force: T,
    task: CartpoleTask,
) CartpoleStep(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const advanced = cartpoleContinuousStep(T, state, force);
    const judged = cartpoleOutcome(T, advanced.state, task);
    return .{ .state = advanced.state, .reward = judged.reward, .failed = judged.failed };
}

Scoring and updating: the log-probability comes from the tape, so the gradient flows back to the policy.

const logp = try graph.diagGaussianLogProb(mean, log_std, act_leaf);
const clip_loss: zrl.PpoClipLoss(f64) = try .build(&graph, logp, old_logp, advantages, cfg.clip);

const stats = try zrl.ppoUpdate(f64, gpa, model, rollout, &adv, &ret,
                               observations, actions, cfg, rng, epochs, lr);
PpoClipLoss
/// PPO'S CLIPPED SURROGATE AS A LOSS ON A GRAPH, composed from generic operations.
///
/// For each sample, with r = exp(log p_new - log p_old) the probability ratio between the policy being trained and
/// the policy that collected the rollout, and A the sample's advantage:
///
///     objective = min( r A,  clamp(r, 1 - clip, 1 + clip) A )          loss = - mean over samples of objective
///
/// The loss is the negated mean, so it is descended like every other loss in this library. Its value per sample is
/// `ppoClipSample`, and its mean `ppoClipObjective`, which the GPU kernel shares.
///
/// ---- THE GRAPH ----
///
///     log p_new - log p_old --exp--> r --x A--------------------------------> r A ----+
///                                    |                                                min --> mean --> x -1
///                                    +--> min(r, 1 + clip) --> max(., 1 - clip) x A --+
///
/// The clamp is two `min` operations, using max(a, b) = -min(-a, -b). No operation here knows about PPO, so the graph
/// itself stays free of any reinforcement-learning algorithm.
///
/// ---- THE GRADIENT ----
///
/// `min` sends the whole gradient to the smaller input, and to the left one on a tie. Per sample, the gradient of
/// the objective with respect to log p_new is therefore:
///
///     r inside [1 - clip, 1 + clip]:    both branches are equal; the tie goes left        ->  A r
///     r > 1 + clip and A > 0:           the clamped branch is smaller and constant        ->  0
///     r < 1 - clip and A < 0:           the clamped branch is smaller and constant        ->  0
///     r > 1 + clip and A < 0, or
///     r < 1 - clip and A > 0:           the unclipped branch is smaller                   ->  A r
///
/// which is PPO's rule: a sample stops pushing once the ratio has moved past the clip in the direction its advantage
/// favours. Worked through with clip = 0.2: r = 1.5 and A = 2 give objective min(3, 2.4) = 2.4 and no gradient;
/// r = 1.5 and A = -2 give min(-3, -2.4) = -3 and a gradient of A r = -3, which keeps lowering the ratio.
///
/// ---- THE INPUTS CHANGE EVERY MINIBATCH ----
///
/// The old log-probabilities and the advantages are constants the loss owns, shaped like `log_prob_new`. A minibatch
/// loop writes the next stride's values with `setInputs` before `recompute`; without that, every stride would be
/// optimised against the first one's numbers.
pub fn PpoClipLoss(comptime T: type) type {
    requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The loss, [1, 1]: minus the mean clipped surrogate.
        loss: Var,
        /// The log-probabilities under the rollout's policy, shaped like `log_prob_new`.
        log_prob_old: Tensor(T),
        /// The advantages, shaped like `log_prob_new`.
        advantages: Tensor(T),

        /// The loss of `log_prob_new` ([N, 1] or [1, N], one value per sample) on `graph`. `log_prob_old` and
        /// `advantages` hold one value per sample and are copied into tensors allocated with the graph's allocator.
        pub fn build(
            graph: *Graph(T),
            log_prob_new: Var,
            log_prob_old: []const T,
            advantages: []const T,
            clip: T,
        ) Error!Self {
            const fresh: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(log_prob_new);
            const sample_count: usize = fresh.size();
            if (sample_count != log_prob_old.len or log_prob_old.len != advantages.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            if (clip <= 0 or sample_count == 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            const is_column: bool = fresh.rank == 2 and fresh.shape[1] == 1;
            const is_row: bool = fresh.rank == 2 and fresh.shape[0] == 1;
            if (!is_column and !is_row) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const shape: []const usize = fresh.shape[0..fresh.rank];
            const old_values: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(graph.gpa, shape);
            @memcpy(old_values.data, log_prob_old);
            const advantage_values: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(graph.gpa, shape);
            @memcpy(advantage_values.data, advantages);
            const upper_bound: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(graph.gpa, shape);
            upper_bound.fill(1 + clip);
