            const negated_lower_bound: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(graph.gpa, shape);
            negated_lower_bound.fill(-(1 - clip));
            const advantage: Var = try graph.constant(advantage_values);
            // r = exp(log p_new - log p_old), and the unclipped branch r A.
            const ratio: Var = try graph.exp(try graph.sub(log_prob_new, try graph.constant(old_values)));
            const unclipped: Var = try graph.mul(ratio, advantage);
            // clamp(r, 1 - clip, 1 + clip) = max(min(r, 1 + clip), 1 - clip), with max(a, b) = -min(-a, -b).
            const below_upper: Var = try graph.min(ratio, try graph.constant(upper_bound));
            const negated_clamped: Var = try graph.min(
                try graph.scale(below_upper, -1),
                try graph.constant(negated_lower_bound),
            );
            const clipped: Var = try graph.mul(try graph.scale(negated_clamped, -1), advantage);
            // The smaller branch per sample (ties to the left: the unclipped one), then minus the mean.
            const surrogate: Var = try graph.min(unclipped, clipped);
            const surrogate_row: Var = if (is_column) try graph.transpose(surrogate) else surrogate;
            const mean_surrogate: Var = try graph.reduceRows(surrogate_row, .mean);
            return .{
                .loss = try graph.scale(mean_surrogate, -1),
                .log_prob_old = old_values,
                .advantages = advantage_values,
            };
        }

        /// Write the next minibatch's old log-probabilities and advantages, one value per sample; `recompute` then
        /// evaluates the loss on them.
        pub fn setInputs(
            self: Self,
            log_prob_old: []const T,
            advantages: []const T,
        ) Error!void {
            if (log_prob_old.len != self.log_prob_old.data.len or advantages.len != self.advantages.data.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            @memcpy(self.log_prob_old.data, log_prob_old);
            @memcpy(self.advantages.data, advantages);
        }
    };
}
ppoUpdate
/// Run PPO over a collected rollout: several epochs of shuffled minibatches, each stepped by
/// PLAIN SGD (`stepParameters` -> `sgdStep`) - so `learning_rate` is an SGD rate, not an Adam one.
///
/// ---- WHAT THIS ADDS OVER `ppoEpoch` ----
///
/// `ppoEpoch` scores a rollout against log-probabilities the caller already has. This one
/// RECOMPUTES them: it writes each minibatch into the graph's leaves, re-runs the forward pass
/// so the policy's outputs reflect the parameters as they are NOW, backpropagates and steps.
/// That recomputation is the whole reason PPO has multiple epochs - the ratio is only
/// meaningful because the policy has moved since the data was collected.
///
/// ---- THE ORDER OF THE THREE LOOPS MATTERS ----
///
/// Advantages are normalised ONCE over the whole rollout, before any epoch (see `ppoEpoch`).
/// The shuffle is per EPOCH, so every epoch sees a different partition of the same data. The
/// step is per MINIBATCH. Moving normalisation inside the epoch loop would re-standardise data
/// that has not changed; moving the shuffle outside it would show the optimiser the same
/// partition every time and correlate the updates.
pub fn ppoUpdate(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    model: PpoModel(T),
    rollout: RolloutBuffer,
    advantages: []const f64,
    returns: []const f64,
    observations: Tensor(T),
    actions: Tensor(T),
    config: PpoConfig,
    rng: Rng,
    epochs: usize,
    /// Taken as an argument rather than read from `PpoConfig`, because it is the one setting
    /// that CHANGES during training: PPO anneals it linearly (see `linearLearningRate`). A
    /// config field would be read once and quietly ignored thereafter.
    learning_rate: T,
) Error!PpoStats {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const n: usize = rollout.len;
    if (n < 2 or advantages.len != n or returns.len != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (model.weights.len != model.parameters.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (epochs == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (observations.rank != 2 or observations.shape[0] != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (actions.rank != 2 or actions.shape[0] != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }

    const graph: *Graph(T) = model.graph;
    const obs_leaf: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(model.observations);
    const act_leaf: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(model.actions);
    const adv_leaf: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(model.advantages);
    const old_leaf: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(model.log_prob_old);
    const ret_leaf: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(model.returns);
    const width: usize = adv_leaf.data.len;
    if (width == 0 or width > n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }

    // Normalised once, over the whole rollout, before any epoch begins.
    const scaled: []f64 = try gpa.alloc(f64, n);
    defer gpa.free(scaled);
    @memcpy(scaled, advantages);
    if (config.normalize_advantage) {
        try normalizeAdvantages(f64, scaled, config.advantage_epsilon);
    }

    var walk: MinibatchOrder = try .init(gpa, n, width);
    defer walk.deinit(gpa);

    const obs_dim: usize = observations.shape[1];
    const act_dim: usize = actions.shape[1];
    const log_prob_old: []const f64 = rollout.logProbs();

    var total: PpoStats = .{};
    var counted: usize = 0;
    for (0..epochs) |epoch| {
        walk.reshuffle(rng, @intCast(epoch));
        while (walk.next()) |batch| {
            // A SHORT final stride cannot be written into a fixed-width leaf, and padding it
            // would feed the optimiser duplicated samples. Skipped, and the statistics never
            // counted it either - so the reported numbers describe what was actually stepped on.
            if (batch.len != width) {
                continue;
            }
            for (batch, 0..) |src, dst| {
                @memcpy(
                    obs_leaf.data[dst * obs_dim ..][0..obs_dim],
                    observations.data[src * obs_dim ..][0..obs_dim],
                );
                @memcpy(
                    act_leaf.data[dst * act_dim ..][0..act_dim],
                    actions.data[src * act_dim ..][0..act_dim],
                );
                adv_leaf.data[dst] = @floatCast(scaled[src]);
                old_leaf.data[dst] = @floatCast(log_prob_old[src]);
                ret_leaf.data[dst] = @floatCast(returns[src]);
            }

            // The clip node COPIED its old log-probs and advantages when the tape was built,
            // so they have to be refreshed for this stride - see `PpoClipLoss.setInputs`. Without
            // it the loop optimises against the first minibatch forever and never says so.
            try model.clip_loss.setInputs(old_leaf.data, adv_leaf.data);
            try graph.recompute();
            try graph.backward(model.loss);
            try stepParameters(T, graph, model.weights, model.parameters, learning_rate);

            // Statistics from the log-probabilities this minibatch actually produced.
            const new_value: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(model.log_prob_new);
            var ratio_sum: f64 = 0;
            var clipped: f64 = 0;
            var kl_sum: f64 = 0;
            for (0..width) |k| {
                const lp_new: f64 = @floatCast(new_value.data[k]);
                const lp_old: f64 = @floatCast(old_leaf.data[k]);
                const ratio: f64 = @exp(lp_new - lp_old);
                ratio_sum += ratio;
                if (ratio < 1 - config.clip or ratio > 1 + config.clip) {
                    clipped += 1;
                }
                // The same estimator `ppoObjective` uses, so the two agree by construction.
                kl_sum += lp_old - lp_new;
            }
            const w_f: f64 = @floatFromInt(width);
            total.clip_fraction += clipped;
            total.approx_kl += kl_sum;
            total.policy_loss += @as(f64, @floatCast(graph.valueOf(model.loss).data[0])) * w_f;
            counted += width;
        }
    }
    if (counted == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const inv: f64 = 1.0 / float64(counted);
    return .{
        .policy_loss = total.policy_loss * inv,
        .clip_fraction = total.clip_fraction * inv,
        .approx_kl = total.approx_kl * inv,
        .entropy = 0,
    };
}

The exploration spread

The spread of the action distribution has to reach the actions that matter. A cartpole responds to forces of a few newtons; a policy sampling with σ = 0.05 only ever tries forces near zero, never observes what a real push does, and so receives no gradient toward one. When learning stalls, check that the action distribution covers the part of the action space where the reward changes before changing the learning rate.

3. Environments and episodes#

An environment is where experience comes from. zimrrl's is the cartpole: a step function with no hidden state, two tasks, and the two ways an episode can end, which every algorithm downstream has to keep apart.

3.1 Cartpole#

var s = zrl.cartpoleReset(f64, rng, episode);
const step = zrl.cartpoleStep(f64, s, .right);
// step.state, step.reward, step.failed
cartpoleReset
/// A fresh cartpole, every value drawn uniformly from a small band around upright.
///
/// The band is the standard one. Starting exactly upright would make the first few steps
/// deterministic and let a policy memorise them.
pub fn cartpoleReset(comptime T: type, rng: Rng, episode: u32) CartpoleState(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const band: T = 0.05;
    const stream: Rng = rng.split(episode);
    return .{
        .cart = (stream.unitFloat(T, 0) - 0.5) * 2 * band,
        .cart_rate = (stream.unitFloat(T, 1) - 0.5) * 2 * band,
        // The band is the standard one and is quoted in radians, like the pole's angle.
        .pole_rad = (stream.unitFloat(T, 2) - 0.5) * 2 * band,
        .pole_rate_rad = (stream.unitFloat(T, 3) - 0.5) * 2 * band,
    };
}
cartpoleStep
/// The cart-and-pole control benchmark: push a cart left or right to keep a pole upright.
///
/// A PURE FUNCTION - THE STATE GOES IN AND THE NEXT STATE COMES OUT
///
/// The step is PURE: a state in, the next state out. A step that updated its state in place would
/// make every rollout copy the observation out BEFORE calling it; forgetting would produce a rollout
/// whose observations are all one step late, which trains and converges to something slightly
/// wrong.
///
/// Returning the next state makes it unforgettable. It also makes the step replayable - the same
/// state and action give the same answer, always - which is what a test needs and what a
/// debugger needs.
///
/// RESET IS A SEPARATE FUNCTION, AND THAT REMOVES TWO FLAGS
///
/// Starting an episode is its own call: `cartpoleReset` gives a state, and `cartpoleStep` only ever
/// steps. A reset folded into the step would need a `force_reset` parameter, and then a
/// `no_transition` special case, because a forced reset produces no reward and no termination.
///
/// RADIANS HERE, AND THE SUFFIX SAYS SO - BECAUSE THIS ANGLE IS DIFFERENTIATED
///
/// The rest of this codebase moved to turns, and for geometry that was right: a quarter turn is
/// exactly 0.25, and `sinTurns` is exact where `sin(tau/4)` is not.
///
/// **A differentiated angle is the other case.** `d/dtheta sin(theta)` is `cos(theta)` only in
/// radians; in turns it is `tau * cos`, and every derivative in a dynamics system picks up that
/// factor. The cartpole equations are differential equations, so radians are the unit in which
/// they have no constants in them at all.
///
/// The first version of this held the pole in turns and converted the angular acceleration from
/// radians on the way in. That conversion was a place to be wrong, and I was: **the pole fell tau
/// times too fast** and every sign still looked right. Choosing the unit the equations are
/// written in removes the conversion rather than fixing it.
///
/// So: **turns for angles that are drawn, radians for angles that are differentiated**, and the
/// name carries the unit either way. `pole_rad` cannot be mistaken for `pole_turns`.
///
/// The limit is not a rounded constant: `radFromDeg(12)` is twelve degrees exactly as far as the
/// type can express it, where a literal `0.2095` would be 12.0035 degrees.
///
/// The dynamics are the standard ones - Barto, Sutton and Anderson 1983 - and the integration is
/// SEMI-IMPLICIT Euler: the position update uses the NEW velocity. Gym's default is explicit
/// Euler, which drifts over a long episode.
pub fn cartpoleStep(
    comptime T: type,
    state: CartpoleState(T),
    push: Push,
) CartpoleStep(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const push_force: T = 10.0;
    const force: T = switch (push) {
        .left => -push_force,
        .right => push_force,
    };
    return cartpoleContinuousStep(T, state, force);
}

The cartpole is a cart on a rail with a pole hinged on top; pushing the cart left or right keeps the pole upright. The dynamics are the standard ones (Barto, Sutton and Anderson, 1983). Three choices shape the API.

The step is a pure function. cartpoleStep takes a state and returns the next one, with the reward and whether the pole fell. Nothing is updated in place, so a rollout cannot record an observation that a later step has already overwritten.

Starting an episode is its own call. cartpoleReset draws a starting state; the step never resets, so it needs no special case for a reset that yields no reward and no termination.

The state's four numbers are named — cart, cart_rate, pole_rad, pole_rate_rad — rather than slots of an array, so two of them cannot be exchanged without the call site saying so.

Radians, and the names say so

zimr uses turns for angles that are drawn and radians for angles that are differentiated. The cartpole's equations are differential: the derivative of sin(θ) is cos(θ) only in radians, while in turns every derivative picks up a factor of tau. In radians the equations need no conversion constant, so there is none to get wrong. The field names carry the unit, and the failure angle is radFromDeg(12), twelve degrees as exactly as the type can hold.

The integration is semi-implicit Euler: the position update uses the new velocity, which drifts less over a long episode than explicit Euler.

3.2 Two cartpole tasks#

holdswingup
startnear uprightnear π — hanging
reward1 per surviving stepcos(angle)
ends on angleyes, past 12°no

The last row makes swingup a different problem rather than a harder one. The pole has to rotate through the bottom, so ending the episode on angle would end every episode within a few steps. Swingup needs energy pumping — swinging back and forth to build amplitude — which local correction does not discover, and its reward is cos(angle) because a flat survival bonus says nothing about which way is up.

var state = zrl.cartpoleTaskReset(f64, rng, episode, .swingup);
const outcome = zrl.cartpoleTaskStep(f64, state, force, .swingup);
cartpoleTaskReset
/// A fresh episode for a named task.
///
/// The only difference is WHERE the pole starts, and it is the whole difference: `hold` begins
/// near upright, `swingup` begins near `pi` - hanging - because a swingup that started upright
/// would be a hold with a worse reward function.
pub fn cartpoleTaskReset(
    comptime T: type,
    rng: Rng,
    episode: u32,
    task: CartpoleTask,
) CartpoleState(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    var state: CartpoleState(T) = cartpoleReset(T, rng, episode);
    if (task == .swingup) {
        state.pole_rad += pi;
    }
    return state;
}
cartpoleTaskStep
/// One continuous step under a named task.
///
/// `cartpoleContinuousStep` is this with `.hold`. The dynamics are identical - a task changes what is
/// REWARDED and what ENDS an episode, never how the cart moves.
pub fn cartpoleTaskStep(
    comptime T: type,
    state: CartpoleState(T),
    force: T,
    task: CartpoleTask,
) CartpoleStep(T) {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const advanced = cartpoleContinuousStep(T, state, force);
    const judged = cartpoleOutcome(T, advanced.state, task);
    return .{ .state = advanced.state, .reward = judged.reward, .failed = judged.failed };
}

The dynamics are identical between the two tasks. A task changes what is rewarded and what ends an episode, never how the cart moves.

3.3 How an episode ends: terminal and truncated#

An episode stops for one of two reasons, and they are not the same reason.

// The episode ended: the pole fell. No successor state exists.
const ended: zrl.Transition = .{ .reward = 1.0, .terminal = true };

// The episode was cut at a time limit. The world would have carried on.
const cut: zrl.Transition = .{ .reward = 1.0, .terminal = false, .truncated = true };
Transition
/// One transition, as an off-policy target needs to see it.
pub const Transition = struct {
    reward: f64,
    /// The episode ENDED here: the MDP has no successor, so there is nothing to bootstrap from
    /// and the target is the reward alone.
    terminal: bool,
    /// The episode was CUT here - a time limit, a rollout boundary, an operator stopping the
    /// robot - while the world would have carried on.
    ///
    /// ---- WHY THIS IS A SEPARATE FIELD AND NOT PART OF `terminal` ----
    ///
    /// Both end a trajectory, so one flag looks sufficient and is the obvious design. It is
    /// wrong, and wrong in a way that trains: setting `terminal` on a time limit drops the
    /// bootstrap, which teaches the critic that **the world ends at the horizon**. Every state
    /// near the cut gets a value target that is missing all future reward, the critic learns a
    /// cliff that does not exist, and the policy learns to act as though time is running out.
    /// Nothing crashes and no test fails - the loss curve looks fine, the returns are simply
    /// worse than they should be.
    ///
    /// A truncated transition bootstraps EXACTLY like a mid-episode one. The only thing
    /// truncation changes is that the trajectory stops; the value estimate does not.
    ///
    /// A replay that carries one `done` flag cannot express the distinction, and the horizon
    /// bug that follows trains without failing. The on-policy `RolloutStep` splits `terminal`
    /// from `episode_end` for the same reason; this is the off-policy half of that decision.
    truncated: bool = false,

    /// `terminal` and `truncated` are mutually exclusive: a step either ended the episode or
    /// was cut short of it, never both.
    ///
    /// Checked rather than assumed because the two arrive from different places - `terminal`
    /// from the environment, `truncated` from the rollout loop's step counter - and a caller
    /// that sets both is expressing a contradiction it does not know it has. Failing here is
    /// the only moment anyone will notice; downstream, `terminal` simply wins and the bootstrap
    /// disappears exactly as in the bug this field exists to prevent.
    pub fn validate(self: Transition) Error!void {
        if (self.terminal and self.truncated) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
    }

    /// Whether the target should bootstrap from the next state.
    ///
    /// The whole point of the split, in one line: a TRUNCATED step bootstraps exactly like a
    /// mid-episode one, because the world did not end - only the looking did.
    pub fn bootstraps(self: Transition) bool {
        return !self.terminal;
    }
};

A value function estimates the reward still to come. When an episode genuinely ends there is no future, so the target is the reward alone. When it is cut at a time limit there is a future, which the run simply did not observe, so the target still includes the value of the state where it stopped. A truncated transition bootstraps exactly like one in the middle of an episode.

A single done flag cannot express the difference. Reporting a time limit as terminal removes the bootstrap at every horizon cut: the critic learns that value drops to zero at the time limit — a cliff the environment does not have — and the policy learns to behave as though time were running out. Nothing fails visibly; the returns are lower than they should be.

Off-policy, the rule lives in one place, and both dqnTarget and sacTarget ask it:

pub fn bootstraps(self: Transition) bool {
    return !self.terminal;
}

validate refuses a transition that sets both flags: terminal comes from the environment and truncated from the rollout loop's step counter, so a caller can set both without noticing.

On-policy, RolloutStep makes the same distinction with terminal (there is no future here) and episode_end (do not carry the advantage trace past this step). Every terminal step ends the trace; a truncation ends the trace without being terminal. RolloutBuffer.record takes terminal and truncated and sets both fields.

4. Experience: rollouts, replay and advantages#

Experience is stored before it is learned from. On-policy methods keep one rollout and discard it after the update; off-policy methods keep a ring of past transitions and sample from it. Either way, each action is then scored by how much better it did than expected, and the samples are visited in minibatches.

4.1 Rollouts and replay#

The two families of methods store experience differently. An on-policy method records one rollout with the policy it is about to update, learns from it, and discards it. An off-policy method keeps a ring of past transitions and samples batches from it many times over.

var rollout: zrl.RolloutBuffer = try zrl.RolloutBuffer.init(gpa, horizon);
defer rollout.deinit(gpa);

try rollout.record(reward, value, next_value, terminal, truncated, log_prob);

var adv: [horizon]f64 = undefined;
var ret: [horizon]f64 = undefined;
try rollout.advantages(cfg.gamma, cfg.lambda, &adv, &ret);
RolloutBuffer
/// A fixed-horizon on-policy rollout: what was recorded, and the advantages it implies.
///
/// ---- WHY THIS REFUSES TO WRAP ----
///
/// A replay buffer is a ring: it holds the last N transitions and the oldest fall out. A ROLLOUT
/// buffer is not. GAE is a backward recurrence over a CONTIGUOUS horizon - each step's advantage
/// is built from the one after it - so overwriting step 0 while step 199 is still in flight does
/// not lose one sample, it silently corrupts every advantage that depended on the overwritten
/// trace. `record` returns `Error.DomainError` when full rather than wrapping, because a horizon
/// that quietly became a ring is not detectable from the numbers afterwards.
///
/// ---- WHY `terminal` AND `truncated` ARE BOTH ARGUMENTS ----
///
/// They do different things to the recurrence, and `gae` already knows the difference:
///
///   terminal   kills the BOOTSTRAP. There is no successor state, so the target is the reward.
///   truncated  breaks the TRACE but keeps the bootstrap. The episode did not end; the looking
///              did, so the value of where we stopped still counts.
///
/// A terminal step is always a trace break, so passing `terminal` implies both. Passing
/// `truncated` for a real episode end drops the bootstrap and teaches the critic a cliff that
/// does not exist - see `Transition.truncated` for the same distinction off-policy.
pub const RolloutBuffer = struct {
    steps: []RolloutStep,
    /// The log-probability the acting policy assigned, recorded at collection time. PPO's ratio
    /// is against THIS, not against a recomputed value - the policy has moved by the time the
    /// update runs, which is the entire point of the clip.
    log_prob: []f64,
    len: usize,

    pub fn init(gpa: Allocator, horizon: usize) Error!RolloutBuffer {
        if (horizon == 0) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        const steps: []RolloutStep = try gpa.alloc(RolloutStep, horizon);
        errdefer gpa.free(steps);
        const log_prob: []f64 = try gpa.alloc(f64, horizon);
        return .{ .steps = steps, .log_prob = log_prob, .len = 0 };
    }

    pub fn deinit(self: *RolloutBuffer, gpa: Allocator) void {
        gpa.free(self.steps);
        gpa.free(self.log_prob);
        self.* = undefined;
    }

    pub fn capacity(self: RolloutBuffer) usize {
        return self.steps.len;
    }

    pub fn isFull(self: RolloutBuffer) bool {
        return self.len == self.steps.len;
    }

    /// Start a new rollout. The recorded steps are not cleared - `len` is what bounds every
    /// read, so stale values below it are unreachable.
    pub fn clear(self: *RolloutBuffer) void {
        self.len = 0;
    }

    pub fn record(
        self: *RolloutBuffer,
        reward: f64,
        value: f64,
        next_value: f64,
        terminal: bool,
        truncated: bool,
        log_prob: f64,
    ) Error!void {
        if (self.len == self.steps.len) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        if (terminal and truncated) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        self.steps[self.len] = .{
            .reward = reward,
            .value = value,
            .next_value = next_value,
            .terminal = terminal,
            // A terminal step is ALWAYS a trace break; a truncation is a break without being
            // terminal. This is the only place the two map onto `RolloutStep`'s pair, so the
            // caller never has to remember that `episode_end` means "or".
            .episode_end = terminal or truncated,
        };
        self.log_prob[self.len] = log_prob;
        self.len += 1;
    }

    /// Fill `out_advantages` and `out_returns` from what was recorded.
    ///
    /// The slices must be exactly `len` long: a longer one would leave the tail uninitialised
    /// and a shorter one is a shape error rather than a truncation, because silently computing
    /// advantages for part of a rollout is the kind of thing that trains.
    pub fn advantages(
        self: RolloutBuffer,
        gamma: f64,
        lambda: f64,
        out_advantages: []f64,
        out_returns: []f64,
    ) Error!void {
        if (self.len == 0) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        if (out_advantages.len != self.len or out_returns.len != self.len) {
            return Error.ShapeMismatch;
        }
        return gae(self.steps[0..self.len], gamma, lambda, out_advantages, out_returns);
    }

    /// The recorded log-probabilities, for PPO's ratio.
    pub fn logProbs(self: RolloutBuffer) []const f64 {
        return self.log_prob[0..self.len];
    }
};

A rollout buffer is not a ring. GAE walks backwards through a contiguous stretch of time, building each step's advantage from the one after it, so overwriting the start of a rollout while its end is still being collected would corrupt every advantage that depends on it. record therefore refuses when the buffer is full rather than wrapping, and clear starts the next rollout.

A replay buffer is a ring. ReplayBuffer stores fixed-width transitions and overwrites the oldest once full: a transition gathered ten thousand steps ago says less about the current policy than a recent one, and overwriting the oldest slot is the simplest schedule that forgets. A transition is a flat slice whose layout the caller decides — the buffer does not interpret it, so one type serves discrete and continuous problems. sample is addressable: the same (rng, index) yields the same batch.

A world model learns from windows of consecutive steps of one environment rather than from single transitions; SequenceReplay (9.9) keeps one such ring per environment.

4.2 Advantages: GAE#

try zrl.gae(steps, 0.99, 0.95, &advantages, &returns);
gae
/// Generalized Advantage Estimation over a rollout, plus the returns to fit a critic against.
///
/// WHAT IT IS FOR
///
/// A policy gradient needs to know whether an action did better than expected. The crude answer
/// is the whole discounted return, which is unbiased and extremely noisy; the other extreme is
/// the one-step TD error, which is low-variance and biased by whatever the critic gets wrong.
///
/// GAE interpolates with `lambda`: **0 gives the one-step error, 1 gives the full return**, and
/// values between trade bias for variance. 0.95 is the usual choice and is not magic.
///
/// `gamma` discounts the future as everywhere else in RL.
///
/// THE RECURRENCE RUNS BACKWARD, AND IT HAS TO
///
/// Each advantage depends on the one after it, so a forward loop would need the answer before it
/// had it. Walking back from the end costs one pass and no extra storage.
pub fn gae(
    steps: []const RolloutStep,
    gamma: f64,
    lambda: f64,
    out_advantages: []f64,
    out_returns: []f64,
) Error!void {
    if (out_advantages.len != steps.len or out_returns.len != steps.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (gamma < 0 or gamma > 1 or lambda < 0 or lambda > 1) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var running: f64 = 0;
    var i: usize = steps.len;
    while (i > 0) {
        i -= 1;
        const step: RolloutStep = steps[i];
        // `terminal` kills the BOOTSTRAP: no next state exists, so its value is zero.
        const bootstrap: f64 = if (step.terminal) 0 else gamma * step.next_value;
        const delta: f64 = step.reward + bootstrap - step.value;
        // `episode_end` kills the TRACE: the next step's advantage belongs to another trajectory.
        running = if (step.episode_end) delta else delta + gamma * lambda * running;
        out_advantages[i] = running;
        // The return the critic should have predicted, which is the advantage plus what it did
        // predict. Fitting the critic against this rather than the raw discounted return is what
        // makes the two halves consistent.
        out_returns[i] = running + step.value;
    }
}

A policy gradient needs to know whether an action did better than expected. The whole discounted return answers that without bias but with a great deal of noise; the one-step TD error answers it with little noise but with whatever bias the critic has. GAE interpolates between the two with lambda:

lambda 0     the one-step TD error
lambda 0.5   between
lambda 1     the full discounted return

0.95 is the usual choice; gamma discounts the future as everywhere else.

terminal stops the bootstrap; episode_end stops the trace

Each step carries the two flags of 3.3. terminal means the episode ended: the next state's value is zero by definition, whatever the critic predicts for a state that does not exist. episode_end means the rollout stopped here, terminal or not: the advantage must not carry across into a different trajectory.

reward 1, value 1, next_value 99, gamma 1

terminal = true    ->  advantage 0     the 99 is ignored
terminal = false   ->  advantage 99    the 99 is used

A truncation sets episode_end without terminal: the bootstrap stands, because the next state exists, but the trace stops. Marking it terminal would discard a legitimate estimate and bias the critic low; marking a real termination only with episode_end would bootstrap off a state the agent never reached and bias it high.

The recurrence runs backward

bootstrap    = γ · next_value[i] · (1 − terminal[i])
δ            = reward[i] + bootstrap − value[i]
running      = δ + γλ · (1 − episode_end[i]) · running     // walked backwards
advantage[i] = running
return[i]    = running + value[i]

Each advantage depends on the one after it, so the walk starts at the end: one pass, no extra storage. gae runs the recurrence over RolloutStep values; generalizedAdvantage runs it over separate arrays, with the flags as 0 or 1.

The returns are the advantages plus the critic's own predictions. The critic is fitted against them rather than against the raw discounted return, which keeps the two estimates consistent. With λ = 1 and every value zero, the recurrence reduces to the plain discounted return, which is what discountedReturns computes.

4.3 Minibatches#

var walk: zrl.MinibatchOrder = try zrl.MinibatchOrder.init(gpa, samples, cfg.minibatch);
defer walk.deinit(gpa);

for (0..cfg.epochs) |epoch| {
    walk.reshuffle(rng, @intCast(epoch));
    while (walk.next()) |batch| {
        // `batch` is a slice of sample indices. Everything above this line is the same for
        // every on-policy algorithm; everything below it is what makes one PPO and one SAC.
    }
}
MinibatchOrder
/// The shuffled minibatch walk every on-policy update needs, owned once.
///
/// ---- WHY THIS IS ITS OWN TYPE, BEFORE THERE ARE TWO CALLERS ----
///
/// A PPO update is: normalise the advantages, shuffle the sample order, walk it in minibatch
/// strides for some epochs, and do distribution-specific work inside each stride. The only part
/// that differs between the discrete and continuous versions is that innermost work - the rest
/// is identical bookkeeping.
///
/// Two updates written out in full drift apart: a config field honoured on one path and ignored on
/// the other makes the same struct behave differently depending on which function was called, and
/// nothing fails when it happens. Owning the walk once keeps the discrete and continuous updates
/// from diverging.
///
/// ---- WHY THE SHUFFLE IS PER-EPOCH AND SEEDED FROM THE EPOCH NUMBER ----
///
/// `Rng` here is INDEXED, not a stream: `intBelow(index, bound)` is a pure function of the seed
/// and the index. That is what makes a run reproducible, and it is also a trap - drawing every
/// swap with the same index returns the same number and the "shuffle" is a no-op. So the walk
/// splits the generator per epoch and uses the loop counter as the draw index.
pub const MinibatchOrder = struct {
    /// Sample indices, permuted in place by `reshuffle`.
    order: []usize,
    /// How many indices each `next` yields. The final stride of an epoch may be shorter.
    size: usize,
    cursor: usize,

    pub fn init(gpa: Allocator, count: usize, size: usize) Error!MinibatchOrder {
        if (count == 0 or size == 0) {
            return Error.DomainError;
        }
        const order: []usize = try gpa.alloc(usize, count);
        for (order, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = i;
        }
        return .{ .order = order, .size = size, .cursor = 0 };
    }

    pub fn deinit(self: *MinibatchOrder, gpa: Allocator) void {
        gpa.free(self.order);
        self.* = undefined;
    }

    /// Fisher-Yates, and restart the walk.
    ///
    /// Backwards from the end, drawing `j` in `[0, i]` INCLUSIVE - `intBelow(i + 1)`. Drawing
    /// from `[0, i)` instead is the classic off-by-one that produces a permutation which is not
    /// uniform: some orderings become unreachable, and a sample can never stay where it was.
    pub fn reshuffle(self: *MinibatchOrder, rng: Rng, epoch: u32) void {
        // RESET TO IDENTITY FIRST. Without this, epoch 1 permutes whatever epoch 0 left behind,
        // so the result depends on the HISTORY of calls and `reshuffle(rng, 1)` means two
        // different things depending on what ran before it. A whole run is still deterministic
        // that way, which is what makes the bug comfortable to keep - but the epoch argument
        // stops being a label for a permutation, and resuming a run at epoch 5 gives a
        // different order than reaching epoch 5 by running.
        //
        // A counter-based generator exists so that `(seed, index)` names a value. This makes
        // `(rng, epoch)` name a permutation, which is the same promise one level up.
        for (self.order, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = i;
        }
        const stream: Rng = rng.split(epoch);
        var i: usize = self.order.len;
        while (i > 1) {
            i -= 1;
            const j: usize = stream.intBelow(@intCast(i), @intCast(i + 1));
            const tmp: usize = self.order[i];
            self.order[i] = self.order[j];
            self.order[j] = tmp;
        }
        self.cursor = 0;
    }

    /// The next stride of indices, or null at the end of the epoch.
    ///
    /// The last stride is returned SHORT rather than dropped. A dropped tail silently discards
    /// up to `size - 1` samples every epoch, which for a small rollout is a large fraction of
    /// the data - and the loss curve looks fine, because the samples were never counted.
    pub fn next(self: *MinibatchOrder) ?[]const usize {
        if (self.cursor >= self.order.len) {
            return null;
        }
        const stop: usize = @min(self.cursor + self.size, self.order.len);
        const slice: []const usize = self.order[self.cursor..stop];
        self.cursor = stop;
        return slice;
    }

    /// How many strides one epoch yields, counting a short final one.
    pub fn strides(self: MinibatchOrder) usize {
        return @divCeil(self.order.len, self.size);
    }
};

Every on-policy update has the same skeleton: shuffle the sample order, walk it in minibatch strides for a few epochs, and do something specific to the policy's distribution inside each stride. MinibatchOrder is that skeleton, so the discrete and continuous updates share one copy of it. Three details decide whether the shuffle is a real one.

The last stride is kept. Ten samples in strides of four make three strides: four, four and a short two. Dropping the short one would discard a fifth of the data every epoch without any sign in the loss, so next returns the final stride short.

Each swap uses its own draw index. Rng is indexed rather than a stream: intBelow(index, bound) is a pure function of the seed and the index, which is what makes a run replayable. Drawing every swap with the same index would return the same number each time and permute nothing, so the walk splits the generator per epoch and uses the loop counter as the draw index.

Each epoch starts from the identity. reshuffle resets the order before shuffling. Otherwise epoch 1 would permute whatever epoch 0 left behind, and resuming a run at epoch 5 would give a different order than reaching epoch 5 by running. With the reset, (rng, epoch) names a permutation, as (seed, index) names a value.

5. Policies#

A policy is a distribution over actions. The distributions themselves live in zimrnum; this chapter is about how reinforcement learning uses them: sampling to act, log-probabilities to learn, the tanh squash that keeps a continuous action inside its bounds, and attention for observations that are sets.

5.1 Distributions as types#

const policy = zn.Categorical(f64){ .logits = scores };
const action = try policy.sample(rng, row, stream);
const lp     = try policy.logProb(gpa, row, action);
const bonus  = try policy.entropy(gpa, row);
Categorical
/// A distribution over discrete actions, parameterised by logits.
///
/// WHY THIS IS A TYPE AND NOT THREE FUNCTIONS
///
/// A policy gradient needs `log p(action)` for the action it actually took, and an exploration
/// bonus needs the entropy. Both have to be computed from **the same normalisation `sample`
/// used** - otherwise the gradient points somewhere the sampler never went.
///
/// Bundling them means the three cannot disagree; as separate functions, the caller would be
/// responsible for pairing them correctly.
///
/// LOGITS, NOT PROBABILITIES
///
/// The input is unnormalised scores. A network's last layer produces exactly that, and asking a
/// caller for probabilities means asking them to normalise - which is the step that overflows
/// when a logit is large, and which `logSoftmaxRows` exists to do stably.
pub fn Categorical(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// Unnormalised scores, one row per batch element.
        logits: Tensor(T),

        /// Draw one action for row `row`.
        ///
        /// Delegates to `categorical`, which subtracts the row maximum before exponentiating and
        /// walks a cumulative sum. `index` selects an independent stream so two draws in one
        /// step do not correlate.
        pub fn sample(self: Self, rng: Rng, row: usize, index: u32) Error!usize {
            if (self.logits.rank != 2 or row >= self.logits.shape[0]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            const slice: Tensor(T) = try (try self.logits.slice(0, row, 1)).reshape(
                &.{self.logits.shape[1]},
            );
            return categorical(T, slice, rng, index);
        }

        /// The most likely action, for evaluation rather than collection.
        ///
        /// The discrete twin of `SquashedGaussian.deterministic`, and needed for the same reason:
        /// a return measured while sampling is measuring the exploration too, and is systematically
        /// worse than what the policy would do if deployed.
        ///
        /// Ties go to the LOWEST index. Not because that is better, but because it is decided -
        /// an evaluation that broke ties randomly would be irreproducible for a reason nobody would
        /// think to look for.
        pub fn greedy(self: Self, row: usize) Error!usize {
            const classes: usize = self.logits.shape[1];
            if (self.logits.rank != 2 or row >= self.logits.shape[0]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            var best: usize = 0;
            var best_logit: T = self.logits.at2(row, 0);
            for (1..classes) |k| {
                const v: T = self.logits.at2(row, k);
                if (v > best_logit) {
                    best_logit = v;
                    best = k;
                }
            }
            return best;
        }

        /// `log p(action)` for one row.
        ///
        /// This is `logSoftmax(logits)[action]`, computed by the stable route rather than as
        /// `log(softmax(x))`. The naive form loses the value entirely once a probability falls
        /// below the smallest representable float - and for a confident policy that is the
        /// common case, not the edge case.
        pub fn logProb(self: Self, gpa: Allocator, row: usize, action: usize) Error!T {
            const classes: usize = self.logits.shape[1];
            if (self.logits.rank != 2 or row >= self.logits.shape[0] or action >= classes) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            const normalised: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(gpa, &.{ 1, classes });
            defer gpa.free(normalised.data);
            const one_row: Tensor(T) = try self.logits.slice(0, row, 1);
            try logSoftmaxRows(T, normalised, one_row);
            return normalised.at2(0, action);
        }

        /// The Shannon entropy of one row, in nats.
        ///
        /// High entropy means the policy is still exploring; zero means it always picks the same
        /// action. Added to a loss with a small coefficient, it is what stops a policy collapsing
        /// onto one action before it has tried the others.
        pub fn entropy(self: Self, gpa: Allocator, row: usize) Error!T {
            const classes: usize = self.logits.shape[1];
            if (self.logits.rank != 2 or row >= self.logits.shape[0]) {
                return Error.OutOfRange;
            }
            const normalised: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(gpa, &.{ 1, classes });
            defer gpa.free(normalised.data);
            try logSoftmaxRows(T, normalised, try self.logits.slice(0, row, 1));
            var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            for (0..classes) |k| {
                const log_p: T = normalised.at2(0, k);
                // `p * log p` from the LOG, so a vanishing probability contributes a vanishing
                // term rather than `0 * -inf`, which is NaN.
                total.add(@exp(log_p) * log_p);
            }
            return -total.value();
        }
    };
}

A policy gradient needs log p(action) for the action actually taken, and an exploration bonus needs the entropy. Both must come from the same normalisation sample used, or the gradient points somewhere the sampler never went. Making the distribution a type, with sample, logProb and entropy as its methods, keeps the three consistent.

The input is logits, not probabilities — the unnormalised scores a network's last layer produces. Normalising first loses the value exactly when the policy is confident:

logits 0 and 800, asking for log p of the first

log(softmax(x))   ->  -inf      softmax gave exactly 0
logSoftmax(x)     ->  -800      correct

Entropy is an exploration bonus. It is highest for a uniform policy — over three actions, exactly log 3 — and, added to a loss with a small coefficient, it keeps the policy from collapsing onto one action before the others have been tried. The term p log p is computed from the log, so a vanishing action contributes zero rather than 0 × -inf.

DiagGaussian takes log_std, not std. A network outputs an unconstrained number and a deviation must be positive; exp guarantees it, where a clamp that binds would give a zero gradient and leave the network unable to learn its way out. An equal step in log_std is an equal ratio change in the spread. "Diagonal" means the dimensions are independent, so the joint log-density is the sum of the per-dimension ones.

A Gaussian's entropy does not depend on its mean, only on its spread: 0.5 log(2πe) per dimension, plus log_std.

5.2 Sampling versus training#

Most quantities in this library exist twice: as a number, and as a node on the graph. The number is exact but cannot be trained through.

numbertapeuse the tape one for
DiagGaussian.logProbGraph.diagGaussianLogProbcontinuous PPO
Categorical.logProbGraph.categoricalLogProbdiscrete PPO
SquashedGaussian.sampleGraph.squashedReparameterizeSAC's actor
klGaussianDiagGraph.klGaussianDiagmotion priors, trust regions

The differentiable version has the same name in the Graph namespace. The plain one is for acting and reporting; the graph one goes inside a loss.

The reparameterised sample

SAC's actor loss is alpha * log p(a) - Q(s, a), where a is a sample, and drawing a random number cannot be differentiated. The reparameterisation trick moves the randomness out of the path: eps is drawn once as a constant, and u = mean + std * eps is an ordinary differentiable expression of the parameters.

const draw = try graph.squashedReparameterize(mean, log_std, noise);
// draw.action feeds the critic; draw.log_prob feeds the entropy term.

Squashing through tanh changes the density, so the log-probability includes the correction of 5.3. Without it the entropy term is computed against the wrong density, and the temperature tunes toward a target that does not match the policy's actual entropy.

5.3 The squash correction#

var pre: [dim]f64 = undefined;
var action: [dim]f64 = undefined;
try policy.sample(rng, row, &pre, &action);   // keep BOTH
const lp = try policy.logProb(row, &pre);     // scored from `pre`, not `action`

A continuous action usually has bounds — a motor torque, a steering angle. An unbounded Gaussian sometimes proposes a value outside them, and clipping it afterwards changes its density from the one the policy scored, so the gradient no longer matches what happened. Squashing the sample through tanh puts the bound inside the distribution, and the density changes by the derivative of the transform:

log p(a) = log p(u) - log(1 - tanh(u)^2)      with a = tanh(u)

Written that way, the correction fails in floating point exactly where a trained agent operates, saturated against its bound:

u = 20   ->   tanh(u) == 1.0 exactly
              1 - 1 = 0
              log(0) = -inf

The stable identity keeps every term finite for any u, because softplus never forms the 1:

-log(1 - tanh(u)^2)  =  -2 log2 + 2u + 2 softplus(-2u)

logProb takes the pre-squash value u, not the action. Recovering u from a would need atanh, which is unbounded at the limits and loses its precision exactly where a saturated policy sits. Storing u costs one float per dimension, so sample returns both.

log_std is clamped to [-20, 2], SAC's usual range, inside both sample and logProb, from the same two public constants: a policy that sampled with one spread and scored with another would be off-policy against itself. Without the clamp, a head emitting a log_std of −60 early in training gives a spread that underflows to zero and an infinite log-density.

5.4 Attention over a scene#

When the observation is a set — points sampled from nearby geometry, for example — a fixed-size vector does not fit. Each element is encoded, and the encodings are combined by an operation that ignores their order, so the network cannot learn the order in which a sampler happened to emit them.

// Pool over a set: the max is symmetric, so the result does not depend on ordering.
const pooled = try graph.reduceRows(per_point_features, .max);

// Or let the elements attend to each other.
const out = try graph.encoderBlock(tokens, weights, 1.0 / @sqrt(head_width), 1.0e-5);

Attention and its scale

Attention is three matrix products and a softmax:

const scores = try graph.matmul(query, try graph.transpose(key));
const weights = try graph.softmaxRows(try graph.scale(scores, score_scale));
const out = try graph.matmul(weights, value);

Because softmax rows sum to one, every output is a weighted average of the values, weighted by how well each query matches each key. The score_scale should be 1/√d: Q KT sums d products, so without the scale a wider head drives the softmax toward one-hot and its gradient vanishes. It is a parameter because multi-head attention scales by the head width, not the model width.

encoderBlock is pre-norm, x + f(norm(x)): the path from input to output passes through no normalisation, so the gradient reaches the first layer undiminished and deep stacks train without a learning-rate warmup. Its six projections travel in a named EncoderWeights, so wq and wk cannot be exchanged by position.

6. On-policy training: PPO and AWR#

On-policy methods learn from the rollout the current policy has just collected. PPO limits how far one update can move the policy; AWR regresses the policy onto its own better-than-expected actions.

6.1 PPO: what the clip is for#

The policy-gradient idea is simple: increase the probability of actions that did better than expected. A single large update, however, can move the policy somewhere the collected data says nothing about — and that data is all there is, because it came from the old policy.

PPO's answer is a trust region. It measures how much the policy changed as a ratio, and declines to reward changes beyond a band:

pub fn ppoClipSample(
    comptime T: type,
    log_prob_new: T,
    log_prob_old: T,
    advantage: T,
    clip: T,
) T {
    const ratio: T = @exp(log_prob_new - log_prob_old);
    const unclipped: T = ratio * advantage;
    const clipped: T = clamp(ratio, 1 - clip, 1 + clip) * advantage;
    return @min(unclipped, clipped);
}

The @min is the whole algorithm. With a positive advantage the objective stops improving once the ratio passes 1 + clip; with a negative one, once it drops below 1 − clip. Overshooting in the improving direction earns nothing, while a move in the wrong direction is never clipped away: a ratio of 5 with a negative advantage costs its full price, so the gradient that corrects a bad step survives. The function returns an objective to maximise; a gradient step descends its negation, which 6.2 builds on the graph.

Three orderings worth getting right

Normalise advantages once, over the whole rollout, not per minibatch: a minibatch of 32 estimates its own mean and spread, so the scale would shift between strides.

Shuffle per epoch, so that each pass sees a different partition (4.3).

Weight statistics by minibatch size. Ten samples in strides of four make strides of 4, 4 and 2; averaging the three stride-means equally would give the last two samples a third of the reported approx_kl instead of a fifth, and approx_kl is what an early-stopping rule reads.

6.2 The PPO objective and its two health numbers#

const stats = try zrl.ppoObjective(
    advantages,        // modified in place if normalising
    log_prob_old,
    log_prob_new,
    entropies,
    .{},               // PpoConfig defaults
);
ppoObjective
/// The clipped surrogate objective over a batch, with the diagnostics that go with it.
///
/// Returns the loss to MINIMISE: the negated objective, so it drops into a gradient step like
/// any other loss. `advantages` is modified in place when the config asks for normalisation.
///
/// THE KL ESTIMATOR IS `exp(r) - 1 - r`, NOT `old - new`
///
/// The cheap estimator every reference implementation started with is `mean(log_old - log_new)`,
/// which is unbiased and **can come out negative** - and a negative divergence is not a small
/// number, it is a wrong one. A caller thresholding on it either never triggers or triggers on
/// noise.
///
/// The k3 estimator `exp(r) - 1 - r` with `r = log_new - log_old` is **non-negative for every
/// r**, because `exp(r) >= 1 + r` everywhere.
pub fn ppoObjective(
    advantages: []f64,
    log_prob_old: []const f64,
    log_prob_new: []const f64,
    entropies: []const f64,
    config: PpoConfig,
) Error!PpoStats {
    const n: usize = advantages.len;
    if (log_prob_old.len != n or log_prob_new.len != n or entropies.len != n) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (n == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    if (config.clip <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }

    if (config.normalize_advantage) {
        var total: CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
        for (advantages) |a| {
            total.add(a);
        }
        const mean: f64 = total.value() / float64(n);
        var spread: CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
        for (advantages) |a| {
            spread.add((a - mean) * (a - mean));
        }
        // POPULATION deviation, not sample: this is the whole batch, not a sample from it, and
        // the distinction is visible for a small minibatch.
        const deviation: f64 = @sqrt(spread.value() / float64(n));
        for (advantages) |*a| {
            a.* = (a.* - mean) / (deviation + config.advantage_epsilon);
        }
    }

    var objective: CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
    var kl: CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
    var entropy_total: CompensatedSum(f64) = .{};
    var clipped: usize = 0;
    for (0..n) |i| {
        const r: f64 = log_prob_new[i] - log_prob_old[i];
        const ratio: f64 = @exp(r);
        const unclipped: f64 = ratio * advantages[i];
        const bounded: f64 = clamp(ratio, 1 - config.clip, 1 + config.clip) * advantages[i];
        // THE MINIMUM, which is what makes it pessimistic: the objective takes the worse of the
        // two branches, so a ratio that moved favourably stops paying once it leaves the trust
        // region while an unfavourable one keeps counting.
        objective.add(@min(unclipped, bounded));
        if (ratio < 1 - config.clip or ratio > 1 + config.clip) {
            clipped += 1;
        }
        kl.add(@exp(r) - 1 - r);
        entropy_total.add(entropies[i]);
    }
    const count: f64 = @floatFromInt(n);
    return .{
        // NEGATED: the objective is maximised, a loss is minimised.
        .policy_loss = -objective.value() / count,
        .clip_fraction = float64(clipped) / count,
        .approx_kl = kl.value() / count,
        .entropy = entropy_total.value() / count,
    };
}

Two numbers, because the loss cannot tell

A broken PPO run and a healthy one look the same in the loss, because the clipped objective is meant to stop improving once the policy has moved far enough. ppoObjective reports two numbers that tell them apart:

clip_fractionWhat it means
near 0the policy barely moved — the epochs after the first did nothing
near 1slamming the clip on every sample — the step is too large and the clip is all that holds it together

A sharp rise in approx_kl is the usual trigger for stopping an update's epochs early.

A KL estimate that cannot go negative

The simple estimate mean(log_old - log_new) is unbiased but can come out negative, and a threshold on a negative divergence either never fires or fires on noise. The k3 estimator is non-negative for every sample, because exp(r) ≥ 1 + r everywhere:

r = log_new - log_old

cheap:  mean(-r)          can be negative
k3:     exp(r) - 1 - r    non-negative for every r

Advantage normalisation

Raw advantages carry the scale of the reward: an environment paying 1 per step and one paying 1000 would need learning rates a thousand times apart. Subtracting the mean and dividing by the deviation, on by default, makes the step size a property of the algorithm rather than of the reward's units. The deviation is the population one, since the batch is the whole set rather than a sample of it.

The objective as a loss on the graph

ppoClipObjective computes PPO's objective as a plain function, which the graph cannot differentiate. zrl.PpoClipLoss builds the same objective, negated, as a loss on the graph from generic operations: the ratio r = exp(log pnew − log pold), the two branches r A and clamp(r, 1 − ε, 1 + ε) A, their minimum, and the mean. The old log-probabilities and the advantages are constants the loss owns; a minibatch loop writes the next stride's values with setInputs before recomputing.

const clip_loss: zrl.PpoClipLoss(f64) = try .build(&graph, logp_new, logp_old, advantages, 0.2);
try graph.backward(clip_loss.loss);
// The next minibatch: new old log-probabilities and advantages, then the same graph again.
try clip_loss.setInputs(next_logp_old, next_advantages);
try graph.recompute();
PpoClipLoss
/// PPO'S CLIPPED SURROGATE AS A LOSS ON A GRAPH, composed from generic operations.
///
/// For each sample, with r = exp(log p_new - log p_old) the probability ratio between the policy being trained and
/// the policy that collected the rollout, and A the sample's advantage:
///
///     objective = min( r A,  clamp(r, 1 - clip, 1 + clip) A )          loss = - mean over samples of objective
///
/// The loss is the negated mean, so it is descended like every other loss in this library. Its value per sample is
/// `ppoClipSample`, and its mean `ppoClipObjective`, which the GPU kernel shares.
///
/// ---- THE GRAPH ----
///
///     log p_new - log p_old --exp--> r --x A--------------------------------> r A ----+
///                                    |                                                min --> mean --> x -1
///                                    +--> min(r, 1 + clip) --> max(., 1 - clip) x A --+
///
/// The clamp is two `min` operations, using max(a, b) = -min(-a, -b). No operation here knows about PPO, so the graph
/// itself stays free of any reinforcement-learning algorithm.
///
/// ---- THE GRADIENT ----
///
/// `min` sends the whole gradient to the smaller input, and to the left one on a tie. Per sample, the gradient of
/// the objective with respect to log p_new is therefore:
///
///     r inside [1 - clip, 1 + clip]:    both branches are equal; the tie goes left        ->  A r
///     r > 1 + clip and A > 0:           the clamped branch is smaller and constant        ->  0
///     r < 1 - clip and A < 0:           the clamped branch is smaller and constant        ->  0
///     r > 1 + clip and A < 0, or
///     r < 1 - clip and A > 0:           the unclipped branch is smaller                   ->  A r
///
/// which is PPO's rule: a sample stops pushing once the ratio has moved past the clip in the direction its advantage
/// favours. Worked through with clip = 0.2: r = 1.5 and A = 2 give objective min(3, 2.4) = 2.4 and no gradient;
/// r = 1.5 and A = -2 give min(-3, -2.4) = -3 and a gradient of A r = -3, which keeps lowering the ratio.
///
/// ---- THE INPUTS CHANGE EVERY MINIBATCH ----
///
/// The old log-probabilities and the advantages are constants the loss owns, shaped like `log_prob_new`. A minibatch
/// loop writes the next stride's values with `setInputs` before `recompute`; without that, every stride would be
/// optimised against the first one's numbers.
pub fn PpoClipLoss(comptime T: type) type {
    requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The loss, [1, 1]: minus the mean clipped surrogate.
        loss: Var,
        /// The log-probabilities under the rollout's policy, shaped like `log_prob_new`.
        log_prob_old: Tensor(T),
        /// The advantages, shaped like `log_prob_new`.
        advantages: Tensor(T),

        /// The loss of `log_prob_new` ([N, 1] or [1, N], one value per sample) on `graph`. `log_prob_old` and
        /// `advantages` hold one value per sample and are copied into tensors allocated with the graph's allocator.
        pub fn build(
            graph: *Graph(T),
            log_prob_new: Var,
            log_prob_old: []const T,
            advantages: []const T,
            clip: T,
        ) Error!Self {
            const fresh: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(log_prob_new);
            const sample_count: usize = fresh.size();
            if (sample_count != log_prob_old.len or log_prob_old.len != advantages.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            if (clip <= 0 or sample_count == 0) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            const is_column: bool = fresh.rank == 2 and fresh.shape[1] == 1;
            const is_row: bool = fresh.rank == 2 and fresh.shape[0] == 1;
            if (!is_column and !is_row) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const shape: []const usize = fresh.shape[0..fresh.rank];
            const old_values: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(graph.gpa, shape);
            @memcpy(old_values.data, log_prob_old);
            const advantage_values: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(graph.gpa, shape);
            @memcpy(advantage_values.data, advantages);
            const upper_bound: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(graph.gpa, shape);
            upper_bound.fill(1 + clip);
            const negated_lower_bound: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(graph.gpa, shape);
            negated_lower_bound.fill(-(1 - clip));
            const advantage: Var = try graph.constant(advantage_values);
            // r = exp(log p_new - log p_old), and the unclipped branch r A.
            const ratio: Var = try graph.exp(try graph.sub(log_prob_new, try graph.constant(old_values)));
            const unclipped: Var = try graph.mul(ratio, advantage);
            // clamp(r, 1 - clip, 1 + clip) = max(min(r, 1 + clip), 1 - clip), with max(a, b) = -min(-a, -b).
            const below_upper: Var = try graph.min(ratio, try graph.constant(upper_bound));
            const negated_clamped: Var = try graph.min(
                try graph.scale(below_upper, -1),
                try graph.constant(negated_lower_bound),
            );
            const clipped: Var = try graph.mul(try graph.scale(negated_clamped, -1), advantage);
            // The smaller branch per sample (ties to the left: the unclipped one), then minus the mean.
            const surrogate: Var = try graph.min(unclipped, clipped);
            const surrogate_row: Var = if (is_column) try graph.transpose(surrogate) else surrogate;
            const mean_surrogate: Var = try graph.reduceRows(surrogate_row, .mean);
            return .{
                .loss = try graph.scale(mean_surrogate, -1),
                .log_prob_old = old_values,
                .advantages = advantage_values,
            };
        }

        /// Write the next minibatch's old log-probabilities and advantages, one value per sample; `recompute` then
        /// evaluates the loss on them.
        pub fn setInputs(
            self: Self,
            log_prob_old: []const T,
            advantages: []const T,
        ) Error!void {
            if (log_prob_old.len != self.log_prob_old.data.len or advantages.len != self.advantages.data.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            @memcpy(self.log_prob_old.data, log_prob_old);
            @memcpy(self.advantages.data, advantages);
        }
    };
}

In the backward pass the clip decides the update: min sends the gradient to the smaller branch, so where the clamped branch is smaller the gradient is zero, and a sample stops pushing once its ratio has passed the clip in the direction its advantage favours. With ε = 0.2, a ratio of 1.5 and an advantage of 2 give min(3, 2.4) = 2.4 and no gradient; the same ratio with an advantage of −2 gives min(−3, −2.4) = −3, and the gradient keeps lowering the ratio.

6.3 AWR: imitate your own good actions#

AWR is the simplest complete algorithm in this library. Instead of a policy gradient, it performs supervised regression onto the actions already taken, weighted by how good each one was:

try zrl.advantageWeights(f64, &weights, &advantages, beta, 20.0);
const loss = -try zn.maskedMean(f64, &weighted_logp, &was_sampled);
maskedMean
/// The mean of `values` over the entries `keep` marks true.
///
/// ---- WHY AWR NEEDS THIS AND PPO DOES NOT ----
///
/// AWR trains its actor only on steps where the action was SAMPLED, not where it was taken
/// deterministically - a deterministic action carries no information about what the policy would
/// have explored, so regressing onto it teaches the policy to repeat a choice it did not make
/// probabilistically.
///
/// ---- AN EMPTY MASK IS AN ERROR, NOT A ZERO ----
///
/// Returning 0 for "nothing selected" is the tempting default and it is wrong in the direction
/// that hides: a zero loss looks like a converged one. If a whole minibatch happened to contain
/// no sampled actions, the caller needs to know that rather than to descend nothing.
pub fn maskedMean(comptime T: type, values: []const T, keep: []const bool) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (values.len != keep.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    var counted: usize = 0;
    for (values, keep) |v, wanted| {
        if (wanted) {
            total.add(v);
            counted += 1;
        }
    }
    if (counted == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    return total.value() / @as(T, @floatFromInt(counted));
}
advantageWeights
/// AWR's per-sample weights: `exp(advantage / beta)`, capped.
///
/// ---- WHAT AWR IS, IN ONE LINE ----
///
/// Imitate your own good actions, weighted by how good they were. The actor loss is
/// `-mean(weight * log_prob)` - a supervised regression onto the actions already taken, with the
/// advantage deciding how much each one counts. No ratio, no clipping of a policy change, and no
/// requirement that the data be on-policy, which is what makes it an off-policy method.
///
/// ---- THE CAP IS LOAD-BEARING, NOT DEFENSIVE ----
///
/// `exp` of a large advantage is enormous. One sample with an advantage five betas above the
/// rest carries `e^5` times the weight of an average one, so the batch's entire gradient becomes
/// "do that". The update is then stable, confident, and learning a single action.
///
/// **It reports a small loss while doing it**, because a weighted mean dominated by one term is
/// still a small number. Nothing in the curves says the batch collapsed to a sample.
///
/// `beta` is the temperature: large makes the weights uniform and the update pure imitation,
/// small makes it greedy and fragile. The cap is usually 20, which at the default beta is about
/// three standard deviations of advantage - past that the sample is an outlier, not a lesson.
pub fn advantageWeights(
    comptime T: type,
    out: []T,
    advantages: []const T,
    beta: T,
    max_weight: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (out.len != advantages.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (beta <= 0 or max_weight <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    // The exponent at which the cap binds. Compared against BEFORE the `exp`, not after: a
    // large advantage gives `exp(800)`, which is infinity in f64 - and an infinity that meets a
    // zero advantage elsewhere in the batch is a NaN no later clamp ever sees.
    const capping_exponent: T = @log(max_weight);
    for (out, advantages) |*w, advantage| {
        const exponent: T = advantage / beta;
        // ---- BOUNDED BOTH WAYS, WHICH THE REFERENCE IMPLEMENTATIONS ARE NOT ----
        //
        // Capping the top is standard. The bottom matters for the same reason and is usually
        // left alone: `exp(-800)` underflows to EXACTLY ZERO, so a sample far below average
        // contributes nothing at all - and if a whole minibatch sits below average, which
        // happens before the advantages are normalised or when a policy is briefly bad, every
        // weight is zero, the loss is zero, and the update does nothing while reporting
        // convergence.
        //
        // The floor is `1/max_weight`, symmetric with the cap in log space, so the ratio between
        // the best and worst weighted sample is bounded by `max_weight^2`. A sample can be made
        // negligible; it cannot be made absent.
        //
        // Both bounds are compared BEFORE the `exp` rather than clamping its result: `exp(800)`
        // is infinity, and an infinity that meets a zero elsewhere in the batch is a NaN no
        // later clamp ever sees. The exact endpoints also matter - `exp(log(max_weight))` differs
        // from `max_weight` in the last bit, so a caller testing `weight == max_weight` to
        // detect saturation would never see it, and detecting saturation is how you learn the
        // cap is set wrong.
        if (exponent >= capping_exponent) {
            w.* = max_weight;
        } else if (exponent <= -capping_exponent) {
            w.* = 1 / max_weight;
        } else {
            w.* = @exp(exponent);
        }
    }
}

There is no ratio and no trust region, and the data need not be on-policy, so AWR also works from a replay buffer. beta is a temperature: a large value makes the weights uniform and the update pure imitation; a small one makes it greedy.

The weights are bounded at both ends. Without a cap, one sample five betas above the rest would carry e5 times the weight of an average one and dominate the batch. Without a floor, exp of a large negative advantage underflows to exactly zero, and a minibatch that sits wholly below average would produce no update. The floor is 1/max_weight, symmetric with the cap in log space: a sample can become negligible but never absent.

maskedMean restricts the loss to steps whose action was sampled, since a deterministic action says nothing about what the policy would have explored.

7. Off-policy training: DQN, SAC and TD3#

Off-policy methods keep their data and learn from it many times. That is more sample-efficient, and it brings one problem: a value function trained on its own estimates drifts upward. The methods in this chapter differ mainly in how they counter that drift.

7.1 The off-policy loop#

On-policy methods discard their data after each update; off-policy methods keep it, which is far more sample-efficient and brings a new problem. The value function is trained on its own predictions, and the maximum over noisy estimates is biased upward: the bias feeds back through the target, the critic drifts optimistic, and the policy chases actions it has overrated. The common remedy is a minimum over several estimates:

// TD3: the smaller of two independently-initialised critics.
const pessimistic = try graph.min(q1, q2);

// DroQ: the smallest over M dropout masks of one critic.
var lowest = estimates[0];
for (estimates[1..]) |next| lowest = try graph.min(lowest, next);

Apart from the target, the loop is the same for every off-policy method: sample a batch, recompute, descend the critic loss, let the target network follow. offPolicyUpdate is that loop, and the caller passes the targets.

const loss = try zrl.offPolicyUpdate(f64, model, &replay, targets,
                                    rng, step, learning_rate, follow);
offPolicyUpdate
/// One off-policy training pass: sample, descend, let the target network follow.
///
/// ---- WHY THE POLYAK STEP IS AFTER THE GRADIENT STEP AND NOT BEFORE ----
///
/// The target network exists to make the regression target stand still while the online network
/// chases it. Updating it BEFORE the gradient step would move the goal the batch was aimed at
/// partway through the aim - the targets were computed from the old target network, so the
/// follow has to come after the step that used them.
///
/// The effect is subtle and never fails: training still converges, just more slowly and less
/// stably, and the cause looks like a bad learning rate.
///
/// ---- `follow` IS THE SMALL NUMBER ----
///
/// `polyakUpdate(target, online, follow)` moves the target a FRACTION `follow` of the way toward
/// the online weights, so the usual value is 0.005 and not 0.995. Passing the complement gives a
/// target that tracks almost instantly - which is the same as having no target network at all,
/// and reintroduces exactly the instability one is for.
pub fn offPolicyUpdate(
    comptime T: type,
    model: OffPolicyModel(T),
    replay: *const ReplayBuffer(T),
    targets: []const T,
    rng: Rng,
    step_index: u32,
    learning_rate: T,
    follow: T,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (model.weights.len != model.parameters.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (model.target_weights.len != model.online_weights.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (follow < 0 or follow > 1) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const graph: *Graph(T) = model.graph;
    const batch_leaf: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(model.batch);
    const target_leaf: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(model.targets);
    if (targets.len != target_leaf.data.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }

    // Sampled STRAIGHT INTO the leaf. An earlier version took a scratch buffer, filled it, and
    // memcpy'd it here - which made every caller allocate a batch-sized array whose only job was
    // to be copied out of. `ReplayBuffer.sample` writes into whatever slice it is given, so the
    // leaf is the slice.
    try replay.sample(rng, step_index, batch_leaf.data);
    @memcpy(target_leaf.data, targets);

    try graph.recompute();
    try graph.backward(model.loss);
    try stepParameters(T, graph, model.weights, model.parameters, learning_rate);

    // AFTER the step, for the reason above.
    for (model.target_weights, model.online_weights) |target, online| {
        try polyakUpdate(T, target, online, follow);
    }

    return graph.valueOf(model.loss).data[0];
}

follow is the small number — typically 0.005, not 0.995 — because polyakUpdate moves the target network that fraction of the way toward the online weights; the complement would make it track instantly, as if there were no target network. The follow happens after the gradient step, because the batch's targets were computed from the target network as it was.

7.2 The DQN target#

const y = try zrl.dqnTarget(f64, transition, q_next_target, q_next_online, 0.99, .double);
dqnTarget
/// The regression target for one transition: `r + gamma * (1 - terminal) * Q(s', a')`.
///
/// WHY `double` EXISTS, AND WHAT IT FIXES
///
/// Vanilla DQN takes `max_a' Q_target(s', a')`. The maximum of noisy estimates is **biased
/// upward** - if every Q is right on average but wrong individually, the largest one is likely
/// to be one that happened to be over-estimated. Bootstrapping off it feeds that error back in,
/// and the values climb away from anything the environment pays.
///
/// Double DQN splits the two jobs: the ONLINE network picks which action looks best, the TARGET
/// network says what it is worth. The two networks have independent errors, so the selection
/// no longer systematically picks the target's over-estimates.
///
/// It is the same fix in one line, and it is why `double` is the sensible default for new code
/// even though `vanilla` is what the original paper did.
///
/// `q_next_online` is ignored for `vanilla` - pass it anyway so a caller switching between the
/// two changes one argument rather than restructuring.
pub fn dqnTarget(
    comptime T: type,
    transition: Transition,
    q_next_target: []const T,
    q_next_online: []const T,
    gamma: T,
    kind: DqnKind,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (q_next_target.len == 0 or q_next_online.len != q_next_target.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (gamma < 0 or gamma > 1) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    // A TERMINAL TRANSITION HAS NO FUTURE, so the target is the reward alone. Not
    // `reward + gamma * something_small` - there is no next state, and its value is zero by
    // definition rather than by estimate.
    try transition.validate();
    if (!transition.bootstraps()) {
        return transition.reward;
    }
    const bootstrap: T = switch (kind) {
        .vanilla => blk: {
            var best: T = q_next_target[0];
            for (q_next_target[1..]) |q| {
                best = @max(best, q);
            }
            break :blk best;
        },
        .double => blk: {
            // The ONLINE net chooses...
            var chosen: usize = 0;
            var best: T = q_next_online[0];
            for (q_next_online[1..], 1..) |q, i| {
                if (q > best) {
                    best = q;
                    chosen = i;
                }
            }
            // ...and the TARGET net values that choice.
            break :blk q_next_target[chosen];
        },
    };
    return transition.reward + gamma * bootstrap;
}

vanilla:  y = r + gamma*(1-terminal) * max_a' Q_target(s', a')
double:   a* = argmax_a' Q_online(s', a')
          y = r + gamma*(1-terminal) * Q_target(s', a*)

Vanilla DQN bootstraps from the maximum of the target network's Q values, and that maximum is biased upward: if every estimate is right on average but wrong individually, the largest is likely to be one that happened to be overestimated. Double DQN splits the two jobs: the online network chooses the action and the target network values it. Their errors are independent, so the choice no longer favours the target's overestimates.

The bias is large even for a modest action set. If the true value of each of eight actions is zero and each network's estimates carry independent unit noise, the vanilla target averages about 1.4 — the expected maximum of eight standard normals — while the double target averages zero. When the two networks agree, the estimators give the same answer, so the choice matters only while the estimates are noisy.

explainedVariance

A falling value loss says the critic is fitting something, not that it is fitting the returns. A critic that predicts the mean return for every state has a small, shrinking loss and an explained variance of exactly 0. A negative value means worse than that constant baseline, which is common early in training and invisible in the loss.

7.3 SAC and its variants#

const y = try zrl.sacTarget(f64, transition, next_estimates, next_log_prob,
                           gamma, alpha, .minimum);
sacTarget
/// The SAC regression target for one transition.
///
/// WHAT MAKES IT SAC RATHER THAN TD3
///
/// The entropy term. TD3's target is `r + gamma * min_i Q_i(s', a')`; SAC subtracts
/// `alpha * log p(a'|s')` from the bootstrap, so an action the policy was CONFIDENT about is
/// worth less than an equally-good action it was uncertain about.
///
/// That single term is what makes the objective "maximise return **and** keep exploring", and it
/// is why SAC does not need an exploration schedule bolted on: the incentive is in the value
/// function.
///
/// `next_estimates` is a slice for the reason `CriticAggregate` documents - two networks, M
/// dropout passes, or a random subset of an ensemble all arrive the same way.
pub fn sacTarget(
    comptime T: type,
    transition: Transition,
