    next_estimates: []const T,
    next_log_prob: T,
    gamma: T,
    alpha: T,
    how: CriticAggregate,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (gamma < 0 or gamma > 1 or alpha < 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    try transition.validate();
    if (!transition.bootstraps()) {
        // No next state, so neither its value NOR its entropy enters. Keeping the entropy term
        // here would pay a bonus for the uncertainty of an action that was never taken.
        return transition.reward;
    }
    const q: T = try aggregateCritics(T, next_estimates, how);
    return transition.reward + gamma * (q - alpha * next_log_prob);
}

Four algorithms, one call

The off-policy methods in common use differ mainly in where their several estimates come from:

MethodEstimates come fromCombined by
TD3, SACtwo separate networksminimum
DroQM dropout passes of one netminimum
REDQa random M of an ensemble of Nminimum
CrossQone LayerNorm net, no target networksingle value

next_estimates is a slice rather than a pair, so all four are the same call; a single estimate passes through unchanged, which makes CrossQ a one-element list rather than a special case.

The minimum is biased downward on purpose. A minimum of unbiased estimates underestimates — the minimum of two standard normals averages −0.56 — and that asymmetry is intended: an overestimate is bootstrapped into the next target and compounds, while an underestimate only slows learning. .mean is for critics regularised elsewhere, such as a dropout or LayerNorm critic, which is already conservative. REDQ redraws its subset of M critics every step (M = 2 of N = 10 in the paper), so that no fixed pair's shared error dominates every target.

The entropy term

TD3:  y = r + gamma * min_i Q_i(s', a')
SAC:  y = r + gamma * (min_i Q_i(s', a') - alpha * log p(a'|s'))

An action the policy was confident about is worth less than an equally good action it was uncertain about, so the incentive to explore lives in the value function and needs no separate schedule. With alpha = 0 the target is exactly TD3's. A terminal transition drops the entropy term along with the value: there is no next action whose uncertainty could be rewarded.

The temperature and its negative target

alpha is learned rather than set, because a fixed value trades return against exploration at a rate that depends on the reward scale, the action dimension and the stage of training. Its gradient is alpha * (entropy - target_entropy), with the usual target -action_dim. With target_entropy = -2:

log_prob -5   entropy  5   above target  ->  gradient +, alpha falls
log_prob -1   entropy  1   still above   ->  gradient +, alpha falls less steeply
log_prob +3   entropy -3   below target  ->  gradient -, alpha rises
log_prob +2   entropy -2   AT the target ->  gradient 0

The fixed point is log_prob = +2. A continuous density can exceed 1, so a positive log-probability is an ordinary state for a concentrated policy. The parameter is log_alpha, for the same reason DiagGaussian takes log_std.

7.4 A deterministic actor: TD3#

TD3's actor computes one action per state, squashed by a tanh into [−1, 1], where SAC's samples one. Four small helpers each address one way a deterministic actor goes wrong.

Exploration on a schedule. A deterministic policy would repeat the same action from a state, so acting adds Gaussian noise around it, starting at a standard deviation of 0.4 and settling to 0.1 over the first 40% of training, as TD4 does; linearSchedule gives the value at any step from the step alone.

Target smoothing. The target action gets noise too (0.2, clipped to ±0.5), so the target averages the critic over a neighbourhood of actions rather than trusting one sharp peak that the actor would learn to aim at. Both uses are the same arithmetic — noise clipped, then the action clamped to [−1, 1] — so addGaussianActionNoise serves both.

Delayed policy updates. The actor climbs the critic's estimate, and a critic that has just moved is a noisy target, so the critic learns at every update and the actor, with both target networks, at every second one; policyUpdateDue says when.

Saturation. Once the tanh's input is large its slope is nearly zero, and the action sticks at its bound. preActivationPenalty charges (|u| − 1)² for every pre-activation u outside [−1, 1] and nothing inside, pulling the actor back to where the tanh responds.

// Acting: the policy's actions plus exploration noise annealing from 0.4 to 0.1 (no clip on it).
const exploration_std: f32 = try zn.linearSchedule(f32, 0.4, 0.1, 0.4, step, total_steps);
try zrl.addGaussianActionNoise(f32, rng, @intCast(step), policy_actions, exploration_std, std.math.inf(f32), actions);

// The critic's target: the target actor's actions, smoothed.
try zrl.addGaussianActionNoise(f32, rng, update_index, target_actions, 0.2, 0.5, smoothed_target_actions);

// Learning: the critic at every update, the actor at every second one.
if (try zrl.policyUpdateDue(critic_updates_done, 2)) {
    const penalty: zn.Var = try graph.preActivationPenalty(pre_activation);
    // actor loss = -mean(Q1) + coefficient * penalty
}
linearSchedule
/// A VALUE THAT MOVES IN A STRAIGHT LINE, THEN STAYS - an annealing schedule.
///
/// From `start_value` at step 0 to `end_value` at step `decay_fraction * total_steps`, linearly, and `end_value`
/// from then on:
///
///         value
///   start   |*
///           |   *.
///           |      *.
///           |         *.
///     end   |            *---------------------------------
///           +------------|---------------------------------|---> step
///           0     decay_fraction * total_steps        total_steps
///
/// TD4 anneals its exploration noise this way: a standard deviation of 0.4 at the start, falling to 0.1 over the
/// first 40% of training - explore boldly while the critic knows little, then settle. The value comes from the step
/// alone - no state to carry, nothing to forget to update - so any step can be asked about at any time. Both ends
/// are returned exactly (no rounding drift onto the end value).
pub fn linearSchedule(
    comptime T: type,
    start_value: T,
    end_value: T,
    decay_fraction: T,
    step: u64,
    total_steps: u64,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (total_steps == 0 or !(decay_fraction >= 0 and decay_fraction <= 1)) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const total_steps_as_float: T = @floatFromInt(total_steps);
    const decay_steps: T = decay_fraction * total_steps_as_float;
    const step_as_float: T = @floatFromInt(step);
    // How far along the slope, from 0 at its start to 1 at its end. A slope of no length is over at step 0.
    const progress_along_slope: T = if (decay_steps == 0) 1 else step_as_float / decay_steps;
    if (progress_along_slope >= 1) {
        return end_value;
    }
    return start_value + (end_value - start_value) * progress_along_slope;
}
Var
/// A handle to a value inside a `Graph`. An index, not a pointer.
///
/// WHY VALUES ARE INDICES AND NOT TENSORS
///
/// The obvious design keys gradients off the tensor itself - its `data.ptr`, say. That breaks
/// immediately here, because a view SHARES `data.ptr` with its parent: `w` and
/// `w.transpose(0,1)` would be the same key while being different values in the graph. Worse, a
/// tensor is copied by value throughout this library, so there is no stable address to key on at
/// all.
///
/// An index into the graph's own arrays sidesteps both. The graph owns the correspondence between
/// a value, its tensor, and its gradient, and nothing about `Tensor` has to change to support it.
pub const Var = u32;
addGaussianActionNoise
/// ACTIONS PLUS CLIPPED GAUSSIAN NOISE, KEPT IN [-1, 1] - one function for both of TD3's kinds of action noise:
///
///       out_i = clamp( action_i + clamp(noise_std * n_i, -noise_clip, noise_clip),  -1, 1 ),     n_i ~ N(0, 1)
///
/// EXPLORATION. Acting, a deterministic policy would try the same action from a state forever; noise around it
/// explores. TD4: `noise_std` from `linearSchedule`, and no clip on the noise (pass infinity).
///
/// TARGET SMOOTHING. Forming the critic's target, the target action gets noise too (TD3: std 0.2, clipped to +-0.5),
/// so the target averages the critic over a small neighbourhood of actions instead of trusting one sharp peak - a
/// peak the actor would otherwise learn to aim at even where the critic is simply wrong.
///
/// The noise is clipped first, so one extreme draw cannot throw an action far; then the action is clamped to the
/// range a tanh policy can produce.
///
/// `(rng, draw_index)` addresses the draw: each draw gets its OWN stream, `rng.split(draw_index)`, and value i is that
/// stream's i-th number. No two draws share numbers, however many draws and however wide the batch. (A single counter
/// such as draw_index * len + i wraps at 2^32: with 1024 environments x 64 action dimensions - 65536 values, 2^16 -
/// draw 65536 would land back on draw 0's counters and the exploration noise would repeat exactly every 65536 steps.)
pub fn addGaussianActionNoise(
    comptime T: type,
    rng: Rng,
    draw_index: u32,
    actions: []const T,
    noise_std: T,
    noise_clip: T,
    actions_out: []T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (actions_out.len != actions.len) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (!(noise_std >= 0) or !(noise_clip >= 0)) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const draw_stream: Rng = rng.split(draw_index);
    for (actions, actions_out, 0..) |action, *noisy_action, value_index| {
        const value_index_as_u32: u32 = @intCast(value_index);
        const standard_normal_draw: T = draw_stream.normal(T, value_index_as_u32);
        const raw_noise: T = noise_std * standard_normal_draw;
        const clipped_noise: T = clamp(raw_noise, -noise_clip, noise_clip);
        noisy_action.* = clamp(action + clipped_noise, -1, 1);
    }
}
policyUpdateDue
/// TD3'S DELAYED POLICY UPDATES: is it the actor's turn, once `critic_updates_done` critic updates have run?
///
/// The critic learns at every update; the actor (and both target networks) only at every `policy_delay`-th. The
/// actor climbs the critic's estimate, and a critic that has just moved is a noisy hill - letting it settle for a
/// few updates first makes the climb steadier (Fujimoto, van Hoof and Meger 2018; TD4 keeps a delay of 2). Asked
/// after each critic update, counting from 1:
///
///       critic updates done:    1   2   3   4   5   6   ...
///       policy_delay = 2:       .   A   .   A   .   A          A: update the actor and the targets
///       policy_delay = 3:       .   .   A   .   .   A
///
/// A delay of 1 is no delay: every update. A delay of 0 has no meaning and is refused.
pub fn policyUpdateDue(critic_updates_done: u64, policy_delay: u64) Error!bool {
    if (policy_delay == 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    const critic_has_updated: bool = critic_updates_done != 0;
    const a_whole_delay_has_passed: bool = critic_updates_done % policy_delay == 0;
    return critic_has_updated and a_whole_delay_has_passed;
}

8. Imitation and tracking#

When the goal is to move like reference motion, the reward has to be learned or designed. This chapter covers adversarial imitation, the gradient penalty that keeps its discriminator useful as a reward, and tracking rewards that compare a motion with its reference.

8.1 Adversarial imitation#

When there is reference motion but no way to write down what moving like it means, a discriminator can be trained to tell the reference from the policy, and its confusion used as the reward.

const style = zrl.discriminatorReward(f64, logit, reward_scale, 1.0e-4);
discriminatorReward
/// The style reward a discriminator gives a policy: `-log(1 - D(x))`, floored and scaled.
///
/// ---- HOW AN ADVERSARY BECOMES A REWARD ----
///
/// A discriminator is trained to tell reference motion from the policy's. Its output, run
/// through a sigmoid, is its confidence that what it saw was REAL. So `1 - D` is its confidence
/// that the policy produced it, and `-log(1 - D)` is large exactly when the discriminator has
/// been fooled. Maximising it is "produce motion this thing believes".
///
/// There is no hand-written style term anywhere in AMP, ASE or HIL. This is the whole of it.
///
/// ---- THE FLOOR IS LOAD-BEARING AND BINDS WHEN THINGS GO WELL ----
///
/// As the policy improves, `sigmoid(logit)` approaches 1, `1 - sigmoid` approaches 0, and the
/// log diverges. **A policy that is WINNING earns an unbounded reward**, which drives the value
/// function to infinity and the advantages with it - so the run collapses at the moment it
/// starts succeeding, which is the hardest moment to attribute.
///
/// The floor caps the reward at `-log(smallest) * reward_scale`. It is not defensive clutter and
/// it is not tunable away: a `smallest` below the type's smallest positive value is RAISED to it,
/// so the reward is bounded whatever the caller passes. See the body for why that is enforced
/// rather than documented.
///
/// ---- SCALAR-FIRST, SO A KERNEL CAN CALL IT ----
///
/// No allocation, no error union, no slice - the same discipline as `ppoClipSample` and
/// `cartpoleOutcome`, and for the same reason: the style reward is computed per sample for every
/// transition in a rollout, which is where the wall clock goes.
/// `reward_scale` and `smallest`, not `scale` and `floor`: this file exports tensor ops by both
/// of those names, and a parameter would shadow them. The same rename `cosineLearningRate`'s
/// `lowest` needed, and the fourth time the `reserved-math-names` rule has earned its place.
pub fn discriminatorReward(
    comptime T: type,
    logit: T,
    reward_scale: T,
    smallest: T,
) T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    // `1 - sigmoid(x)` is `sigmoid(-x)` exactly, and the second form does not cancel: for a
    // confident discriminator `sigmoid(x)` rounds to 1.0 and the subtraction gives exactly zero,
    // where `sigmoid(-x)` underflows smoothly toward it. The floor would hide the difference and
    // the two would still disagree about WHERE it binds.
    const fooled: T = zm.sigmoid(-logit);
    // ---- THE FLOOR IS ENFORCED, NOT REQUESTED ----
    //
    // The doc above used to claim `smallest = 0` was "refused". It was not: this function is
    // scalar-first and has no error union to refuse with, so a zero passed straight through,
    // `@max(fooled, 0)` returned `fooled`, and a saturated `fooled` of exactly zero gave
    // `log(0)` - the infinite reward the floor exists to prevent, delivered by the argument that
    // was supposed to prevent it.
    //
    // A caller's floor is now raised to the smallest positive value of the type if it is lower.
    // That still bounds the reward - at about 87 for f32 - and the bound is a fact rather than a
    // request. Documenting a precondition would have been the other honest option; enforcing it
    // is better, because nothing else in this file can check it either.
    const enforced: T = @max(smallest, zm.floatMin(T));
    return -@log(@max(fooled, enforced)) * reward_scale;
}

The discriminator's sigmoid output is its confidence that what it saw was real, so -log(1 - D) is large exactly when it has been fooled; maximising it means producing motion the discriminator believes. AMP, ASE and HIL use no hand-written style term beyond this.

The floor binds when training goes well: as the policy wins, 1 - sigmoid(logit) approaches zero and the log diverges. An unbounded reward would drive the value function, and the advantages with it, toward infinity just as the policy starts to succeed.

Skill embeddings

ASE conditions the policy on a latent z drawn from the unit hypersphere and rewards an encoder for recovering z from the resulting motion, which pushes different values of z to produce visibly different motion.

try zn.l2NormalizeRows(f64, unit_z, raw_z, 1.0e-8);
const similarity = try graph.rowDot(unit_z, encoder_prediction);
l2NormalizeRows
/// Scale each row to unit length.
///
/// ---- WHY ASE NEEDS THIS AND WHAT BREAKS WITHOUT IT ----
///
/// ASE conditions the policy on a latent `z` drawn from the unit hypersphere, and rewards an
/// encoder for recovering `z` from the resulting motion. That reward is `sum(z * prediction)` -
/// a dot product, which is a COSINE SIMILARITY only because both vectors have length one.
///
/// Skip the normalisation and the reward keeps working and stops meaning what it says: the
/// encoder can raise its score by predicting LARGER vectors rather than better-aimed ones, and
/// the diversity objective quietly becomes a magnitude contest. Nothing fails; the skills just
/// stop being distinct.
///
/// So the test for this function is not "does it normalise" - it is "does the encoder reward
/// stay bounded by 1", which is what unit length buys.
pub fn l2NormalizeRows(
    comptime T: type,
    out: Tensor(T),
    a: Tensor(T),
    epsilon: T,
) Error!void {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or out.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const rows: usize = a.shape[0];
    const cols: usize = a.shape[1];
    if (out.shape[0] != rows or out.shape[1] != cols or cols == 0) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }
    if (epsilon <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    for (0..rows) |r| {
        var total: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
        for (0..cols) |c| {
            const v: T = a.data[a.base + r * cols + c];
            total.add(v * v);
        }
        // `epsilon` inside the root, not added to it. A zero row then normalises to zero rather
        // than to `1/sqrt(epsilon)` times zero - which is the same answer - but a NEAR-zero row
        // is scaled by something finite instead of exploding, which is the case that matters.
        const length: T = @sqrt(total.value() + epsilon);
        for (0..cols) |c| {
            out.data[out.base + r * cols + c] = a.data[a.base + r * cols + c] / length;
        }
    }
}

The encoder reward is a dot product, and it is a cosine similarity only because both sides have length one. Without the normalisation the encoder could raise its score by predicting longer vectors rather than better-aimed ones.

8.2 Differentiating a gradient#

Some losses contain the gradient of the network being trained. An ordinary backward pass cannot produce a gradient that can itself be differentiated; gradientOf can.

Why: a discriminator that stays useful as a reward

An adversarial discriminator D left alone converges toward a step function: a near-perfect classifier whose output jumps from 0 to 1 across a sharp boundary. That is a good classifier and a poor reward, since the policy receives almost no gradient away from the boundary. The remedy is to penalise steepness — how fast D changes with its input:

    L  =  classification loss  +  w · ‖ ∂D/∂x ‖²
                                      └────────┘
                                   how steep D is at x

Descending L needs ∂L/∂θ for the weights θ, and L contains ∂D/∂x, so the update needs a derivative of a derivative:

    ∂        ⎛  ∂D  ⎞
    ──   ·   ⎜ ──── ⎟        a derivative of a derivative
    ∂θ       ⎝  ∂x  ⎠

Why an ordinary backward pass cannot give it

A backward pass fills tensors with numbers. ∂D/∂x arrives as correct but inert floats, with no record that they came from the weights, so their dependence on θ cannot be differentiated. The gradient has to arrive as an expression, still connected to what it was built from.

The smallest case

Take a discriminator that is a dot product with a weight vector:

    D(x) = x · w = x₀w₀ + x₁w₁ + x₂w₂

Its gradient with respect to the input is the weight vector itself:

    ∂D/∂x₀ = w₀        ∂D/∂x₁ = w₁        ∂D/∂x₂ = w₂

    so      ∂D/∂x = w

so the penalty and its derivative are

    P = ‖ ∂D/∂x ‖² = w₀² + w₁² + w₂²
    ∂P/∂w₀ = 2w₀       ∂P/∂w₁ = 2w₁       ∂P/∂w₂ = 2w₂

    so      ∂P/∂w = 2w

Every step is ordinary calculus; what matters is the representation. If ∂D/∂x comes back as the numbers (2.0, -1.0, 0.5), the last step is impossible. If it comes back as the expression this is w, the last step is an ordinary differentiation.

A backward pass that builds instead of computes

    backward       reads a number, multiplies, writes a number into a tensor
    gradientOf     reads a node,   emits    a multiply node onto the tape
const input_gradient = try graph.gradientOf(logit, disc_input);
const penalty = try graph.squaredRowNorm(input_gradient);
try graph.backward(try graph.add(disc_loss, try graph.scale(penalty, w)));

penalty is an ordinary Var, so the ordinary backward pass differentiates it without knowing it contains a gradient.

No second-order machinery is needed, because every backward rule is itself an expression in operations the graph already has:

    g is the gradient arriving from above

    matrix product   y = x A    ->   ∂L/∂x = g AT                a matmul against a transpose
    product          y = a b    ->   ∂L/∂a = g b,  ∂L/∂b = g a      two multiplies
    sigmoid          s = σ(z)   ->   ∂L/∂z = g s (1 − s)         two multiplies and a subtract

Matmul, transpose, multiply and subtract all have derivatives on the graph, so the graph gradientOf emits is differentiable for the same reason any other graph is, and gradientOf can be applied again to a graph that already contains one.

gradientOf supports the operations a discriminator is built from — add, subtract, multiply, scale, matmul, relu, sigmoid, tanh, row sums and transpose — and returns an error for anything else. A silent first-order fallback would turn the penalty into a constant with zero derivative and leave the discriminator unregularised while every number stayed finite.

8.3 Tracking rewards and DTW#

A motion-tracking reward combines quantities in different units — metres of position error, radians of rotation, metres per second of velocity. Summing raw errors would let the quantity with the largest numeric range dominate.

const term = zrl.trackingKernel(f64, position_error, weight, alpha);
trackingKernel
/// One term of a motion-tracking reward: `weight * exp(-alpha * error)`.
///
/// ---- WHY AN EXPONENTIAL KERNEL AND NOT `-error` ----
///
/// A tracking reward has to combine quantities in different units - metres of position error,
/// radians of rotation, metres per second of velocity - into one number. Summing raw errors
/// makes whichever has the largest numeric range dominate, and the policy optimises the units
/// rather than the motion.
///
/// `exp(-alpha * error)` maps every term into `(0, 1]`, so `weight` sets the relative importance
/// deliberately instead of by accident of scale. One at zero error, decaying smoothly.
///
/// ---- `alpha` IS PER QUANTITY, AND THAT IS NOT A DETAIL ----
///
/// The kernel SATURATES. Too large an `alpha` and the reward is zero almost everywhere, so the
/// policy gets no gradient and every imperfect motion looks equally bad; too small and it sits
/// near 1 and says nothing. The useful range is where `alpha * error` is around 1 for a typical
/// error - which differs by orders of magnitude between a joint angle and a root height.
///
/// HIL uses six of these with alphas from 0.05 to 20. A single shared alpha would be wrong for
/// five of them, which is the argument for passing it per call rather than fixing it here.
///
/// Scalar-first, so a kernel can call it - the same discipline as `ppoClipSample`.
pub fn trackingKernel(comptime T: type, err: T, weight: T, alpha: T) T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    // `@abs` rather than trusting the caller: an error is a magnitude, and a signed one would
    // make the kernel EXPLODE on the negative side instead of decaying - a reward that grows
    // without bound the further the motion drifts in one direction.
    return weight * @exp(-alpha * @abs(err));
}

weight * exp(-alpha * |error|) maps every term into (0, 1], so weight sets the relative importance and perfect tracking earns exactly the weight. alpha is chosen per quantity because the kernel saturates: too large and the reward is near zero everywhere, too small and it sits near 1, and either way it carries no signal about improvement. The useful range is where alpha * error is about 1 for a typical error, which differs by orders of magnitude between a joint angle and a root height.

Scoring a motion that ran at a different speed

A policy that clears an obstacle faster or slower than the reference scores badly frame by frame even when its motion is correct: by frame 30 it is at the reference's frame 24, and the comparison measures timing.

const distance = try zn.dtwDistance(f64, gpa, generated, reference);
dtwDistance
/// Dynamic Time Warping distance between two sequences of feature vectors.
///
/// ---- WHY A MOTION CANNOT BE SCORED FRAME BY FRAME ----
///
/// A policy clearing an obstacle does it faster or slower than the reference did. Compare the
/// two frame by frame and a CORRECT motion scores badly, because by frame 30 the generated
/// version is at the reference's frame 24. The error measured is the timing, not the motion.
///
/// DTW aligns the sequences first - stretching and compressing time to find the pairing that
/// minimises total distance - and reports the cost of that best alignment. A motion performed
/// perfectly at a different speed scores ZERO, which is the whole point.
///
/// ---- NO GRADIENT, DELIBERATELY ----
///
/// This is a METRIC, not a loss. The alignment is a discrete argmin over paths and is not
/// usefully differentiable; soft-DTW exists and is a different function. Using this to report
/// how well a policy tracked is right; using it to train is not what it is.
///
/// ---- COST ----
///
/// O(n * m) in time AND memory - the full cost matrix. For the ten-step windows a discriminator
/// uses that is nothing. For whole episodes at 30Hz against every reference clip it is the
/// dominant cost of evaluation, and the standard answer is a Sakoe-Chiba band. **Banding changes
/// the answer**: it is an approximation that forbids alignments straying far from the diagonal,
/// not an optimisation, so it belongs behind an explicit parameter rather than applied quietly.
pub fn dtwDistance(
    comptime T: type,
    gpa: Allocator,
    a: Tensor(T),
    b: Tensor(T),
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (a.rank != 2 or b.rank != 2) {
        return Error.UnsupportedShape;
    }
    const n: usize = a.shape[0];
    const m: usize = b.shape[0];
    const dims: usize = a.shape[1];
    if (b.shape[1] != dims or n == 0 or m == 0) {
        return Error.ShapeMismatch;
    }

    // Two rows, not the whole matrix. The recurrence only ever reads the previous row, so the
    // memory is O(m) even though the time is O(n*m) - which for whole-episode comparison is the
    // difference between kilobytes and megabytes per pair.
    const scratch: []T = try gpa.alloc(T, 2 * (m + 1));
    defer gpa.free(scratch);
    var previous: []T = scratch[0 .. m + 1];
    var current: []T = scratch[m + 1 ..];

    const unreachable_cost: T = inf(T);
    @memset(previous, unreachable_cost);
    previous[0] = 0; // the empty prefix of `a` aligns with the empty prefix of `b` at no cost

    for (1..n + 1) |i| {
        current[0] = unreachable_cost;
        for (1..m + 1) |j| {
            var squared: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
            for (0..dims) |d| {
                const delta: T = a.data[a.base + (i - 1) * dims + d] - b.data[b.base + (j - 1) * dims + d];
                squared.add(delta * delta);
            }
            const step: T = @sqrt(squared.value());
            // The three moves: advance both (a match), advance `a` (the generated motion is
            // slower here), advance `b` (faster). Taking the cheapest is what buys invariance to
            // speed.
            const best: T = @min(previous[j - 1], @min(previous[j], current[j - 1]));
            current[j] = step + best;
        }
        const swap: []T = previous;
        previous = current;
        current = swap;
    }
    return previous[m];
}

Dynamic Time Warping aligns the sequences first — stretching and compressing time to find the pairing with the least total distance — and reports that cost; the same motion at half speed scores zero. It is a metric, not a loss: the alignment is a discrete minimum over paths and is not usefully differentiable. It costs O(n·m); the usual speed-up, a Sakoe-Chiba band, forbids alignments far from the diagonal and so changes the answer.

9. World models: learning to imagine#

A policy trained only on what really happened needs a great deal of experience. A world model lets a learner imagine instead: roll a learned model of the world forward from the current state through a sequence of actions, and ask how that would turn out. The TD-MPC family — TD-MPC2, BMPC, EfficientTDMPC — never reproduces what the world looks like. It learns a compact latent state, a way to step that state forward, and heads that read a reward and a value from it, and trains them on the states the model itself reaches when it rolls forward, so that planning asks the model only the questions it was trained to answer.

This chapter builds one agent of that family, BMPC, from zimrnum's and zimrrl's building blocks, in the order they depend on each other: the opening section draws the whole agent, and each later section fills in one of its boxes with what the piece is, how to use it, and a worked example.

The course: an agent that plans in its imagination#

BMPC runs two loops at once. Acting happens every frame: the observation is encoded into a latent state, the planner searches over short sequences of actions by imagining where each would lead, and the search's answer is both acted on and remembered. Learning happens every update: stretches of remembered play train the world model to imagine better and the policy to propose what the planner would have chosen. The two loops share one set of networks — learning trains them, acting reads them as they are at that moment.

The course follows the arrows backwards from the networks to the decision. First the world model's parts (9.1–9.6: one network in many graphs, the layers, numbers predicted as distributions, the rollout, its loss, one update). Then the planner that uses it (9.7: scoring a plan by imagination; 9.8: MPPI's search). Then the memory that learning draws from (9.9), three tools the losses need (9.10–9.12), and the policy and how it learns from the planner (9.13–9.14). Last, the pieces that close the loop: the policy's own plans inside the search (9.15), the targets the value heads learn (9.16), one decision assembled from all of it (9.17), one update of the world model, assembled the same way (9.18), the policy update and the whole update (9.19), and reanalyze, which updates the planner's stored answers (9.20).

9.1 A network is its numbers plus its arithmetic#

In zimrnum a network is two things kept apart: its numbers — the weight and bias tensors — and its arithmetic, the operations that turn an input into an output. The numbers live in a Params; the arithmetic is added to a graph by binding them. Keeping the two apart is what lets one network play three roles at once, which is exactly what a TD-MPC learner asks of its value network:

  • Trained. In the training graph the tensors enter as parameters: the backward pass fills their gradients and an Optimizer changes them in place.
  • Frozen. In the planner, and wherever targets are built, the same network is only read. Bound .frozen, the same tensors enter as constants — a constant wraps the tensor it is given rather than copying it, so the planner always reads the weights as the last optimiser step left them, with nothing to keep in sync.
  • Target. A value network trained toward targets it computes itself chases its own tail. The cure is a second copy that trails the trained one slowly: after each update it moves a small fraction of the way toward the trained weights. With a fraction of 0.01 the target is roughly an average of the network's last hundred versions — recent enough to keep improving, steady enough to aim at.
const live: zn.Params(f32) = try .init(allocator, &.{ &.{ 64, 32 }, &.{ 1, 32 } });
const target: zn.Params(f32) = try live.clone(allocator);

// Learned in the training graph ...
const trained_handles = try live.bind(&training_graph, .trained, allocator);
// ... read, live, in the planner ...
const planner_handles = try live.bind(&planning_graph, .frozen, allocator);
// ... and trailed by the target after every update.
try target.follow(live, 0.01);
Params
/// A NETWORK'S PARAMETERS - every tensor one network reads, held as ONE set that can be bound into ANY graph.
///
/// ---- WHY A SET, AND WHY "BIND" ----
///
/// In this library a network is two things kept apart: its NUMBERS (the weight and bias tensors - this type) and its
/// ARITHMETIC (a builder function that, given a graph and the bound numbers, adds the network's operations to that
/// graph). Keeping them apart is what lets one network take part in several graphs at once - which is exactly what
/// the TD-MPC family of algorithms (TD-MPC2, BMPC, EfficientTDMPC) asks of it:
///
///   * in the TRAINING graph the network is being learned: its tensors are graph PARAMETERS, the backward pass fills
///     their gradients, and an `Optimizer` built on them changes them in place;
///   * in the PLANNING graph (the MPPI planner, scoring hundreds of candidate action sequences) and in the graph that
///     builds VALUE TARGETS, the same network is only READ: its tensors are graph CONSTANTS - no gradient, no update -
///     yet they must be the CURRENT numbers, the ones the last optimizer step left behind;
///   * and a TARGET copy of the value network trails the trained one slowly (see "the target network" below).
///
/// BMPC's PyTorch code keeps three module "views" over two tensor sets for this (`_Qs`, `_detach_Qs`, `_target_Qs`).
/// Here it is two `Params` and a choice made when binding:
///
///   * `bind(graph, .trained)` - the tensors enter the graph as parameters;
///   * `bind(graph, .frozen)` - the SAME tensors enter as constants. `Graph.constant` wraps the tensor it is handed; it
///     does not copy it. So a frozen binding READS THE LIVE WEIGHTS: after an optimizer step in the training graph, the
///     planning graph's next `recompute` computes with the stepped weights - nothing to copy, nothing to re-sync, no
///     way for the two to drift apart. (The known answers below prove it, and prove that a copying binding would fail.)
///
/// ---- THE TARGET NETWORK ----
///
/// Temporal-difference learning trains a value network toward a target made FROM a value network: "the reward now,
/// plus the discounted value of where we land next". If that target came from the very network being trained, every
/// update would move the target along with the prediction - the network chasing its own tail, which easily diverges.
/// The cure (DQN, Mnih et al. 2015; its smooth "Polyak" form from DDPG, Lillicrap et al. 2016) is a second copy that
/// FOLLOWS the trained one slowly: after each update, move it a small fraction tau of the way toward the trained
/// weights. BMPC uses tau = 0.01, which makes the target roughly an average of the network's last hundred versions -
/// recent enough to keep improving, slow enough to be a steady thing to aim at. `clone` makes the copy; `follow` moves
/// it; it is bound `.frozen` wherever targets are computed.
///
/// ---- LEARNING RATES, AND MEMORY ----
///
/// A learning rate per set (BMPC's encoder learns at 0.3 times the others' rate) needs nothing here: build one
/// `Optimizer` per set, each with its own rate. Every tensor is a whole, contiguous allocation made by `init` or
/// `clone` from the allocator they are given, and `deinit` returns them to it (with an arena, freeing the arena is
/// enough). If an allocation fails part way, what was already allocated is given back - with any allocator.
pub fn Params(comptime T: type) type {
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The tensors, in the order the network's builder reads them.
        tensors: []Tensor(T),

        /// A set of zero-filled tensors, one per shape. The network's builder then initialises them its own way
        /// (BMPC starts most layers from a truncated normal, and the reward and value heads' last layers at exactly
        /// zero - so an untrained model predicts a reward and a value of zero everywhere).
        pub fn init(allocator: Allocator, shapes: []const []const usize) Error!Self {
            const tensors: []Tensor(T) = try allocator.alloc(Tensor(T), shapes.len);
            var allocated_count: usize = 0;
            errdefer {
                for (tensors[0..allocated_count]) |already_allocated| {
                    allocator.free(already_allocated.data);
                }
                allocator.free(tensors);
            }
            for (tensors, shapes) |*tensor, shape| {
                tensor.* = try Tensor(T).alloc(allocator, shape);
                allocated_count += 1;
                tensor.fill(0);
            }
            return .{ .tensors = tensors };
        }

        /// A second set of the same shapes, holding a COPY of these values - where a target network starts: equal to
        /// the trained network, and from then on following it (`follow`).
        pub fn clone(self: Self, allocator: Allocator) Error!Self {
            const copies: []Tensor(T) = try allocator.alloc(Tensor(T), self.tensors.len);
            var allocated_count: usize = 0;
            errdefer {
                for (copies[0..allocated_count]) |already_allocated| {
                    allocator.free(already_allocated.data);
                }
                allocator.free(copies);
            }
            for (copies, self.tensors) |*copy, original| {
                copy.* = try Tensor(T).alloc(allocator, original.shape[0..original.rank]);
                allocated_count += 1;
                // Both are whole, contiguous allocations of the same shape (made by `init` or `clone`), so the
                // element buffers line up one for one.
                @memcpy(copy.data, original.data);
            }
            return .{ .tensors = copies };
        }

        /// Give every tensor back to `allocator` - the one `init` or `clone` was handed. (With an arena: not needed.)
        pub fn deinit(self: Self, allocator: Allocator) void {
            for (self.tensors) |tensor| {
                allocator.free(tensor.data);
            }
            allocator.free(self.tensors);
        }

        /// Every tensor into `graph`, as `binding` says. The returned values are in the tensors' order - the order
        /// the network's builder reads them - and are allocated from `allocator`. A `.trained` binding's values are
        /// also exactly what an `Optimizer` is built on.
        pub fn bind(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            binding: Binding,
            allocator: Allocator,
        ) Error![]Var {
            const bound_values: []Var = try allocator.alloc(Var, self.tensors.len);
            errdefer allocator.free(bound_values);
            // Trained: differentiated by the graph, stepped in place by an `Optimizer` built on these values. Frozen:
            // the same tensor, read afresh at every `recompute`; never differentiated, never stepped.
            for (bound_values, self.tensors) |*bound_value, tensor| {
                bound_value.* = try bindTensor(T, graph, tensor, binding);
            }
            return bound_values;
        }

        /// Move this set - a TARGET network - the fraction `fraction` of the way toward `online`, tensor by tensor:
        /// every weight becomes `(1 - fraction) * target + fraction * online`. That is `polyakUpdate`, written in
        /// exactly this form so that `fraction = 1` is a true copy and `fraction = 0` changes nothing, to the last bit.
        /// Called once per update, with BMPC's tau = 0.01.
        pub fn follow(self: Self, online: Self, fraction: T) Error!void {
            if (self.tensors.len != online.tensors.len) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            for (self.tensors, online.tensors) |target_tensor, online_tensor| {
                try polyakUpdate(T, target_tensor, online_tensor, fraction);
            }
        }
    };
}

A different learning rate for one part of a network — BMPC's encoder learns at 0.3 times the others' rate — needs nothing special: give each part its own Params and its own Optimizer.

9.2 The layers a world model is built from#

TD-MPC2 builds every network's hidden layers from one kind of layer: a linear map, a layer normalisation, an activation. A world model is rolled forward through itself and its values are bootstrapped from its own predictions, so without the normalisation activations can drift further at every step until the losses explode; normalising after every linear map keeps each layer's output on the same footing however the weights move. zimrnum provides the pieces on the template of its Dense layer — a layer owns its tensors and records itself onto any graph:

  • LayerNorm — each row brought to mean 0 and spread 1, then a learned gain and bias per feature.
  • NormedDense — BMPC's layer: Dense, optional dropout, LayerNorm, an activation.
  • Activation — none (a head's raw output), ReLU, Mish, or SimNorm. SimNorm softmaxes small groups of features, so an encoder's or a dynamics network's output is a bundle of small probability vectors, bounded however large the inputs, which keeps a rolled-out latent from wandering off.
  • Mlp — a stack of layers described by a Spec, so an architecture is data; its last layer is a plain Dense for a head, or a normed layer ending in SimNorm for an encoder or dynamics network.

A rollout applies the dynamics network once per step, always with the same parameters, so a network is bound to the graph once and applied as many times as needed. Binding it at every step would create a separate copy of each weight per step, each receiving only its own share of the gradient.

const ends_in_sim_norm: zn.Activation = .{ .sim_norm = 8 }; // groups of 8, each a probability vector
const encoder: zn.Mlp(f32) = try .init(allocator, zn.Rng.init(1), .{
    .input = observation_width,
    .hidden = &.{256},
    .output = 512,
    .output_activation = ends_in_sim_norm,
});

// Bind once ...
const encoder_handles = try encoder.bind(&graph, .trained, allocator);
// ... apply as often as the graph needs it.
const latent = try encoder.apply(&graph, encoder_handles, observations, null);

// A target network is the same architecture over a copy of the numbers.
const target_numbers = try (try encoder.params(allocator)).clone(allocator);
const target_encoder = try encoder.withParams(target_numbers, allocator);

// The pieces on their own: one of BMPC's layers, and the norm inside it (gain 1, bias 0 to start).
const layer: zn.NormedDense(f32) = try .init(allocator, zn.Rng.init(2), 512, 512, .small_normal, .mish, 0);
const norm: zn.LayerNorm(f32) = try .init(allocator, 512);
Activation
/// What a layer ends in. A value, so a network's architecture can be written down as a `Mlp.Spec` and read back.
pub const Activation = union(enum) {
    /// Nothing - a head's raw output: logits, a value, a mean.
    none,
    relu,
    /// TD-MPC2's choice inside every layer.
    mish,
    /// SimNorm with this group size - how TD-MPC2's encoder and dynamics end (`simNormRows`).
    sim_norm: usize,
};
Mlp
/// A MULTI-LAYER PERCEPTRON in TD-MPC2's shape (`common/layers.py`'s `mlp`): hidden `NormedDense` layers, and a last
/// layer that is either a plain `Dense` - a HEAD, whose raw output is what the network predicts (logits over bins, a
/// policy's mean and log-std) - or a `NormedDense` ending in an activation (an ENCODER or DYNAMICS network ending in
/// SimNorm, so its output is a latent state). BMPC's networks, in this form:
///
///     encoder   mlp(obs,          [256],      512, act = SimNorm(8))
///     dynamics  mlp(512 + action, [512, 512], 512, act = SimNorm(8))
///     reward    mlp(512 + action, [512, 512], 101)                      last layer zeroed
///     value     mlp(512,          [512, 512], 101, dropout 0.01)         last layer zeroed, twice (an ensemble)
///     policy    mlp(512,          [512, 512], 2 * action)
///
/// Written down as a `Spec` - so an architecture is data - and built by `init`. `attachAs` records the whole stack on
/// any graph and hands back its output and every parameter's handle (for an `Optimizer`); `params` presents its
/// tensors as a `Params` (for a target copy: `params().clone()`, then `withParams`, `follow`); `deinit` frees them.
/// No `Sequential`: this struct IS the composition, and `attachAs` chains the layers.
pub fn Mlp(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        /// The architecture, as data.
        pub const Spec = struct {
            input: usize,
            hidden: []const usize,
            output: usize,
            hidden_activation: Activation = .mish,
            /// `.none` makes the last layer a plain `Dense` (a head); anything else a `NormedDense` ending in it.
            output_activation: Activation = .none,
            /// Dropout on the FIRST hidden layer only (BMPC's value heads: 0.01), 0 for none.
            first_dropout: T = 0,
            scheme: InitScheme = .small_normal,
            /// Zero the last layer's weight and bias (only a plain last layer): BMPC's reward and value heads start
            /// out predicting exactly zero everywhere, so early targets are not chased through random noise.
            zero_last: bool = false,
        };

        /// The last layer: a head, or a normed layer ending in an activation.
        pub const Last = union(enum) {
            plain: Dense(T),
            normed: NormedDense(T),
        };

        /// What `attachAs` hands back: the output, and EVERY parameter's handle in the tensors' order.
        pub const Attached = struct {
            out: Var,
            values: []Var,
        };

        spec: Spec,
        hidden: []NormedDense(T),
        last: Last,

        pub fn init(allocator: Allocator, rng: Rng, spec: Spec) Error!Self {
            try checkSpec(spec);
            const hidden: []NormedDense(T) = try allocator.alloc(NormedDense(T), spec.hidden.len);
            var built_count: usize = 0;
            errdefer {
                for (hidden[0..built_count]) |layer| {
                    freeTensors(allocator, &layer.tensors());
                }
                allocator.free(hidden);
            }
            var fan_in: usize = spec.input;
            for (hidden, spec.hidden, 0..) |*layer, width, index| {
                const dropout: T = if (index == 0) spec.first_dropout else 0;
                const layer_rng: Rng = rng.split(@intCast(index));
                layer.* = try NormedDense(T).init(
                    allocator,
                    layer_rng,
                    fan_in,
                    width,
                    spec.scheme,
                    spec.hidden_activation,
                    dropout,
                );
                built_count += 1;
                fan_in = width;
            }
            const last_rng: Rng = rng.split(@intCast(spec.hidden.len));
            const last: Last = switch (spec.output_activation) {
                .none => .{ .plain = try Dense(T).init(allocator, last_rng, fan_in, spec.output, spec.scheme) },
                else => .{ .normed = try NormedDense(T).init(
                    allocator,
                    last_rng,
                    fan_in,
                    spec.output,
                    spec.scheme,
                    spec.output_activation,
                    0,
                ) },
            };
            if (spec.zero_last) {
                switch (last) {
                    .plain => |head| {
                        head.weight.fill(0);
                        head.bias.fill(0);
                    },
                    // A normed last layer's zero would be undone by its norm; the spec check refused it.
                    .normed => unreachable,
                }
            }
            return .{ .spec = spec, .hidden = hidden, .last = last };
        }

        /// Refuse a spec that describes no network - or one whose SimNorm cannot divide its width.
        fn checkSpec(spec: Spec) Error!void {
            if (spec.input == 0 or spec.output == 0) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            for (spec.hidden) |width| {
                if (width == 0) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
                try checkActivationFits(spec.hidden_activation, width);
            }
            try checkActivationFits(spec.output_activation, spec.output);
            if (spec.zero_last and spec.output_activation != .none) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            if (spec.first_dropout < 0 or spec.first_dropout >= 1) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
        }

        fn checkActivationFits(activation: Activation, width: usize) Error!void {
            switch (activation) {
                .sim_norm => |group| if (group == 0 or width % group != 0) {
                    return Error.DomainError;
                },
                else => {},
            }
        }

        /// Give every tensor back to `allocator` (the one `init` was handed), and the layers' storage.
        pub fn deinit(self: Self, allocator: Allocator) void {
            for (self.hidden) |layer| {
                freeTensors(allocator, &layer.tensors());
            }
            switch (self.last) {
                .plain => |head| freeTensors(allocator, &.{ head.weight, head.bias }),
                .normed => |layer| freeTensors(allocator, &layer.tensors()),
            }
            allocator.free(self.hidden);
        }

        fn freeTensors(allocator: Allocator, tensors_to_free: []const Tensor(T)) void {
            for (tensors_to_free) |tensor| {
                allocator.free(tensor.data);
            }
        }

        /// How many tensors the network has: four per normed layer, two for a plain head.
        pub fn tensorCount(self: Self) usize {
            const last_count: usize = switch (self.last) {
                .plain => 2,
                .normed => 4,
            };
            return 4 * self.hidden.len + last_count;
        }

        /// Record the whole network onto `graph`, its parameters TRAINED and dropout OFF; `values` from `allocator`.
        pub fn attach(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            input: Var,
            allocator: Allocator,
        ) Error!Attached {
            return self.attachAs(graph, input, .trained, null, allocator);
        }

        /// Record the whole network onto `graph`: each layer's output is the next one's input; the parameters enter
        /// as `binding` says; dropout (the first hidden layer's, if the spec has any) is drawn from `dropout_rng`, or
        /// left out when that is null. `values` holds every parameter's handle in the tensors' order, allocated from
        /// `allocator` - what an `Optimizer` is built on for a `.trained` binding.
        pub fn attachAs(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            input: Var,
            binding: Binding,
            dropout_rng: ?Rng,
            allocator: Allocator,
        ) Error!Attached {
            const bound_parameters: []Var = try self.bind(graph, binding, allocator);
            errdefer allocator.free(bound_parameters);
            const output: Var = try self.apply(graph, bound_parameters, input, dropout_rng);
            return .{ .out = output, .values = bound_parameters };
        }

        /// Every tensor into `graph`, as `binding` says, in the tensors' order - the handles `apply` reads, allocated
        /// from `allocator`. BIND ONCE, APPLY MANY TIMES: a rollout applies the same dynamics network at every step,
        /// and every application must read the same handles, or the graph would see H separate parameters each
        /// holding a slice of the gradient.
        pub fn bind(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            binding: Binding,
            allocator: Allocator,
        ) Error![]Var {
            const bound_parameters: []Var = try allocator.alloc(Var, self.tensorCount());
            errdefer allocator.free(bound_parameters);
            var bound_count: usize = 0;
            for (self.hidden) |layer| {
                for (layer.tensors()) |tensor| {
                    bound_parameters[bound_count] = try bindTensor(T, graph, tensor, binding);
                    bound_count += 1;
                }
            }
            switch (self.last) {
                .plain => |head| {
                    bound_parameters[bound_count] = try bindTensor(T, graph, head.weight, binding);
                    bound_parameters[bound_count + 1] = try bindTensor(T, graph, head.bias, binding);
                },
                .normed => |layer| {
                    for (layer.tensors()) |tensor| {
                        bound_parameters[bound_count] = try bindTensor(T, graph, tensor, binding);
                        bound_count += 1;
                    }
                },
            }
            return bound_parameters;
        }

        /// The network's ARITHMETIC over handles from `bind`: each layer's output is the next one's input; dropout
        /// (the first hidden layer's, if the spec has any) is drawn from `dropout_rng`, or left out when that is null.
        pub fn apply(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            bound_parameters: []const Var,
            input: Var,
            dropout_rng: ?Rng,
        ) Error!Var {
            if (bound_parameters.len != self.tensorCount()) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            // The handles are consumed in `bind`'s order, four per normed layer, and each layer's output becomes the
            // next one's input.
            var parameters_used: usize = 0;
            var activations: Var = input;
            for (self.hidden, 0..) |layer, layer_index| {
                // Every layer gets its own rng stream (only the first hidden layer has a dropout rate to use it).
                const layer_rng: ?Rng = if (dropout_rng) |rng| rng.split(@intCast(layer_index)) else null;
                const layer_parameters: [4]Var = bound_parameters[parameters_used..][0..4].*;
                activations = try layer.applyBound(graph, layer_parameters, activations, layer_rng);
                parameters_used += 4;
            }
            return switch (self.last) {
                .plain => blk: {
                    const head_weight: Var = bound_parameters[parameters_used];
                    const head_bias: Var = bound_parameters[parameters_used + 1];
                    break :blk try Dense(T).applyBound(graph, head_weight, head_bias, activations);
                },
                .normed => |layer| blk: {
                    const layer_parameters: [4]Var = bound_parameters[parameters_used..][0..4].*;
                    break :blk try layer.applyBound(graph, layer_parameters, activations, null);
                },
            };
        }

        /// Every tensor, in the order `attachAs` binds them, as a `Params` over the SAME tensors (the slice from
        /// `allocator`; the tensors shared). `params().clone()` starts a target network.
        pub fn params(self: Self, allocator: Allocator) Error!Params(T) {
            const every_tensor: []Tensor(T) = try allocator.alloc(Tensor(T), self.tensorCount());
            var gathered_count: usize = 0;
            for (self.hidden) |layer| {
                every_tensor[gathered_count..][0..4].* = layer.tensors();
                gathered_count += 4;
            }
            switch (self.last) {
                .plain => |head| {
                    every_tensor[gathered_count..][0..2].* = .{ head.weight, head.bias };
                },
                .normed => |layer| {
                    every_tensor[gathered_count..][0..4].* = layer.tensors();
                },
            }
            return .{ .tensors = every_tensor };
        }

        /// This architecture over `given`'s tensors (a `params().clone()`): the TARGET network, to be attached
        /// `.frozen` and moved by `given.follow`. The layers' storage comes from `allocator`; the tensors are shared.
        pub fn withParams(self: Self, given: Params(T), allocator: Allocator) Error!Self {
            if (given.tensors.len != self.tensorCount()) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const hidden: []NormedDense(T) = try allocator.alloc(NormedDense(T), self.hidden.len);
            var tensors_used: usize = 0;
            for (hidden, self.hidden) |*copy, layer| {
                copy.* = layer.withTensors(given.tensors[tensors_used..][0..4]);
                tensors_used += 4;
            }
            const last: Last = switch (self.last) {
                .plain => .{ .plain = .{
                    .weight = given.tensors[tensors_used],
                    .bias = given.tensors[tensors_used + 1],
                } },
                .normed => |layer| .{ .normed = layer.withTensors(given.tensors[tensors_used..][0..4]) },
            };
            return .{ .spec = self.spec, .hidden = hidden, .last = last };
        }
    };
}
Rng
/// A reproducible random source addressed by (seed, index) rather than by mutation.
///
/// WHY COUNTER-BASED, AND NOT A STATEFUL GENERATOR
///
/// A classic PRNG carries state and advances it, so drawing the i-th value means drawing the
/// first i. That is fine on one CPU thread and useless everywhere else this library is going:
///
/// * **A GPU kernel has no shared state.** Thread `i` must be able to compute ITS value without
/// any other thread having run. A counter-based generator is a pure function of the index, so
/// the kernel is `value(seed, thread_id)` and needs no sequencing at all.
/// * **CPU and GPU must agree EXACTLY.** The CPU path is this library's oracle (plan section 5 rung
/// 1); an oracle that produces a different random stream than the thing it checks is not an
/// oracle. Same function, same integers, same answer.
/// * **A failing case is reproducible from two numbers.** "seed 7, index 4096" is the whole
/// state, so a bad draw can be re-created in a unit test without replaying a sequence.
///
/// THE MIXER IS DELIBERATELY MODEST AND THE TESTS SAY SO. `mix32` is the murmur3 finalizer -
/// a bijection with good avalanche, applied to `index` folded with `seed`. It is NOT a
/// cryptographic generator and NOT claimed to pass a full statistical battery; what it is pinned
/// on is the properties this library actually relies on: determinism, range, stream independence,
/// and first/second moments. A stronger generator can replace the body without touching a single
/// call site, precisely because the interface is (seed, index) rather than a stream.
pub const Rng = struct {
    seed: u32,

    /// A generator for a given seed. Two `Rng`s with the same seed are the same function.
    pub fn init(seed: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = seed };
    }

    /// A DERIVED generator, so one seed can feed several independent uses without the caller
    /// inventing seeds. `parent.split(0)` and `parent.split(1)` are unrelated streams.
    ///
    /// Mixing the label rather than adding it: `seed + 1` and `seed + 2` are adjacent inputs to
    /// the mixer, and adjacency is exactly what a hash is asked not to preserve. Passing the
    /// label through `mix32` first means the derived seeds are as far apart as any other pair.
    pub fn split(self: Rng, label: u32) Rng {
        return .{ .seed = mix32(self.seed ^ mix32(label ^ 0x9e3779b9)) };
    }

    /// The number of mantissa bits `T` can hold, including the implicit leading one. This is how
    /// many random bits a draw in `[0, 1)` may use: fewer wastes range, MORE ROUNDS UP TO 1.0 and
    /// breaks the half-open interval.
    fn mantissaWidth(comptime T: type) u32 {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return switch (T) {
            f16 => 11,
            f32 => 24,
            f64 => 53,
            else => unreachable,
        };
    }

    /// One 32-bit word of the generator's output at `index`, from independent stream `stream`.
    ///
    /// EACH KIND OF DRAW OWNS A STREAM, SO KINDS DO NOT INTERFERE
    ///
    /// Without this, every method would start from `bits(index)` and drawing two different KINDS
    /// at one index would return values that determine each other - `intBelow(i, n)` would be a
    /// function of `unitFloat(i)`. A caller has no reason to expect that and every reason to be
    /// hurt by it, so the stream is part of the addressing rather than the caller's problem.
    ///
    /// The stream index is folded into the MIXER, not into the index. Folding it into the index
    /// (`bits(index ^ K)`) makes indices `i` and `i ^ K` draw from the same two words with their
    /// roles swapped, so those pairs are not independent - which is exactly the defect this
    /// replaced.
    ///
    /// `stream == 0` adds nothing, so `bits` is unchanged by the introduction of rounds.
    fn word(self: Rng, index: u32, stream: u32) u32 {
        return mix32(mix32(index *% 0x9e3779b9) ^ self.seed ^ (stream *% 0x85ebca6b));
    }

    /// The raw 32-bit draw at `index`, stream 0. Distinct from every other kind of draw.
    pub fn bits(self: Rng, index: u32) u32 {
        return self.word(index, 0);
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)` from stream `stream` (and `stream + 1` when `T` needs more than 32 bits).
    ///
    /// Exactly `mantissaWidth(T)` random bits, scaled by 2^-width. The largest result is
    /// (2^width - 1) / 2^width, which is the largest `T` strictly below one - so the interval is
    /// half-open for every width, including `f16`, where using 24 bits rounds to 1.0 instead.
    fn unitFloatRound(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32, comptime stream: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const width: u32 = comptime mantissaWidth(T);
        const first: u64 = self.word(index, stream);
        const mantissa: u64 = if (comptime width <= 32)
            first >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(32 - width))
        else
            (first << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width - 32))) |
                (@as(u64, self.word(index, stream + 1)) >> comptime @as(u6, @intCast(64 - width)));
        const numerator: T = @floatFromInt(mantissa);
        const denominator: T = @floatFromInt(@as(u64, 1) << comptime @as(u6, @intCast(width)));
        return numerator / denominator;
    }

    /// A float in `[0, 1)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn unitFloat(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        return self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 1);
    }

    /// A float in `[low, high)`, drawn at `index`.
    pub fn uniform(
        self: Rng,
        comptime T: type,
        index: u32,
        low: T,
        high: T,
    ) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        return low + (high - low) * self.unitFloat(T, index);
    }

    /// A standard normal draw (mean 0, variance 1) at `index`, by the Box-Muller transform.
    ///
    /// ITS TWO UNIFORMS COME FROM ITS OWN STREAMS (3-6), not from the uniform stream, so
    /// `normal(i)` and `unitFloat(i)` are independent and a caller mixing them needs no care.
    ///
    /// Box-Muller rather than a ziggurat because a ziggurat REJECTS: the number of uniforms it
    /// consumes varies per sample, which would make the value at `index` depend on how many
    /// rejections happened. That is not a pure function of the index and would not survive being
    /// evaluated on an independent GPU thread. Box-Muller consumes exactly two, always.
    ///
    /// The radius draw is taken from `(0, 1]` rather than `[0, 1)` because `@log(0)` is -inf.
    pub fn normal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, index: u32) T {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        const two_pi: T = 6.283185307179586;
        const radius_draw: T = 1.0 - self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 3);
        const angle_draw: T = self.unitFloatRound(T, index, 5);
        const radius: T = @sqrt(-2.0 * @log(radius_draw));
        return radius * @cos(two_pi * angle_draw);
    }

    /// An integer in `[0, bound)`, drawn at `index` from its own stream. `bound == 0` yields 0.
    ///
    /// Lemire's multiply-shift, not `% bound`: the modulo is biased toward small values whenever
    /// `bound` does not divide 2^32, and the bias stays invisible until something like a replay
    /// buffer over-samples its early entries.
    pub fn intBelow(self: Rng, index: u32, bound: u32) u32 {
        if (bound == 0) {
            return 0;
        }
        const wide: u64 = @as(u64, self.word(index, 7)) * @as(u64, bound);
        return @intCast(wide >> 32);
    }

    /// Fill `out` with uniforms in `[0, 1)`, drawn at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillUniform(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.unitFloat(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }

    /// Fill `out` with standard normal draws, at indices `0..out.len`.
    pub fn fillNormal(self: Rng, comptime T: type, out: []T) void {
        comptime requireFloat(T);
        for (out, 0..) |*slot, i| {
            slot.* = self.normal(T, @intCast(i));
        }
    }
};
NormedDense
/// BMPC'S "NORMEDLINEAR": a `Dense`, then dropout if asked, then a `LayerNorm`, then an activation - the layer TD-MPC2
/// builds every network from (`common/layers.py`). On `Dense`'s template: it owns its four tensors, and `attach` /
/// `attachAs` record it on any graph.
///
/// Why the order is linear, norm, activation: the norm keeps the activation's input on one footing however the
/// weights drift (`LayerNorm`), and the activation then shapes a normalised signal - a Mish of numbers around zero,
/// or a SimNorm of a well-scaled group. Dropout, where a network has it (BMPC's value heads, 0.01 on the first
/// layer), sits before the norm, so the norm sees the thinned signal.
pub fn NormedDense(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        dense: Dense(T),
        norm: LayerNorm(T),
        activation: Activation = .mish,
        /// The dropout rate on the linear output, 0 for none. Applied only when `attachAs` is handed an `Rng`.
        dropout: T = 0,

        /// What `attach` hands back: the output, and handles for the four parameters.
        pub const Attached = struct {
            out: Var,
            weight: Var,
            bias: Var,
            gain: Var,
            norm_bias: Var,
        };

        pub fn init(
            allocator: Allocator,
            rng: Rng,
            fan_in: usize,
            fan_out: usize,
            scheme: InitScheme,
            activation: Activation,
            dropout: T,
        ) Error!Self {
            if (dropout < 0 or dropout >= 1) {
                return Error.DomainError;
            }
            const dense: Dense(T) = try .init(allocator, rng, fan_in, fan_out, scheme);
            errdefer {
                allocator.free(dense.weight.data);
                allocator.free(dense.bias.data);
            }
            const layer_norm: LayerNorm(T) = try .init(allocator, fan_out);
            return .{ .dense = dense, .norm = layer_norm, .activation = activation, .dropout = dropout };
        }

        /// Record the layer onto `graph`, its parameters TRAINED and its dropout OFF.
        pub fn attach(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), input: Var) Error!Attached {
            return self.attachAs(graph, input, .trained, null);
        }

        /// Record the layer onto `graph`, its parameters entering as `binding` says (see `Dense.attachAs`), and its
        /// dropout drawn from `dropout_rng` - or left out when that is null, as at evaluation.
        pub fn attachAs(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            input: Var,
            binding: Binding,
            dropout_rng: ?Rng,
        ) Error!Attached {
            var bound: [4]Var = undefined;
            for (&bound, self.tensors()) |*value, tensor| {
                value.* = try bindTensor(T, graph, tensor, binding);
            }
            return .{
                .out = try self.applyBound(graph, bound, input, dropout_rng),
                .weight = bound[0],
                .bias = bound[1],
                .gain = bound[2],
                .norm_bias = bound[3],
            };
        }

        /// The layer's arithmetic alone, over its four bound handles in `tensors`' order - weight, bias, gain, norm
        /// bias (see `Dense.applyBound` for why binding and arithmetic are kept apart).
        pub fn applyBound(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            bound_parameters: [4]Var,
            input: Var,
            dropout_rng: ?Rng,
        ) Error!Var {
            const weight: Var = bound_parameters[0];
            const bias: Var = bound_parameters[1];
            const norm_gain: Var = bound_parameters[2];
            const norm_bias: Var = bound_parameters[3];
            // 1. The linear map: every output feature a weighted mix of every input feature, plus its bias.
            const linear_output: Var = try Dense(T).applyBound(graph, weight, bias, input);
            // 2. Dropout, where this layer has it and an rng was handed in: a random few features zeroed and the rest
            //    scaled up to keep the expected sum - so no single feature becomes something the next layer leans on.
            const use_dropout: bool = self.dropout > 0 and dropout_rng != null;
            const after_dropout: Var = if (use_dropout)
                try graph.dropout(linear_output, self.dropout, dropout_rng.?)
            else
                linear_output;
            // 3. The layer norm: each row back to mean 0 and spread 1, then the learned per-feature gain and bias.
            const normalised: Var = try self.norm.applyBound(graph, norm_gain, norm_bias, after_dropout);
            // 4. The activation, on a signal the norm has put on a known footing.
            return graph.activate(normalised, self.activation);
        }

        /// The four tensors, in the order `attach` binds them: weight, bias, gain, norm bias.
        pub fn tensors(self: Self) [4]Tensor(T) {
            return .{ self.dense.weight, self.dense.bias, self.norm.gain, self.norm.bias };
        }

        /// The same layer - same activation, dropout and epsilon - over OTHER tensors (four, in `tensors`' order):
        /// how a target network is laid over its own copy of the numbers. Nothing is copied or allocated.
        fn withTensors(self: Self, other_tensors: []const Tensor(T)) Self {
            return .{
                .dense = .{ .weight = other_tensors[0], .bias = other_tensors[1] },
                .norm = .{ .gain = other_tensors[2], .bias = other_tensors[3], .epsilon = self.norm.epsilon },
                .activation = self.activation,
                .dropout = self.dropout,
            };
        }
    };
}
LayerNorm
/// A LAYER NORMALISATION with its own gain and bias, on `Dense`'s template: it owns two tensors, and `attach` /
/// `attachAs` record it onto any graph.
///
/// WHAT IT DOES. Each row is normalised - its mean subtracted, divided by its standard deviation - so every row
/// arrives at the next layer with mean 0 and spread 1, whatever the layer before it produced; then each feature is
/// scaled by a learned GAIN and shifted by a learned BIAS, so the network can undo the normalisation wherever that
/// helps. Started at gain 1 and bias 0, it is exactly `Graph.layerNormRows` (a known answer below).
///
/// WHY THE TD-MPC FAMILY PUTS ONE IN EVERY LAYER (TD-MPC2's "NormedLinear": linear, layer norm, Mish). A world model
/// is rolled forward through ITSELF, and a value is bootstrapped from its own predictions; unnormalised activations
/// can drift step after step until the losses explode. A layer norm after every linear map keeps every activation on
/// the same footing however the weights move - TD-MPC2 credits it, with SimNorm, for training one set of settings
/// across 80 tasks; TD4's config calls it "a strong stabilizer against value divergence".
pub fn LayerNorm(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        gain: Tensor(T),
        bias: Tensor(T),
        /// Added to each row's variance before the square root, so a row of equal values divides by
        /// sqrt(epsilon), not by zero.
        epsilon: T = 1e-5,

        /// What `attach` hands back: the output, and handles for the two parameters.
        pub const Attached = struct {
            out: Var,
            gain: Var,
            bias: Var,
        };

        /// A layer norm over rows of `width` features, at gain 1 and bias 0 - the plain normalisation to start.
        pub fn init(allocator: Allocator, width: usize) Error!Self {
            const gain: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(allocator, &.{ 1, width });
            errdefer allocator.free(gain.data);
            const bias: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(allocator, &.{ 1, width });
            gain.fill(1);
            bias.fill(0);
            return .{ .gain = gain, .bias = bias };
        }

        /// Record the layer norm onto `graph`, the gain and bias TRAINED.
        pub fn attach(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), input: Var) Error!Attached {
            return self.attachAs(graph, input, .trained);
        }

        /// Record the layer norm onto `graph`, the gain and bias entering as `binding` says (see `Dense.attachAs`).
        pub fn attachAs(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            input: Var,
            binding: Binding,
        ) Error!Attached {
            const gain_value: Var = try bindTensor(T, graph, self.gain, binding);
            const bias_value: Var = try bindTensor(T, graph, self.bias, binding);
            return .{
                .out = try self.applyBound(graph, gain_value, bias_value, input),
                .gain = gain_value,
                .bias = bias_value,
            };
        }

        /// The layer's arithmetic alone, over handles already bound (see `Dense.applyBound`).
        pub fn applyBound(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            gain: Var,
            bias: Var,
            input: Var,
        ) Error!Var {
            // 1. Each row to mean 0 and spread 1.
            const normalised: Var = try graph.layerNormRows(input, self.epsilon);
            // 2. Each feature scaled by its gain - copied to every row first, since `mul` wants equal shapes...
            const row_count: usize = graph.valueOf(input).shape[0];
            const gain_rows: Var = try graph.copyToEveryRow(gain, row_count);
            const scaled: Var = try graph.mul(normalised, gain_rows);
            // 3. ...and shifted by its bias (`add` spreads the bias row itself).
            return graph.add(scaled, bias);
        }
    };
}

The weights start as TD-MPC2's do: normal with a standard deviation of 0.02 for every linear layer, and, for the heads that predict a reward or a value, a last layer of exact zeros, so an untrained model predicts zero rather than noise its targets would then chase.

9.3 Predicting a number as a distribution#

A reward head and a value head each predict one number. Trained with a squared error, whose gradient grows with the error, a task whose returns run into the hundreds takes large, noisy steps where one with returns near 1 takes small ones, and every task needs its own tuning. DreamerV3, TD-MPC2 and BMPC instead have the head output a distribution over fixed bins, trained by cross-entropy, whose gradient — predicted probabilities minus the target's — stays between −1 and 1 whatever the size of the number. One set of settings then serves every task.

Two pieces make it work. Symlog, sign(x) · log(1 + |x|), compresses large magnitudes and leaves small ones nearly unchanged, so bins spread evenly over [−10, 10] in symlog space cover values from about −22000 to 22000 while staying dense near zero. The two-hot target shares the symlogged number between the two bins around it in proportion to closeness: a number a quarter of the way from bin 2 to bin 3 gives three quarters of its weight to bin 2. The split preserves the mean, so reading a prediction back — the probability-weighted mean of the bins, then symexp — returns exactly the number a two-hot encodes.

Twin distributional critics. The same bins on the identity axis carry a distributional critic's return atoms (C51, D4PG, TD4). TD4 keeps two such critics and, per sample, builds its target from the one whose distribution has the lower mean — the distributional form of TD3's minimum. A weight below 1 blends in the other twin along their quantiles rather than mixing their densities: halfway between point masses at 10 and 30 is a single point mass at 20, not two bumps that later backups would smear.

const squashed: f32 = zn.symlog(f32, 1000.0); // about 6.9: large magnitudes pulled in
const bins: zn.CategoricalScalar(f32) = .{}; // 101 bins on [-10, 10], symlog space
var target: [101]f32 = undefined;
try bins.encode(reward, &target);             // the two-hot the head is trained toward
const predicted: f32 = try bins.decode(probabilities); // a prediction read back as a number

// TD4's critic target, per row: the twin whose distribution has the lower mean (weight 1: TD3's minimum).
const returns: zn.CategoricalScalar(f32) = .{ .bin_count = 101, .low = -5, .high = 120, .axis = .identity };
try returns.twinTargetRows(first_twin_probabilities, second_twin_probabilities, 1, target_probabilities);
symlog
/// SYMLOG, the "symmetric logarithm" (DreamerV3, Hafner et al. 2023): sign(x) * log(1 + |x|).
///
/// It squashes large magnitudes and leaves small ones almost alone: its slope at zero is 1, so near zero it is the
/// identity; 10 becomes 2.4, 1000 becomes 6.9, 22025 becomes 10. It is odd, defined for every number, and exactly
/// inverted by `symexp`. Why a learner wants it: a network asked to predict returns of 0.5 in one task and 5000 in
/// another cannot use one scale for both - in symlog space they are 0.41 and 8.5, ordinary numbers for one set of
/// settings (one bin range, one learning rate) across every task. `zm.log1p` keeps it exact for tiny values.
pub fn symlog(comptime T: type, x: T) T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    const squashed_magnitude: T = scalarLog1p(@abs(x));
    return if (x < 0) -squashed_magnitude else squashed_magnitude;
}
CategoricalScalar
/// A SINGLE NUMBER PREDICTED AS A DISTRIBUTION OVER BINS - "regression as classification" (DreamerV3; TD-MPC2; BMPC's
/// reward and value heads).
///
/// A head that must predict one number - a reward, a value - outputs `bin_count` logits instead; the number it
/// predicts is the mean of the softmax over fixed bin positions. It is trained with `softCrossEntropy` against a
/// TWO-HOT target: the true number, shared between the two bins around it (`encode`). Why go the long way: the
/// cross-entropy's gradient is predicted minus target, bounded between -1 and 1 whatever the number's size, where a
