        };

        /// The policy on every row: its centres and log spreads, [H * batch, dims].
        policy: TanhMeanGaussian(T),
        /// The planner's means, [H * batch, dims], step-major - fill, then `recompute`. Zeros until filled.
        target_means: Tensor(T),
        /// The LOGS of the planner's spreads, [H * batch, dims] - fill, then `recompute`. Zeros (a spread of 1) until
        /// filled.
        target_log_stds: Tensor(T),
        /// KL(policy || planner) averaged over the action dimensions, per row, [H * batch, 1] - neither masked nor
        /// scaled. Step 0's rows are what `updateScale` reads.
        divergence: Var,
        /// 1 / the running scale, [1, 1]: 1 until `updateScale`.
        inverse_scale: Tensor(T),
        /// Windows per step - how many of `divergence`'s rows are step 0's.
        batch: usize,
        total: Var,

        /// The policy network applied to `latents` on `graph` (its handles from `Mlp.bind`), the loss built on it.
        /// `unfinished`: one [batch, 1] column per step, 1 for a row still counting and 0 for a finished one - the
        /// rollout's own (`LatentRollout.unfinished`), so that one `setFinished` marks every loss of the window.
        /// The target tensors come from `allocator`.
        pub fn build(
            graph: *Graph(T),
            latents: Var,
            unfinished: []const Tensor(T),
            policy_network: Mlp(T),
            policy_bound: []const Var,
            options: Options,
            allocator: Allocator,
        ) Error!Self {
            const horizon: usize = options.horizon;
            const batch: usize = options.batch;
            if (horizon == 0 or batch == 0 or unfinished.len != horizon) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            for (unfinished) |column| {
                if (column.rank != 2 or column.shape[0] != batch or column.shape[1] != 1) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            const latent_value: Tensor(T) = graph.valueOf(latents);
            if (latent_value.rank != 2 or latent_value.shape[0] != horizon * batch) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            // 1. The policy over every row of the window in one pass, bent into its Gaussian.
            const network_output: Var = try policy_network.apply(graph, policy_bound, latents, null);
            const head_options: TanhMeanGaussian(T).Options = .{
                .log_std_low = options.log_std_low,
                .log_std_high = options.log_std_high,
            };
            const policy: TanhMeanGaussian(T) = try .build(graph, network_output, head_options);
            const dimension_count: usize = graph.valueOf(policy.mean).shape[1];
            const dimension_size: T = @as(T, @floatFromInt(dimension_count));
            // 2. The planner's targets - zeros until filled, which is N(0, 1) - and the scale's reciprocal, 1 to start.
            const target_means: Tensor(T) = try zeros(T, allocator, &.{ horizon * batch, dimension_count });
            errdefer allocator.free(target_means.data);
            const target_log_stds: Tensor(T) = try zeros(T, allocator, &.{ horizon * batch, dimension_count });
            errdefer allocator.free(target_log_stds.data);
            const inverse_scale: Tensor(T) = try ones(T, allocator, &.{ 1, 1 });
            errdefer allocator.free(inverse_scale.data);
            // 3. Per row: the KL from the policy to the planner, averaged over the action dimensions (torch's
            //    choice), and the policy's entropy.
            const summed_divergence: Var = try graph.klGaussianDiag(
                policy.mean,
                policy.log_std,
                target_means,
                target_log_stds,
            );
            const divergence: Var = try graph.scale(summed_divergence, 1 / dimension_size);
            const entropy: Var = try graph.diagGaussianEntropy(policy.log_std);
            // 4. Every row's weight in one column: rho^t / (H * batch) for a row still counting, 0 for a finished one.
            //    Built from the shared `unfinished` columns, so every recompute reads the current flags.
            const row_share: T = 1 / (@as(T, @floatFromInt(horizon)) * @as(T, @floatFromInt(batch)));
            var step_weight: T = 1;
            var row_weights: ?Var = null;
            for (unfinished) |column| {
                const step_rows: Var = try graph.scale(try graph.constant(column), step_weight * row_share);
                row_weights = if (row_weights) |earlier| try graph.concat(earlier, step_rows, 0) else step_rows;
                step_weight *= options.rho;
            }
            const weights_as_a_row: Var = try graph.transpose(row_weights.?);
            // 5. Each term a weighted sum - one dot product - the divergence's divided by the scale, the entropy's
            //    times c * dims; the bonus is subtracted, so a wider policy (where warranted) costs less.
            const weighted_divergence: Var = try graph.matmul(weights_as_a_row, divergence);
            const scaled_divergence: Var = try graph.mul(weighted_divergence, try graph.constant(inverse_scale));
            const weighted_entropy: Var = try graph.matmul(weights_as_a_row, entropy);
            const entropy_bonus: Var = try graph.scale(weighted_entropy, options.entropy_coefficient * dimension_size);
            return .{
                .policy = policy,
                .target_means = target_means,
                .target_log_stds = target_log_stds,
                .divergence = divergence,
                .inverse_scale = inverse_scale,
                .batch = batch,
                .total = try graph.sub(scaled_divergence, entropy_bonus),
            };
        }

        /// Torch's order, after a `recompute`: move `kl_scale` with this batch's FIRST-step divergences (the first
        /// `batch` rows - no row is finished at step 0) and set `inverse_scale` to 1 / its new value. Then `recompute`
        /// for the value, and `backward`. `scratch` holds `batch` values.
        pub fn updateScale(
            self: Self,
            graph: *const Graph(T),
            kl_scale: *RunningScale(T),
            scratch: []T,
        ) Error!void {
            const first_step: []const T = graph.valueOf(self.divergence).data[0..self.batch];
            try kl_scale.update(first_step, scratch);
            self.inverse_scale.data[0] = 1 / kl_scale.value;
        }
    };
}

9.15 The policy's own plans in the search#

Every search also weighs what the policy would do: a handful of each state's candidates are the policy's own plans, rolled through the world model and scored like the others. BMPC trains the policy to imitate these searches (9.14), so when the policy is good its plans are among the elites and the search starts from good ground; when it is not, they lose.

One plan, step by step: encode the observation; at each step the policy draws an action from the latent — its squashed mean plus Gaussian noise of its spread, clamped to [-1, 1] (9.13) — and the dynamics move the latent on:

z_0   = encoder(observation)
a_t   = clamp(tanh(m_t) + s_t * eps_t, -1, 1)     // (m_t, log s_t) = the policy's head at z_t
z_t+1 = dynamics(z_t, a_t)

Each plan of a state draws its own noise, so the plans fan out around the policy's choice. Mppi reserves the first policy_candidates rows of every state's block for them, and LatentPolicyPlans fills those rows; like the scorer, it is a graph built once over frozen handles of the live networks and re-run at every decision.

The plans are built once, beside the scorer:

const plans: zrl.LatentPolicyPlans(f32) = try .build(
    &plans_graph,
    encoder,
    try encoder.bind(&plans_graph, .frozen, arena),
    dynamics,
    try dynamics.bind(&plans_graph, .frozen, arena),
    policy,
    try policy.bind(&plans_graph, .frozen, arena),
    .{ .horizon = 3, .states = 1, .plans = 24, .candidates = 512 }, // BMPC's numbers
    arena,
);
LatentPolicyPlans
/// THE POLICY'S OWN PLANS, put into the planner's search - BMPC's (and TD-MPC2's) `num_pi_trajs`.
///
/// ---- WHY THE PLANNER ASKS THE POLICY ----
///
/// MPPI (`Mppi`) searches by sampling plans around a running Gaussian and moving it toward the best ones. Left alone,
/// it starts each decision from little more than the last decision's plan. The policy, meanwhile, is a network trained
/// to propose good actions in one shot - BMPC trains it to imitate exactly these searches. So every search also weighs
/// what the POLICY would do: a few of the candidate plans are the policy's own, rolled through the world model. They
/// are scored like every other candidate; when the policy is good they are among the elites, and the search starts
/// from good ground. `Mppi` leaves the first `policy_candidates` rows of each state's block to the caller, for them.
///
///     one state's block of Mppi's candidate rows (BMPC: 512, 24 of them the policy's):
///
///       rows 0 .. plans - 1            the POLICY'S PLANS - written here once per decision, fixed for the search
///       rows plans .. candidates - 1   MPPI's samples     - redrawn around its Gaussian every round
///
///     every row is scored every round; the policy's rows just never move.
///
/// ---- ONE PLAN ----
///
///     z_0   = encoder(observation)
///     a_t   = clamp(tanh(m_t) + s_t eps_t, -1, 1)    with (m_t, log s_t) the policy's at z_t,    t = 0 .. H-1
///     z_t+1 = dynamics(z_t, a_t)                                                                   t = 0 .. H-2
///
/// The draw is BMPC's (`TanhMeanGaussian`): the policy's mean squashed into [-1, 1], Gaussian noise of its spread
/// added, the sum clamped - not a squashed sample, so no Jacobian appears anywhere. Each plan of a state gets its own
/// noise, so a state's plans fan out around what the policy would do. The last action is drawn, but the latent after
/// it is not needed: the scorer (`LatentPlanScorer`) moves the latents itself when it scores the plan. Reanalyze draws
/// with a wider spread - log s in [-2, 1] instead of [-3, 1] - which `Options.spread` holds.
///
/// ---- BUILT ONCE, RE-RUN AT EVERY DECISION ----
///
/// Like the scorer, the graph is built once over FROZEN handles of the live networks and re-run at every decision:
/// the observations, the current latents and the current actions are tensors the graph reads, rewritten before each
/// `recompute`. The draw happens outside the graph, because each step's action feeds the next step's dynamics: a
/// decision is one recompute for the encoder, then per step one for the policy and - but for the last step - one for
/// the dynamics, 2H in all. The noise is the counter-based `Rng`'s, indexed by (state, plan, step, dimension), so a
/// decision is reproducible from its seed. `allocator` should be an arena, as for the scorer: nothing here is freed
/// on its own.
pub fn LatentPolicyPlans(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        pub const Options = struct {
            horizon: usize,
            states: usize,
            /// Policy plans per state - `Mppi.Options.policy_candidates`.
            plans: usize,
            /// Candidates per state - `Mppi.Options.candidates`: the stride of the action rows the plans go into.
            candidates: usize,
            /// The policy's spread range: [-3, 1] while acting, [-2, 1] for reanalyze's wider exploration.
            spread: TanhMeanGaussian(T).Options = .{},
        };

        options: Options,
        action_width: usize,
        /// This decision's observations, one row per state - the caller writes them before `fill`.
        observations: Tensor(T),
        /// The current step's latents and actions, row `state * plans + plan`.
        latents: Tensor(T),
        actions: Tensor(T),
        start_latents: Var,
        mean: Var,
        log_std: Var,
        next_latents: Var,

        pub fn build(
            graph: *Graph(T),
            encoder: Mlp(T),
            encoder_bound: []const Var,
            dynamics: Mlp(T),
            dynamics_bound: []const Var,
            policy: Mlp(T),
            policy_bound: []const Var,
            options: Options,
            allocator: Allocator,
        ) Error!Self {
            const latent_width: usize = encoder.spec.output;
            const action_width: usize = policy.spec.output / 2;
            const shapes_agree: bool = policy.spec.output == 2 * action_width and policy.spec.input == latent_width and
                dynamics.spec.input == latent_width + action_width and dynamics.spec.output == latent_width;
            const counts_valid: bool = options.horizon > 0 and options.states > 0 and options.plans > 0 and
                options.plans <= options.candidates and action_width > 0;
            if (!shapes_agree or !counts_valid) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const row_count: usize = options.states * options.plans;
            const observations: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(allocator, &.{ options.states, encoder.spec.input });
            errdefer allocator.free(observations.data);
            observations.fill(0);
            const latents: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(allocator, &.{ row_count, latent_width });
            errdefer allocator.free(latents.data);
            latents.fill(0);
            const actions: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(allocator, &.{ row_count, action_width });
            errdefer allocator.free(actions.data);
            actions.fill(0);
            // Each state's latent copied to its plans' rows by a product with a 0/1 matrix - exact, the scorer's way.
            const copy_to_plans: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(allocator, &.{ row_count, options.states });
            errdefer allocator.free(copy_to_plans.data);
            copy_to_plans.fill(0);
            for (0..row_count) |row| {
                copy_to_plans.setAt2(row, row / options.plans, 1);
            }
            const observations_in: Var = try graph.constant(observations);
            const encoded_states: Var = try encoder.apply(graph, encoder_bound, observations_in, null);
            const start_latents: Var = try graph.matmul(try graph.constant(copy_to_plans), encoded_states);
            const latents_in: Var = try graph.constant(latents);
            const policy_output: Var = try policy.apply(graph, policy_bound, latents_in, null);
            const policy_gaussian: TanhMeanGaussian(T) = try .build(graph, policy_output, options.spread);
            const latent_with_action: Var = try graph.concat(latents_in, try graph.constant(actions), 1);
            const next_latents: Var = try dynamics.apply(graph, dynamics_bound, latent_with_action, null);
            return .{
                .options = options,
                .action_width = action_width,
                .observations = observations,
                .latents = latents,
                .actions = actions,
                .start_latents = start_latents,
                .mean = policy_gaussian.mean,
                .log_std = policy_gaussian.log_std,
                .next_latents = next_latents,
            };
        }

        /// The policy's plans for every state, written into `plan_actions` - `Mppi`'s per-step action tensors, the
        /// rows `state * candidates + plan` for each state's first `plans` candidates; every other row is left alone.
        pub fn fill(
            self: Self,
            graph: *Graph(T),
            rng: Rng,
            plan_actions: []const Tensor(T),
        ) Error!void {
            const options: Options = self.options;
            if (plan_actions.len != options.horizon) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            for (plan_actions) |step_actions| {
                const shape_ok: bool = step_actions.rank == 2 and
                    step_actions.shape[0] == options.states * options.candidates and
                    step_actions.shape[1] == self.action_width;
                if (!shape_ok) {
                    return Error.ShapeMismatch;
                }
            }
            // Where every plan starts: its state's encoding.
            try graph.recompute();
            @memcpy(self.latents.data, graph.valueOf(self.start_latents).data);
            for (plan_actions, 0..) |step_actions, step| {
                // The policy at this step's latents: a squashed mean and a log spread per row and dimension.
                try graph.recompute();
                const means: []const T = graph.valueOf(self.mean).data;
                const log_spreads: []const T = graph.valueOf(self.log_std).data;
                for (0..options.states) |state| {
                    const state_rng: Rng = rng.split(@intCast(state));
                    for (0..options.plans) |plan| {
                        const row: usize = state * options.plans + plan;
                        for (0..self.action_width) |dimension| {
                            const at: usize = row * self.action_width + dimension;
                            const noise_index: u32 = @intCast((plan * options.horizon + step) * self.action_width +
                                dimension);
                            const noise: T = state_rng.normal(T, noise_index);
                            const action: T = TanhMeanGaussian(T).draw(means[at], log_spreads[at], noise);
                            self.actions.data[at] = action;
                            step_actions.setAt2(state * options.candidates + plan, dimension, action);
                        }
                    }
                }
                // The latents one step on - not after the last step: the scorer moves them itself.
                if (step + 1 < options.horizon) {
                    try graph.recompute();
                    @memcpy(self.latents.data, graph.valueOf(self.next_latents).data);
                }
            }
        }
    };
}

At one decision the plans are written once, then MPPI's rounds sample the rest, score everything and refit:

const actions: []Tensor(f32) = scorer.actions; // the rows MPPI searches, and the scorer reads
@memcpy(scorer.observations.data, observation_rows);
@memcpy(plans.observations.data, observation_rows);
try mppi.begin(&warm); // last decision's plan shifted one step - zeros where an episode starts
try plans.fill(&plans_graph, rng.split(0), actions); // the policy's rows: fixed for the whole search
for (0..mppi.options.iterations) |iteration| {
    try mppi.sample(actions, rng.split(1), iteration); // every other row: mean + spread x noise
    try scorer.score(&scorer_graph, scores); // the model's imagination
    try mppi.refit(actions, scores);
}
try mppi.choose(actions, .draw_elite, rng.split(2), rng.split(3), first_actions, first_spreads);

9.16 What a stored step is worth: the value targets#

The value heads predict how much a state is worth: the rewards still to come, each discounted by how far ahead it lies. Their target for a stored step is built by the world model itself. It starts from the step's true latent — the encoder's reading of the stored observation, not a rolled-out guess — lets the policy act a step or two, adds up the rewards the model predicts along the way, and closes with the value where the policy leads:

z_0    = encoder(observation)
a_k    = the policy's draw at z_k        // clamp(tanh m + s eps, -1, 1), as in 9.15
target = r_0 + gamma r_1 + ... + gamma^h V_target(z_h)
         // r_k = reward(z_k, a_k), z_k+1 = dynamics(z_k, a_k); BMPC looks one step ahead: h = 1

Three choices make this the right target. The policy, not the action that was actually taken: the planner ends every plan by handing the rest of the future to the policy (the last term of a plan's score is the value at its end), so what it needs is a state's worth under the agent's current behaviour. The model, not the stored reward: the target is the model's own consistent estimate and never asks the replay what came next. The target heads, not the value heads: V_target is the average of slow copies that follow the value heads a little at each update, so a target never chases the very network it is training.

Like the scorer and the policy's plans, the targets are a graph built once over frozen handles of the live networks — one row per stored step of a batch — and re-run at every update:

const targets: zrl.LatentValueTargets(f32) = try .build(
    &targets_graph,
    encoder,
    try encoder.bind(&targets_graph, .frozen, arena),
    policy,
    try policy.bind(&targets_graph, .frozen, arena),
    dynamics,
    try dynamics.bind(&targets_graph, .frozen, arena),
    reward_head,
    try reward_head.bind(&targets_graph, .frozen, arena),
    target_value_heads, // the slow copies, not the heads being trained
    target_value_bounds,
    .{ .rows = windows * horizon, .discount = discount },
    arena,
);
// Every update: the batch's stored observations in, one target per stored step out.
@memcpy(targets.observations.data, stored_observations);
try targets.compute(&targets_graph, rng, value_targets);
LatentValueTargets
/// THE VALUE A STORED STEP IS WORTH - THE TARGET THE VALUE HEADS LEARN (BMPC's `_td_target_V`).
///
/// ---- WHAT THE VALUE HEADS ARE ASKED TO PREDICT ----
///
/// A value head predicts how much a state is worth: the rewards still to come, each discounted by how far ahead it
/// lies. Its target for a stored step is built by the world model itself, starting from that step's TRUE latent -
/// the encoder's reading of the stored observation, not a rolled-out guess - and looking `lookahead` steps ahead
/// along the POLICY:
///
///     z_0    = encoder(observation)
///     a_k    = the policy's draw at z_k            (TanhMeanGaussian.draw: clamp(tanh m + s eps, -1, 1))
///     r_k    = reward(z_k, a_k)                    (read back from the reward head's bins)
///     z_k+1  = dynamics(z_k, a_k)
///     target = r_0 + gamma r_1 + ... + gamma^(h-1) r_(h-1) + gamma^h V_target(z_h)
///
/// V_target is the TARGET value heads' prediction, averaged over the heads: the slow copies that follow the value
/// heads (`Params.follow`, BMPC's tau 0.01), so a target never chases the very network it trains. BMPC looks one
/// step ahead (h = 1): one reward, then the discounted value of where the policy leads.
///
/// WORKED THROUGH (h = 1, gamma = 0.9): the policy draws a_0 at the true latent z_0; the reward head reads r_0 = 0.5;
/// the dynamics move on to z_1; the two target heads say 2.0 and 3.0 there, so V_target = 2.5; the target is
/// 0.5 + 0.9 x 2.5 = 2.75.
///
/// WHY THE POLICY, NOT THE ACTION ACTUALLY TAKEN: the planner ends every plan by handing the rest of the future to
/// the policy (its score's last term is V at the plan's end), so what it needs is the worth of a state under the
/// agent's CURRENT behaviour - and the target follows the policy to measure exactly that. WHY THE MODEL, NOT THE
/// STORED REWARD: the target is the model's own consistent estimate; it never asks the replay what happened next.
///
/// NO EPISODE ENDS INSIDE A TARGET: BMPC's model does not predict terminations, and its tasks only truncate. A task
/// that terminates needs a continue head (not built yet); the row's `terminated` column waits for it.
///
/// ---- BUILT ONCE, RE-RUN AT EVERY UPDATE ----
///
/// Like `LatentPlanScorer` and `LatentPolicyPlans`: one graph over FROZEN handles of the live networks - no
/// gradient ever flows into a target - built for a fixed number of rows (one per stored step of a batch: windows
/// x steps) and re-run with new observations. Each step's action feeds the next step's dynamics, so the policy's
/// draws happen between recomputes: a target costs 2h + 2 of them. The noise is the counter-based `Rng`'s, indexed
/// by (row, step, dimension), so a batch's targets are reproducible from the seed. `allocator` should be an arena.
///
/// ---- THROUGH EVERY DYNAMICS HEAD ----
///
/// With an ensemble of dynamics heads (EfficientTDMPC), the target averages the lookahead over the heads - its eq. 14,
/// for one step:
///
///     target = R(z, a) + gamma x (1 / heads) x sum over heads i of V_target(f_i(z, a)),      a drawn at z
///
/// Each head follows its own chain of latents. The chains are stacked along the rows - block i, rows i x rows ..
/// (i + 1) x rows, is head i's - so the policy, the reward head and the target heads read all of them in one pass,
/// and each dynamics head steps only its own block. Every block starts from the same true latent and draws with the
/// SAME noise for a stored step, so at the first step every head sees the same action a: the reward R(z, a), read at
/// the true latent, is the same in every block, and only the value where each head leads differs. Each head's target
/// is accumulated on its own and the heads' are averaged at the end - summed in order, then scaled once, so heads
/// that agree give exactly the one-head target. With one head nothing is stacked.
pub fn LatentValueTargets(comptime T: type) type {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    return struct {
        const Self = @This();

        pub const Options = struct {
            /// One per stored step the batch holds: windows x steps.
            rows: usize,
            /// How many policy steps the target looks ahead - BMPC's td_horizon, 1.
            lookahead: usize = 1,
            discount: T,
            /// The reward's and the values' bins - the same as the heads were built with.
            bins: CategoricalScalar(T) = .{},
            /// The policy's spread range: the acting one, [-3, 1].
            spread: TanhMeanGaussian(T).Options = .{},
        };

        options: Options,
        action_width: usize,
        /// How many dynamics heads the lookahead runs through: the stacked rows are `dynamics_head_count x rows`.
        dynamics_head_count: usize,
        /// The stored steps' observations, one row each - the caller writes them before `compute`.
        observations: Tensor(T),
        /// The latents and actions of the step being looked at, for every head's chain: block i is head i's.
        latents: Tensor(T),
        actions: Tensor(T),
        start_latents: Var,
        mean: Var,
        log_std: Var,
        reward_probabilities: Var,
        next_latents: Var,
        /// One per target value head, read at `latents`.
        value_probabilities: []Var,
        /// Scratch: one decoded number per stacked row.
        decoded_row_values: []T,
        /// Per stacked row: its head's target so far.
        head_targets: []T,

        pub fn build(
            graph: *Graph(T),
            encoder: Mlp(T),
            encoder_bound: []const Var,
            policy: Mlp(T),
            policy_bound: []const Var,
            dynamics_heads: []const Mlp(T),
            dynamics_bounds: []const []const Var,
            reward_head: Mlp(T),
            reward_bound: []const Var,
            target_value_heads: []const Mlp(T),
            target_value_bounds: []const []const Var,
            options: Options,
            allocator: Allocator,
        ) Error!Self {
            try options.bins.check();
            const latent_width: usize = encoder.spec.output;
            const action_width: usize = policy.spec.output / 2;
            const bin_count: usize = options.bins.bin_count;
            const dynamics_head_count: usize = dynamics_heads.len;
            const policy_fits: bool = policy.spec.input == latent_width and policy.spec.output == 2 * action_width;
            var dynamics_fit: bool = dynamics_head_count > 0 and dynamics_bounds.len == dynamics_head_count;
            for (dynamics_heads) |dynamics| {
                dynamics_fit = dynamics_fit and dynamics.spec.input == latent_width + action_width and
                    dynamics.spec.output == latent_width;
            }
            const reward_fits: bool = reward_head.spec.input == latent_width + action_width and
                reward_head.spec.output == bin_count;
            var heads_fit: bool = target_value_heads.len > 0 and target_value_bounds.len == target_value_heads.len;
            for (target_value_heads) |head| {
                heads_fit = heads_fit and head.spec.input == latent_width and head.spec.output == bin_count;
            }
            const counts_valid: bool = options.rows > 0 and options.lookahead > 0 and action_width > 0;
            if (!policy_fits or !dynamics_fit or !reward_fits or !heads_fit or !counts_valid) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const stacked_rows: usize = dynamics_head_count * options.rows;
            const observations: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(allocator, &.{ options.rows, encoder.spec.input });
            errdefer allocator.free(observations.data);
            observations.fill(0);
            const latents: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(allocator, &.{ stacked_rows, latent_width });
            errdefer allocator.free(latents.data);
            latents.fill(0);
            const actions: Tensor(T) = try .alloc(allocator, &.{ stacked_rows, action_width });
            errdefer allocator.free(actions.data);
            actions.fill(0);
            const value_probabilities: []Var = try allocator.alloc(Var, target_value_heads.len);
            errdefer allocator.free(value_probabilities);
            const decoded_row_values: []T = try allocator.alloc(T, stacked_rows);
            errdefer allocator.free(decoded_row_values);
            const head_targets: []T = try allocator.alloc(T, stacked_rows);
            errdefer allocator.free(head_targets);
            // Where every target starts: the stored step's TRUE latent.
            const observations_in: Var = try graph.constant(observations);
            const start_latents: Var = try encoder.apply(graph, encoder_bound, observations_in, null);
            // The policy, the reward and the dynamics at the step being looked at - every head's chain at once.
            const latents_in: Var = try graph.constant(latents);
            const policy_output: Var = try policy.apply(graph, policy_bound, latents_in, null);
            const policy_gaussian: TanhMeanGaussian(T) = try .build(graph, policy_output, options.spread);
            const latent_with_action: Var = try graph.concat(latents_in, try graph.constant(actions), 1);
            const reward_logits: Var = try reward_head.apply(graph, reward_bound, latent_with_action, null);
            const reward_probabilities: Var = try graph.softmaxRows(reward_logits);
            // Each dynamics head steps its own block of rows; with one head the whole is its block.
            var next_latents: Var = undefined;
            if (dynamics_head_count == 1) {
                next_latents = try dynamics_heads[0].apply(graph, dynamics_bounds[0], latent_with_action, null);
            } else {
                for (dynamics_heads, dynamics_bounds, 0..) |dynamics_head, dynamics_head_bound, head_block| {
                    const block_first_row: usize = head_block * options.rows;
                    const head_block_inputs: Var = try graph.slice(
                        latent_with_action,
                        0,
                        block_first_row,
                        options.rows,
                    );
                    const head_block_next_latents: Var = try dynamics_head.apply(
                        graph,
                        dynamics_head_bound,
                        head_block_inputs,
                        null,
                    );
                    next_latents = if (head_block == 0)
                        head_block_next_latents
                    else
                        try graph.concat(next_latents, head_block_next_latents, 0);
                }
            }
            // The target heads' verdict on the same latents - read once the lookahead has moved them to its end.
            for (target_value_heads, target_value_bounds, value_probabilities) |value_head, bound, *probabilities| {
                const value_logits: Var = try value_head.apply(graph, bound, latents_in, null);
                probabilities.* = try graph.softmaxRows(value_logits);
            }
            return .{
                .options = options,
                .action_width = action_width,
                .dynamics_head_count = dynamics_head_count,
                .observations = observations,
                .latents = latents,
                .actions = actions,
                .start_latents = start_latents,
                .mean = policy_gaussian.mean,
                .log_std = policy_gaussian.log_std,
                .reward_probabilities = reward_probabilities,
                .next_latents = next_latents,
                .value_probabilities = value_probabilities,
                .decoded_row_values = decoded_row_values,
                .head_targets = head_targets,
            };
        }

        /// One target per row into `targets`, from the observations the caller wrote into `observations`.
        pub fn compute(self: Self, graph: *Graph(T), rng: Rng, targets: []T) Error!void {
            const options: Options = self.options;
            if (targets.len != options.rows) {
                return Error.ShapeMismatch;
            }
            const rows: usize = options.rows;
            const latent_width: usize = self.latents.shape[1];
            const numbers_per_block: usize = rows * latent_width;
            @memset(self.head_targets, 0);
            // Every head's chain starts from its stored step's true latent.
            try graph.recompute();
            const true_latents: []const T = graph.valueOf(self.start_latents).data;
            for (0..self.dynamics_head_count) |head_block| {
                const block_latents: []T = self.latents.data[head_block * numbers_per_block ..][0..numbers_per_block];
                @memcpy(block_latents, true_latents);
            }
            var step_discount: T = 1;
            for (0..options.lookahead) |step| {
                // The policy's draw at this step's latents - the same noise for a stored step in every head's
                // chain, so at the first step, where every chain stands on the true latent, every head draws the same
                // action...
                try graph.recompute();
                const means: []const T = graph.valueOf(self.mean).data;
                const log_spreads: []const T = graph.valueOf(self.log_std).data;
                for (0..self.dynamics_head_count * rows) |stacked_row| {
                    const stored_row: usize = stacked_row % rows;
                    const row_rng: Rng = rng.split(@intCast(stored_row));
                    for (0..self.action_width) |dimension| {
                        const at: usize = stacked_row * self.action_width + dimension;
                        const noise_index: u32 = @intCast(step * self.action_width + dimension);
                        const noise: T = row_rng.normal(T, noise_index);
                        self.actions.data[at] = TanhMeanGaussian(T).draw(means[at], log_spreads[at], noise);
                    }
                }
                // ...then the reward it earns, discounted, and the latent each head leads it to.
                try graph.recompute();
                try options.bins.decodeRows(graph.valueOf(self.reward_probabilities), self.decoded_row_values);
                for (self.head_targets, self.decoded_row_values) |*head_target, reward| {
                    head_target.* += step_discount * reward;
                }
                @memcpy(self.latents.data, graph.valueOf(self.next_latents).data);
                step_discount *= options.discount;
            }
            // Where the lookahead ends: the target heads' value, discounted by gamma^h and averaged over the heads.
            try graph.recompute();
            const value_head_divisor: T = @floatFromInt(self.value_probabilities.len);
            const share_per_value_head: T = step_discount / value_head_divisor;
            for (self.value_probabilities) |probabilities| {
                try options.bins.decodeRows(graph.valueOf(probabilities), self.decoded_row_values);
                for (self.head_targets, self.decoded_row_values) |*head_target, value| {
                    head_target.* += share_per_value_head * value;
                }
            }
            // The dynamics heads' targets averaged per stored step (eq. 14): summed head by head, then scaled once.
            if (self.dynamics_head_count == 1) {
                @memcpy(targets, self.head_targets);
                return;
            }
            const share_per_dynamics_head: T = 1 / @as(T, @floatFromInt(self.dynamics_head_count));
            for (targets, 0..) |*target, stored_row| {
                var sum_over_heads: T = self.head_targets[stored_row];
                for (1..self.dynamics_head_count) |head_block| {
                    const head_block_target: T = self.head_targets[head_block * rows + stored_row];
                    sum_over_heads += head_block_target;
                }
                target.* = sum_over_heads * share_per_dynamics_head;
            }
        }
    };
}

9.17 One decision, assembled#

Everything in this chapter meets in one call. robot_bmpc.zig assembles BMPC from these pieces: its Actor holds the planner (Mppi, 9.8), the scorer (9.7) and the policy's plans (9.15), each built once over frozen handles of the live networks, and act makes one decision for every character at once, in three phases.

1. The policy's plans. The policy rolls its own plans through the model into the first rows of every state's block of candidates. They stay put for the whole search: the planner always weighs what the policy would do.

2. The search, without noise. Mppi.plan starts each state's Gaussian from its last plan shifted by one step — the first step has already been taken, so it is dropped, and the new last step starts at zero; where an episode has just begun, everything starts at zero. Only the mean carries over: the spread starts at its ceiling, 2, at every decision, so a search that ended confident does not begin the next one narrow. Then come the rounds of 9.8 — six of them, or eight once the action has twenty dimensions or more (PlannerConfig.iterationsFor), since a larger action space takes more refits to settle — and finally one elite drawn by its weight. What comes back is the target: that elite's first action and the first step's spread. It is computed without exploration noise on purpose: it is what the replay keeps for the policy to imitate (9.14) — the planner's answer, not a random wobble around it.

3. What is acted on. While training, Mppi.explore jitters the target by its spread times a fresh noise draw and clamps it to the range: that is BMPC's exploration, wide where the search was unsure and narrow where it was confident. When evaluating, the target itself is acted on.

@memcpy(self.scorer.observations.data, observations);
@memcpy(self.plans.observations.data, observations);
// 1. The policy's own plans into each state's first candidate rows - fixed for the whole search.
try self.plans.fill(self.plans_graph, rng.split(0), self.scorer.actions);
// 2. MPPI's search, scored by imagination, warm-started but where an episode begins. Noise-free:
//    what comes back is the TARGET - the drawn elite's first action, and the first step's spread.
for (self.warm, first_steps) |*warm, first_step| {
    warm.* = !first_step;
}
try self.mppi.plan(
    self.scorer,
    self.scorer_graph,
    self.warm,
    .draw_elite,
    rng.split(1),
    null,
    self.scores,
    decision.target_means,
    decision.target_spreads,
);
// 3. What is acted on: the target jittered while training, the target itself when evaluating.
switch (mode) {
    .explore => try self.mppi.explore(
        decision.target_means,
        decision.target_spreads,
        rng.split(2),
        decision.actions,
    ),
    .evaluate => @memcpy(decision.actions, decision.target_means),
}

One random stream feeds the whole decision, split three ways — the plans, the search, the exploration — so a decision is reproducible from its seed, and evaluating from the same seed acts on exactly the target that exploring kept. What the replay then stores for this step (9.9) is the observation, the action actually taken (the explored one: it is what the environment saw), the reward, the true next observation, and the target.

9.18 One update, assembled: the world model learns#

Learning mirrors acting: everything meets in one call. robot_bmpc.zig's WorldModelLearner binds the world model trained on a graph of its own (its gradients are what the update is for), the value targets on another, frozen, and update runs the pipeline of 9.6 on stretches of remembered play.

Laying out the windows. The replay hands back W windows of H rows each (9.9). The rollout wants H + 1 blocks of observations: block 0 is encoded as the starting latent, and each later block is what the rollout's prediction for the step before it is checked against. So block 0 is each window's first observation, and block t + 1 is step t's next observation, read from step t's own row. The actions go in one block per step, the rewards become two-hot targets for the reward head, and every stored step's own observation starts its value target.

Which latents each term reads. Two sets of latents meet in the loss. The true latents are the encoder's readings of the stored observations, and the value targets start from them (9.16), so a target is the model's estimate of a real state. The rolled latents are what the dynamics predict from the window's first encoding, and the reward and value heads read those. At planning time the model only ever rolls forward from one encoding, so training the heads on rolled latents — value-equivalent training — teaches them to read the states the model actually reaches, its own errors included. Consistency ties the lanes together, step by step.

The update itself is five steps. The value targets come first, from the target heads, frozen (9.16). Then the forward pass — the rollout and the loss, every step weighted by rhot and every finished step masked (9.4, 9.5) — and the backward one. Then one clip over every parameter at once: if the gradient's global norm is above 20, every gradient is scaled by the same factor, so the update keeps its direction and only gets shorter. Then two Adams: the encoder moves at 0.3 of the rate of everything else, because everything downstream reads its latents. Last, the target heads follow the value heads one percent of the way.

Adam's first step deserves its example. It divides each step by the gradient's own running size, so on the very first update every parameter moves by about the rate, whatever its gradient — 3e-4 for the dynamics and the heads, 9e-5 for the encoder. Small and large gradients alike take one rate-sized step; it is the following updates that let consistent gradients keep moving and noisy ones cancel out.

// 1. W windows of H rows, step-major, and which of their steps are finished.
try replay.sample(rng.split(0), update_index, horizon, self.origins, self.window_rows, self.finished);
// 2. The rollout's inputs and the reward targets.
try self.loadWindows();
// 3. The value targets - frozen, from each stored step's true latent - two-hot, step by step.
try self.targets.compute(self.targets_graph, rng.split(1), self.value_target_values);
for (0..horizon) |step| {
    const step_targets: []const T = self.value_target_values[step * windows ..][0..windows];
    try self.config.bins.encodeRows(step_targets, self.loss.value_targets[step]);
}
// 4. The masks, fresh dropout, the forward pass and the backward one.
try self.rollout.setFinished(self.finished);
self.graph.resampleDropout(rng.split(2));
try self.graph.recompute();
try self.graph.backward(self.loss.total);
// 5. One clip over everything, the two Adams, and the target heads' slow follow.
const clip_limit: T = self.config.gradient_clip;
const gradient_norm: T = try zn.clipGradNorm(T, self.graph, self.model_parameters, clip_limit);
try self.encoder_optimizer.step(self.graph);
try self.model_optimizer.step(self.graph);
for (self.target_value_numbers, self.online_value_numbers) |target_numbers, online_numbers| {
    try target_numbers.follow(online_numbers, self.config.target_follow);
}
clipGradNorm
/// Scale every gradient down together if their combined norm exceeds `max_norm`.
///
/// Returns the norm BEFORE clipping, which is the number worth logging: a spike in it is how you
/// find out a batch was bad, and clipping silently would hide exactly that.
///
/// ONE NORM ACROSS ALL PARAMETERS, NOT ONE PER TENSOR
///
/// This is the part that is easy to get wrong and hard to notice. Clipping each tensor to its own
/// limit **changes the direction of the update**: a layer whose gradient was twice its neighbour's
/// comes out the same size, so the step now points somewhere the loss surface never suggested.
///
/// Scaling everything by one factor keeps the direction exactly and shortens the step, which is
/// what "clip the gradient" is supposed to mean. The test asserts the direction survives, because
/// a per-tensor version passes every test about magnitude.
///
/// WHY THIS MATTERS MORE THAN THE OPTIMISER YOU PICK
///
/// A transformer meets a bad batch, the loss spikes, and one enormous update moves the weights
/// somewhere the model never recovers from. Adam does not save you - it normalises by a RUNNING
/// average, so a single outlier is still an outlier. Clipping is the thing that does.
pub fn clipGradNorm(
    comptime T: type,
    graph: *const Graph(T),
    parameters: []const Var,
    max_norm: T,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (max_norm <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var squares: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    for (parameters) |parameter| {
        const slopes: Tensor(T) = try graph.gradOf(parameter);
        for (0..slopes.size()) |i| {
            const g: T = slopes.data[i];
            squares.add(g * g);
        }
    }
    const total: T = @sqrt(squares.value());
    if (total <= max_norm) {
        return total;
    }
    // A SINGLE FACTOR, APPLIED TO EVERYTHING - that is what keeps the direction.
    const factor: T = max_norm / total;
    for (parameters) |parameter| {
        const slopes: Tensor(T) = try graph.gradOf(parameter);
        for (0..slopes.size()) |i| {
            slopes.data[i] *= factor;
        }
    }
    return total;
}

9.19 The policy update, and the whole update#

An update has two parts. The world model learns first, from the sampled windows (9.18). The policy then learns from the same batch: PolicyLearner moves the policy toward the planner's stored answers (9.14). It has its own graph and its own optimiser, so neither part changes the other's networks. Learner.update runs both.

The latents. The policy is trained at z0 … zH−1, the latents computed by the world model's rollout in this update. During planning, the policy's plans are evaluated inside the model (9.15), so the policy is trained on latents that the model produces. They are copied as values, and no gradient from the policy's loss reaches the world model. By the time of the copy, the world model's optimiser has changed its weights, but the values computed by the forward pass are unchanged.

The targets. Each row stores the Gaussian the planner ended with when the step was played (9.9, 9.17). The means are used as stored, and the spreads are converted to logarithms, the form the KL uses: a stored spread of 0.3 enters as log 0.3 = −1.20, and the empty target of a seeding step, with spread 1, enters as 0. The KL is divided by a running scale (9.12), which this batch updates first, so the loss keeps a steady size while the planner and the policy change. The policy's gradients are then clipped at 20 and its Adam optimiser takes one step. Its epsilon of 1e-5, larger than the world model's 1e-8, damps the steps taken on very small gradients.

// 1. The latents z_0 .. z_(H-1), copied as values from the world model's forward pass.
for (0..horizon) |step| {
    const rolled: []const T = world_model.graph.valueOf(world_model.rollout.rolled[step]).data;
    const step_latents: []T = self.latents.data[step * latents_per_step ..][0..latents_per_step];
    @memcpy(step_latents, rolled);
}
// 2. The planner's targets from the same rows: the means as stored, the spreads as logarithms.
for (0..horizon * windows) |row_index| {
    const stored_row: []const T = rowOf(world_model.window_rows, row_index, row_width);
    const stored_means: []const T = stored_row[self.row.targetMeanStart()..][0..action_width];
    const stored_spreads: []const T = stored_row[self.row.targetSpreadStart()..][0..action_width];
    @memcpy(rowOf(self.loss.target_means.data, row_index, action_width), stored_means);
    const target_log_stds: []T = rowOf(self.loss.target_log_stds.data, row_index, action_width);
    for (target_log_stds, stored_spreads) |*log_std, spread| {
        log_std.* = @log(spread);
    }
}
// 3. The scale is updated from this batch first; the loss is then recomputed with it and
// differentiated.
try self.graph.recompute();
try self.loss.updateScale(self.graph, &self.kl_scale, self.scratch);
try self.graph.recompute();
try self.graph.backward(self.loss.total);
// 4. The policy's gradients are clipped, and its optimiser takes one step.
const clip_limit: T = self.config.gradient_clip;
const gradient_norm: T = try zn.clipGradNorm(T, self.graph, self.parameters, clip_limit);
try self.optimizer.step(self.graph);
clipGradNorm
/// Scale every gradient down together if their combined norm exceeds `max_norm`.
///
/// Returns the norm BEFORE clipping, which is the number worth logging: a spike in it is how you
/// find out a batch was bad, and clipping silently would hide exactly that.
///
/// ONE NORM ACROSS ALL PARAMETERS, NOT ONE PER TENSOR
///
/// This is the part that is easy to get wrong and hard to notice. Clipping each tensor to its own
/// limit **changes the direction of the update**: a layer whose gradient was twice its neighbour's
/// comes out the same size, so the step now points somewhere the loss surface never suggested.
///
/// Scaling everything by one factor keeps the direction exactly and shortens the step, which is
/// what "clip the gradient" is supposed to mean. The test asserts the direction survives, because
/// a per-tensor version passes every test about magnitude.
///
/// WHY THIS MATTERS MORE THAN THE OPTIMISER YOU PICK
///
/// A transformer meets a bad batch, the loss spikes, and one enormous update moves the weights
/// somewhere the model never recovers from. Adam does not save you - it normalises by a RUNNING
/// average, so a single outlier is still an outlier. Clipping is the thing that does.
pub fn clipGradNorm(
    comptime T: type,
    graph: *const Graph(T),
    parameters: []const Var,
    max_norm: T,
) Error!T {
    comptime requireFloat(T);
    if (max_norm <= 0) {
        return Error.DomainError;
    }
    var squares: CompensatedSum(T) = .{};
    for (parameters) |parameter| {
        const slopes: Tensor(T) = try graph.gradOf(parameter);
        for (0..slopes.size()) |i| {
            const g: T = slopes.data[i];
            squares.add(g * g);
        }
    }
    const total: T = @sqrt(squares.value());
    if (total <= max_norm) {
        return total;
    }
    // A SINGLE FACTOR, APPLIED TO EVERYTHING - that is what keeps the direction.
    const factor: T = max_norm / total;
    for (parameters) |parameter| {
        const slopes: Tensor(T) = try graph.gradOf(parameter);
        for (0..slopes.size()) |i| {
            slopes.data[i] *= factor;
        }
    }
    return total;
}

Pretraining. BMPC ends its seeding phase with a burst of updates, made before the stored targets carry information. During those updates only the world model learns:

const world_model_report: WorldModelReport = try self.world_model.learn(rng);
const world_model: *const WorldModelLearner = &self.world_model;
const policy_report: ?PolicyReport = if (pretraining) null else try self.policy.update(world_model);

The discount. BMPC derives the discount from the episode's length. A discount γ = (n − 1) / n weighs rewards over an effective horizon of about n = 1 / (1 − γ) steps. BMPC sets that horizon to a fifth of the episode, n = L / 5, and clamps the discount to [0.95, 0.995], which keeps the horizon between 20 and 200 steps. Bmpc(T).discountFor(L) computes it, and a task stores the result in Config.discount.

9.20 Reanalyze: planning again at stored states#

Each row of the replay stores the answer the planner gave when that step was played (9.9), computed with the networks of that time. The networks improve afterwards, so older targets go out of date, and the policy, which imitates them (9.14), would keep learning outdated answers. Reanalyze plans again: on every tenth update outside pretraining, BMPC takes the first 20 windows of the sampled batch and plans at each of their stored states with the current networks. The new targets replace the old ones in the replay, for later updates, and in the batch, for the policy update of the same update.

Reanalyze as a decision. Planning at H × R stored states is the same computation as acting for a batch of characters (9.17), with three settings: every state is treated as an episode's first step, since a stored state does not continue a previous decision; the decision is made in evaluation mode, since only the target is kept; and the policy's plans are drawn wider (log std at least −2 rather than −3), so the search considers actions further from the policy's proposal. Reanalyzer is an Actor built with these settings.

Order within an update. Reanalyze runs after sampling and before any learning, so the planner uses the networks as they are at the start of the update, and the policy update reads the new targets. Pretraining updates neither reanalyze nor count toward the interval:

try self.world_model.sample(replay, rng, update_index);
if (!pretraining) {
    self.updates += 1;
}
const interval: usize = self.config.reanalyze_interval;
const reanalyzing: bool = !pretraining and interval > 0 and self.updates % interval == 0;
if (reanalyzing) {
    try self.reanalyzer.reanalyze(&self.world_model, replay, rng.split(3));
}

The cost is small: every ten updates 60 targets are planned again, while each update reads 768.

9.21 The schedule: seeding, one burst, then steady updates#

Training runs in frames — one step of every environment at once — and goes through three phases. While seeding, the agent acts at random and makes no updates: the replay fills with experience no planner chose, and every row stores the empty target N(0, 1). Right after the last seed frame comes the burst: many updates at once, as pretraining — the world model learns from the seeded data while the policy and reanalyze wait, since every stored target is still the empty one. Then the steady phase: the planner acts, and every frame is followed by a few updates.

How long to seed. BMPC seeds its one environment for max(5 × episode length, 1000) steps: at least five whole episodes, so the world model has seen episodes from start to end before the planner relies on it. With E environments the count is in frames, so that every environment completes those episodes. Dividing the steps among the environments instead would end seeding, with 8 environments and episodes of 200 steps, after 125 frames — before any episode had ended.

How many updates. Seeding gathers S × E steps, and the burst makes up S × E × u updates, one per seeded step times u. Each later frame adds E steps and E × u updates, so from the burst on the updates made are exactly u times the steps gathered. One environment at u = 1 is BMPC's own schedule: 5000 random steps for episodes of 1000, a burst of 5000 updates, then one update per step.

const schedule: Schedule = .bmpc(environments, episode_length, update_to_data);
const frame: Schedule.Frame = schedule.at(frame_number); // .planned, .updates, .pretraining

9.22 The training loop#

The loop sets every piece of this chapter in motion, one frame at a time, as the schedule says. An environment provides two functions — reset, which starts an episode and writes its first observation, and step, which acts once, writes the observation that follows and returns the reward — and never ends an episode itself.

observation --> act --> action --> environment.step --> reward, next observation
     |      (random while seeding,                                 |
     |       the planner after)                                    v
     +------------------------------------------------------> row --> the replay

then: the schedule's updates for this frame (none, the burst, or E x u)

What a row stores (9.9): the observation the decision was made from, the action acted on — the planner's target jittered by exploration noise, or a random action while seeding — the reward, the true next observation, whether the episode ended there, and the planner's target, or the empty one while seeding.

Episodes have a fixed length L, since BMPC has no notion of termination. The L-th step is stored as truncated, with the true next observation; the environment is then reset, and the next decision is an episode's first, so the planner starts from scratch rather than from its last plan:

frame          0      1      2    |  3      4      5    |  6
episode step   1      2      3    |  1      2      3    |  1          (L = 3)
truncated      -      -      yes  |  -      -      yes  |  -
first step     yes    -      -    |  yes    -      -    |  yes

The discount comes from L, by the rule of 9.19. Acting, environment resets and updates draw from three separate random streams, so the same seeds replay the same run, and adding updates never changes what the environments see. The heart of a frame:

const frame_plan: Schedule.Frame = self.schedule.at(self.frame);
if (frame_plan.planned) {
    try self.actor.act(self.observations, self.first_steps, .explore, frame_rng, self.decision);
} else {
    for (self.decision.actions, 0..) |*action, index| {
        action.* = frame_rng.uniform(T, @intCast(index), -1, 1);
    }
}
// ... every environment steps; its row is filled, with the planner's target or the empty one ...
try self.replay.push(self.rows, self.active);
// ... each environment advances, or begins a new episode at its L-th step ...
for (0..frame_plan.updates) |_| {
    self.last_report = try self.learner.update(&self.replay, update_rng, self.update_index, frame_plan.pretraining);
    self.update_index += 1;
}

last_report keeps the latest update's losses — consistency, reward and value, the gradient norm, and the policy's when it learned — so a run can be watched as it trains.

9.23 Two configurations: BMPC and EfficientTDMPC#

The agent plans for two jobs: to act (9.17), and to reanalyze stored states (9.20). Each job has its own planner settings, a PlannerConfig: how many decisions a plan looks ahead, how many candidates MPPI weighs, how many of them are the policy's own plans, how many elites a refit keeps, and how many rounds it runs. The training window, Config.horizon, is a separate number: how many stored steps a sampled window covers, whatever the planners look ahead.

plannerhorizoncandidateselitesthe policy's plansobjectivepessimism β
BMPCacting35126424final0
reanalyzing35126424final0
EfficientTDMPCacting65126424mean over depths0
reanalyzing36483final10 if episodes can end early, else 0

BMPC gives both jobs the same planner. EfficientTDMPC changes each in one direction. Its acting planner looks six decisions ahead, so more of a plan's score comes from rewards the model predicts and less from the learned value at the plan's end. Its reanalyze planner searches an eighth as widely: reanalyze re-plans many stored states at every round and dominates the training time, and its authors measured the narrower search as slightly better on HumanoidBench and slightly worse on the DeepMind Control Suite, while keeping the training time close to BMPC's. Both configurations are presets, with the discount derived from the episode's length (9.19):

const bmpc_config: Config = .bmpc(episode_length);
const efficient_config: Config = .efficientTdmpc(episode_length, episodes_can_end_early);

The second argument is a fact about the task: whether an episode can end before its length, as a fall ends it. Where it can, EfficientTDMPC's reanalyze planner is pessimistic: every plan's score loses ten standard deviations of the dynamics heads' disagreement (9.7, 9.24), so the policy is distilled toward actions whose value the heads agree on — actions the model has been trained around, away from the edges where a fall waits. Its authors measured it as a large gain where episodes end early and a loss where they do not, and found pessimism while acting no help, so acting keeps β = 0.

EfficientTDMPC also trains four dynamics heads instead of one (9.24). Everything else — the network widths, the learning rates, the reanalyze interval, the discount rule — is the same in both.

9.24 An ensemble of dynamics heads#

EfficientTDMPC trains several dynamics heads instead of one — four, from different initial weights — all working in the same latent space. They share everything else: one encoder, one reward head, one set of value heads. A single dynamics network's errors compound along a rollout, and nothing tells the learner where they are large. Heads that learned the same data from different starting points agree where the data pins the dynamics down and drift apart where it does not; averaging over them evens out any one head's errors.

The heads are trained value-equivalently: each head's rollout is scored by the same losses as BMPC's single head — consistency with the encoded future, and the shared reward and value heads read along its latents — and the world model's loss averages the heads' terms. Every head starts from the same encoded latent, so the heads differ only in where they lead:

consistency = (1 / heads) x sum over heads i of  sum over t of rho^t |head i's z_(t+1) - encoded z_(t+1)|^2 / H
reward      = (1 / heads) x sum over heads i of  the reward's cross-entropy along head i's latents
value       = (1 / heads) x sum over heads i of  the value heads' cross-entropy along head i's latents
total       = 20 consistency + 0.1 reward + 0.1 value

The value target looks one step ahead through every head: one action drawn by the policy at the true latent, the reward it earns there, and the value of where each head says it leads, averaged over the heads:

target = R(z, a) + gamma x (1 / heads) x sum over heads i of V_target(head_i(z, a)),      a drawn at z

The policy learns on every head's latents. Each head's rollout is a different view of the window's states, and the policy is asked to match the planner's targets from all of them, its loss averaged over the heads. Everywhere, the average is the heads' terms summed and divided once, so heads that agree give exactly the numbers one head would. The planner scores every candidate under every head, and keeps the heads' disagreement (9.7). A configuration asks for the ensemble by its number of heads:

config.dynamics_heads = 4; // 1 is BMPC's single dynamics network; Config.efficientTdmpc sets 4

MppiMppi(comptime T: type) typehostModel predictive path integral search: sample, score, refit to the elites, choose; warm-started.
OptionsOptions = structmethodSettings of LatentRollout, WorldModelLoss, TanhMeanGaussian, PolicyDistillationLoss, LatentPlanScorer and Mppi.
ChoiceChoice = enummethodHow Mppi picks its plan: a drawn elite, the best elite, or the mean.
not_a_candidatenot_a_candidate: usize = zm.maxInt(usize);methodMppi.chosen_rows' entry for a choice that is no candidate (the mean).

deinitdeinit(self: Self, allocator: Allocator) voidmethodGive back what init allocated: a masked array's values and mask, a network's or planner's tensors, a replay's rings.
beginbegin(self: Self, warm: []const bool) Error!voidmethodStart an Mppi plan: the mean warm-started, the spread reset wide.
samplesample(self: Self, actions: []const Tensor(T), rng: Rng, iteration: usize) Error!voidmethodUniform with replacement, addressable by (rng, index); SequenceReplay.sample draws step-major windows of one environment with their finished masks and origins; Mppi.sample draws candidate plans.
refitrefit(self: Self, actions: []const Tensor(T), scores: []const T) Error!voidmethodRefit Mppi's Gaussian to its elites, weighted exp(temperature (score - best)).

exploreexplore(self: Self, first_actions: []const T, first_spreads: []const T, exploration_rng: Rng, explored: []T) Error!voidmethodMppi: the chosen first actions jittered by spread x noise and clamped - BMPC's exploration.
